WARRINGTON MUNICIPAL BOWLING

GREENS' LEAGUE.

Sir,—lt has been suggested that a League should be formed in connection with the muni, cipal bowling greens in Warrington, and that a series of matches should be played between teams composed of bowlers who frequent these greens. Will those interested in this move- ment kindly communicate with me with a view to a meeting of representatives from each green being arranged to appoint a committee to carry out the proposal? No doubt, if a competition such as has been thought of was inaugurated, some generous local patron of the game would obligingly offer a trophy to be competed for. J. H. KNOWLES.

49, Samuel-street, Warrington: P.S.—Since I wrote the above the Parks Com- mittee have kindly stated that they would allow the use of the greens by such a League, if formed. J. H. K.

THE SOCIAL TEACHING OF THE BIBLE. Sir,—ln your "last Saturday's issue a letter appears over the title "Latchford Wesleyan," who comments on a speech of mine at a recent Wesleyan meeting. The speech was a call to the Wesleyans for a more active Christian ser- vice, and specially emphasised social service. "Latchford Wesleyan" has evidently misunder- stood my remarks. Probably the fault was mine. .In so large a subject I found it difficult

to express all I meant within the compass of a chairman's address. Certainly it was not in my thought to urge Wesleyans to take up as a Chris- tian duty such subjects as named by the writer, Free Trade, for example.

I acknowledged the dangers and the difficul.

ties arising out of a wider Christian service. • One difficulty would arise in determining how far to go. But surely we have principles to guide us. For instance, the Free Trade issue is primarily economic; it has become political by the adoption of the opposite principle by a party, but it is not directly moral or spiritual, and for the Church to advocate either Free Trade or Tariff Reform would be altogether out- side its province. Nothing of this character was in mind, and I would deprecate any Church be- coming political in a party sense. "Latchford Wesleyan" says, "It must be re- membered, however, that we are Bible men first and last." Good! Then let me point "Latchford Wesleyan" and every other Wes- leyan to this same Bible. Mr. Keeble says : "Protestants, Individualists, Evangelists and

Methodists, of the respected old order, are sur- prised to find that there is any social teaching, as such, in the Bible. They, and their fathers before them, have so long regarded the Bible as a divine revelation, wholly devoted to the re- demption and personal salvation of the indi- vidual soul, that it comes as a shock to be in- formed that law-givers and prophets, Christ and Apostles, all alike, bore more upon their hearts the nation and community, the cities and

classes, tribes and families, of the earth and of Israel, than they did individuals." Take the laws of the Hebrew people, put aside the forms and what alone applies to the special conditions of this far off time, consider what are the guiding principles of this wonderful Mosaic code. To do this I cannot do better than quote Henry George : " It is not the protection of property, but the protection of humanity, that is the aim of the Mosaic code. Its sanctions are not directed to securing the strong in heaping up wealth, so much as to preventing the weak from being crowded to the wall. At every point it inter- poses its barriers to the selfish greed, that, if left unchecked, will surely differentiate men into landlord and serf, capitalists and workmen, millionaire and tramp, ruler and ruled. Its Sab- bath day and Sabbath year secure even the low-

liest rest and leisure: With the blast of the Jubilee trumpets the slave goes free, the debt

that cannot be paid is cancelled, and a redivi- , sion of the land secures again to the poorest his fair share in the bounty of the common Creator. The reaper must leave something for the gleaner; even the ox cannot be muzzled as he treadeth out the corn. Everywhere in every- thing the dominant idea is that of our homely phrase, 'Live and let live !' " Bishop Gore says : "When you read the Old Testament one thing burns itself into your heart and mind, and that is that the prophets of God betray extra- ordinarily little interest in how much the rich man can make out of industry—that is not their business—but they betray extraordinary and in- tense interest in seeing that there shall be no grinding of the faces of the poor.' " The emphasis of the Bible on social ethics is continued all through the prophetic writings. The words of Isaiah may be taken as typical : "Is not this the fast that I have chosen? to loose the bands of wickedness, to undo the bands of the yoke, to let the oppressed go free, and that ye break every yoke? Is it not to deal thy bread to the hungry, and that thou bring the poor that are cast out to thy house? When thou sweat the naked, that thou cover him; and that thou hide not thyself from thy own flesh? Then shall thy light break forth as the morn- ing, and thy healing shall spring forth speedily." . . . . And then how rich the New Testament is in social teaching, not as a separate part of the Gospel, the good news, but as an essential part of it. The key note was struck by the Master Himself in the words "Love thy neighbour as thyself." Christ preached the social side of the Gospel by every deed of physical healing and uplifting, and He made it the test of eternal life in His portrayal of the final iudgment, "Inasmuch as ye did," &c. Let "Latchford Wesleyan" adhere to his posi- tion of being a "Bible man first and last," and he will help to bring the Church to which we both belong into touch with the environment in which it is placed. I should be sorry to think it has lost its power of adaptation. CHARLES E. PARKER. April 25, 1914. P.S.—I would recommend to "Latchford Wesleyan" and to all others an admirable book W.M.U.S. Service, which can published by ftrohem the Methodist Book Room, entitled "The Social Teaching of the Bible."

LETTERS BY AN IRlSHMAN—continued.

sometimes of whole families, who lay down and died, one after the other, upon the floor of their miserable cabins, and so remained uncoffined and unburied, till chance unveiled the appalling scene." In that visitation Ireland literally could not afford decent burial for her dead; the hinged coffin was a grim feature of those years. No European nation in modern times has suffered what, between 1846-49, Ireland suffered under the government of England. Yet those were years when the country was exporting, weekly, shiploads of food, while the people lay starving at home. Lord John• Russell admitted that in 1847 the wheat crop was above the average, and cattle were abundant. The Government's main task was to protect the cargoes of food on their way to the ports. Irishmen have always refused to believe that any Irish Government would have allowed their people to starve while ships were carrying away from their shores food which the labour of their hands had produced. Nor did the decrease in the population which followed put an end to the famine. There was dearth again in 1859-60; finally in 1879 another failure of the potato crop was a main motive cause for that revolution which has at last put an end to the system which inevitably resulted in the recurrence of these horrors. To-day The 'lrish people do not thank the Tory party for their Land Bill of 1903. They have to thank Parnell, Davitt, Dillon and the Land League— agenciese r n m eEnglishp out. BeforeG o v- nt theirdwihdie England Utmost a to od stamp I turn to what I may term the "New Order," let us first illustrate the condition to which eighty years of administration and law-making from Westminster had brought Ireland. In the spring of 1880, 117,454 were in receipt of poor law relief. The Mansion House Fund, ,estab- lished to cope with the famine, gave food or money to 512,625 during the last week of February of that year. Special correspondents were sent to report. They regarded the habita- tions of the Irish peasant as the most despicable hovels imaginable. There was no comparison in the Steepes, the Bulgarian villages, the Hindoo hamlets, nor even in Catewayo's dominions. General Gordon in 1880 wrote: "I must say, from all accounts and from my own , observations, that the state of our fellow coun- trymen in the parts I have named is worse than that of any people in the world, let alone , Europe. I believe that these people are made as we are—that they are patient beyond belief, loyal, but at the same time broken-spirited and desperate, living on the verge of starvation in places where we could not keep our cattle. The Bulgarians, Anatolians, Chinese and Indians are better off than many of them are. lam not well off, but I would offer Lord or his agent £l,OOO if either of them would live one week in one of these poor devil's places and feed as these people do." _ _

All this misery had grown up under English government; it was, in truth, directly attribut- able to it. Yet England complained that the Irish were "disloyal." Let it be freely and fully admitted that all this is changed. In the extreme West of Ire- land people are still poor. But they pay a rent that has been fixed by a legal tribunal, and that is often less than half what they paid in 1880. If they, improve their land, if by carrying soil on their backs, or by quarrying out huge stones, they create land, the benefit goes to them, not

to the landlord. In thousands of cases they own their own holdings, paying off the cost by small yearly instalments. The beginnings of prosperity are there, since no longer can all above a bare margin of subsistence be extracted from them. But who is answerable for this change? What has caused it?

F.W.R.

ESSAYS ON "FIRST-AID."

Adjudication in the Zam-Buk Competition.

Girls Do Better Than Boys.

SMINGT (Regd. Trade MarU COFFEE ESSENCE The Coffee Maid, of Syminftnn's, says "Sing a song of Symington's, the kettle's on the sin. A little drop of Essence, makes Coffee for a kin-,." THOS. SY.III::GTON & CO.. Edinburgh and London.

_ The competition amongst school 'children for essays on "First Aid," organised by C. E. Ful- ford, Ltd., the sole proprietors of Zam-Buk, has just concluded. Nearly 10,000 essays were sent in from schools and scholars in practically every corner of the British Isles. According to the Judges' report, the essays disclose a much improved knowledge of "First- Aid," due probably to the intrOduction of the study in tho regular curriculurn of Council Schools, but strangely enough the standard of merit is higher in the case of girls than boys. The essay which secured the highest number of marks, written by Miss Mildred Swinscoe, aged 13, of the Berry Brow Council' School, Hudders- field, displays, a really high degree of skill and ingenuity. The Silver Cup awarded to the teacher whose scholars displayed the best average knowledge of "First-Aid," has been won by Miss M. E. Barnes. assistant mistress of the Manor-road Girls' School, West Ham, Essex.

LEITERS BY AN IRISHMAN.

Survey of the Facts Governing the Demand for Home Rule.

Two Thousand Five Hundred Children compete at

School Sports.

===== 0002601_19140530_art0022.txt | included=true | included =====
V.-IRELAND UNDER THE UNION.

In 1800 the Union was carried—by what means it is superfluous to describe. A million and a quarter was spent in bribery, and by a singular irony, this expenditure was charged to the pub- lic debt of Ireland. The sum was not widely distributed, since a very few persons controlled an enormous number of seats in the Irish Parliament. Lord Downshire, for instance, got £52,000 for his share; Lord Ely, over £45,000; Lord Carrick, £14,000; and so on down to the Earl of Masserene and his three brothers, each of whom received £3,750.

DIFFERENT TREATMENT FOR SCOTLAND AND IRELAND. It is sometimes argued by Englishmen that since the Union with Scotland has been upon the whole a success, the Union with Ireland must inevitably have resulted for the good of Ire- land, but for the unfitness of the Irish either to govern or be governed. The best reply to this argument is found in an article written by an eminent Unionist, Professor Dicey, in which he says: "To the average Englishman's concep- tion the difference of results lies in the differ- ence between Scotch and Irish character Yet this view, so popular and so plausible, is dis- missed by me as a gross misrepresentation of past events which can be confuted by a simple reference to facts which lie .on the very surface of history." Why did the Scotch Union succeed? First, because the Scotch Act of Union embodied what was, not in name only, but in reality, a treaty or contract freely made between two independ- ent States. Scotland accepted a bargain, made for her by ministers of her own choosing, after full deliberation, in which her material interests were richly secured. She gave up her independ- ence; she secured full participation in the ad- vantages of English rule. Her violent discon- tent and dislike to the loss of independence were worn away by a prosperity steadily grow- ing. Before the Union, Scotland was excluded from the English Colonial trade. After the Union, she enjoyed and profited to the full by all its vast opportunities. "But," says Professor Dicey, "the Union with Ireland lacked all that element of free consent between independent contracting parties which lies at the basis of every genuine contract." Of the deliberate negotiation, of the calm, satisfactory, business- like haggling for national advantages, which marked the negotiations between the Scotch and the English Commissioners; of the close con- sideration of minute details by competent repre- sentatives of both countries, there is not a trace in the negotiations, if negotiations they can be called, between England and Ireland. There was plenty of haggling over the details of in- dividual bribes; none over the interests of the country. What were the driving forces? One or two facts are patent—the Irish Protestants were dazed with horror at the massacres of the Rebellion; the Irish Catholics were lulled into acquiescence by promises which were made only to be broken; no appeal was made to the Irish constituencies; and the members of both Houses were corrupted. "The Act of Union," says Dicey, "was an agreement which, could it have been referred to a court of law, must at once have been cancelled as a contract hope- lessly tainted with fraud and corruption." What a great verdict for a Unionist 1 Sec- ondly, the Union with Scotland was carried out in a wholly different spirit. Favour was shown throughout to the weaker nation; the Scotch, from a merely mercantile point of view, got by far the best of the bargain. In Ireland, the case was far different. After a century of legisla- tion directed expressly against every industry that competed with any English interest, Ire- land had, in 1782, achieved at once commercial and legislative freedom. In gaining independ- ence, Ireland gained also those very privileges of free participation in oversea trade which Scotland sacrificed her independence to gain. The corrupt aristooracy which sold Ireland's freedom, sold also the character of that right to protect and foster her own industrial life, under which the growth of manufacturing industry had been so swift. Thirdly, the institutions for which Scotchmen seriously cared were maintained or secured by the Union. The one great national institution— the Church of Scotland—derived new security and greatly increased power from the means which politically amalgamated Scotland with England. The result of the respect paid to Scotch institutions was that, while Scotland became an inseparable part of Great Britain, Scotch affairs remained after, even more than before the Union, under the control of Scotland. Contrast this with Ireland. The Church of Ireland, indeed, was secured, but it was not a Church of the Irish people. Godolphin ensured to the Scotch a cherished Institution. Pitt buttressed up in Ireland an alien anomaly. Professor Dicey says: "The assembly which sat in Dublin had what the Scotch Parliament had Irishd ic ate d c o national m mere t not, strong claims on the sentimental interests had produced of the people whom it represented; it had independence;vin- it withinha the twenty freed twenty years preceding its death a brilliant body of statesmen and orators; it had become, in short, a centre of national life." Of the passing of the Act of Union, Mr. Lecky, the Unionist, has said, "ii was not only a great It ime, it was like most crimes, a great blunder. united the Parliaments and divided the nations."

THE PRACTICAL TEST OF RESULTS. Without going into the question of England's moral title to govern Ireland—which Ireland has always denied—let us apply the Practical test of results. Has the government been suc- cessful? Has it been just? Has it been con- ducted on rational lines? First, apply, again, the test of famine. There was famine of un- usual severity in 1817. De Beaumont, an ad- mirably informed observer, reported that the famine fever had affected, a million and a half persons in Ireland, of whom vast numbers per- ished. In 1819 a Parliamentary Committee re- ported "That the general distress and deficiency of employment are so notorious as to render the production of any particular evidence to estab- lish the extent and variety of the evil unneces- sary."

In another Committee of mg, Mr. Doherty, the Irish Solicitor-General, stated "that there was then (1823) in Ireland the existence of a con- dition of things which the lower animals in England would scarcely endure, and which, in fact, they did not endure." And this, Humani- tarianists, was a comparison with the position of the people. In the debate on the Irish Registration Bill, February, 1841, Lord Derby stated : "That persons having from 15 to 20 acres of land are generally from April to September in a state of the greatest destitution, living on potatoes, without either milk or meat, and con- sidering themselves very happy if they have dry potatoes enough—men who during a great part of the year lived on dry potatoes—men whom the landlords, letting their lands at a rack rent, may upon any day turn loose upon the world, to starve in the last degree of misery." And this, it should be observed, is previous to the famine of 1846 and 1847, which may be re- garded as the turning point in modern Irish history. Things then reached such a pitch of horror that it become apparent that the appoint- ment of Committees would not suffice to meet the case. The Census Commissioners wrote when the main harvest of death was over, though copious gleanings for pestilence still remained : "No pen has recorded the numbers of the forlorn and starving who perished by the way- side or in the ditches, or of the mournful groups,

HOW GROUND BECOMES TAINTED.

One would expect that it was only in the case of those with a very limited area of ground at their disposal that there was trouble to any extent with tainted soil, but unfortunately this is not the case. Farmers, as well as others, are affected by this condition of affairs, although

they have many acres over which it would'be possible to run their birds. People do not seem to realise that the same laws apply to poultry as to the rest of the natural world, but until they do birds are likely to suffer from this ter- rible complaint. if grass land is used con- stantly for poultry it should only be stocked with that number which will produce sufficient manure for the grass that will be grown and taken off the land. If birds are given their liberty, twenty-five td the acre is quite enough; if kept in runs, not more than fifty to one hun- dred and fifty, according to the conditions, should be run together. The latter figure ap- plies to those cases in which, the scratching-shed system is employed. It is possible to keep many more birds to the acre than the figure stated if the arrangements are such that two runs are provided for each pen. In this case one run is used for six months of ,the year, whilst the other run is resting, or, in other words, producing a crop. In the case of backyard poultry keeping, where a few birds are crowded on to a very small area of land, it is necessary to renew the upper soil at frequent intervals, so that there will be no accumulation of excreta.

THE EXAMINER, SATURDAY, MAY 30, 1914. FARM AND GARDEN.

DRAGGED BY A HARROW.

Teeth of a harrow became fixed in the cloth- ing of a man who Was harrowing plough land near Redhill. He was dragged up and down the field by the team more than 500 yards, and bled to death.

ACCIDENT NOT OUTRAGE.

A horse wasi found disembowelled in a field near Walsall, separated only by a canal from another field where two maiming outrages re- cently occurred. The horse had to be shot. It is clearly a case of accident, anti not' a maiming outrage, as a portion of iron fencing was dis: covered knocked down, with some hair on the spikes. The Police believe that other cases, which have been described as maiming outrages, are also due .to accident. _

Until the thunderstorms on Friday .RELIEF FOR PARCHED LANDS. night' and Saturday morning there had been no rain of appreciablb volume for a fortnight; Says "The Times," and the ,result was reflected in the stinted appearance of .the 'grass and rotation crops. The landscape In the storm zone—fortu- nately wide enough to embrace the parched area—has been greatly refreshed. The first deluge was too heavy to penetrate far into the . soil, most of it running off on the crusted sur- face, but the more moderate "rains tluit fol- lowed sank to the roots of the plants and have produced a wonderful effect. ! : ' • The value of the week-end rajns to the country can be estima,ted -•from the fact that in the middle of the' week the situation recalled to mind that three years ago, when hay was a partial failure, the spring cereals were parched, and the root crops failed to germinate, ending in a season of scarcity that will long be remem- bered. The fresh supplies of surface moisture have given the needed stiznulus, and now that they have begun to more warm' temperatures, 'espe- cially during the nights, will rush them for. forming the thick undergrowth that brings weight and. quality of yield in the meadows and abundant -nutritious herbage on the :pastures. , , ,Owing to the limited supplies of grazing .f nini kern Ireland many pastures atregariesatalyrrivunindger-stocked, and this has mini- mised.the immediate effects of 'a peculiarly try- ing season. The break-up of, the drought has averted- this and many other equally unacceptable' ex- pedients.

UNITED _STATES BEEF SUPPLY.

Less than a decade ago the United States was one of the largest beef-exporting nations in the world; to-day it is an importing nation. From 1905 to 1912 there 'was a decrease in the value of beef cattle and beef exports leaving the United States from 72,435,000 dollars to 14,602,000 dol- lars. This marked decrease in exports is due chiefly to the increased domestic demand, and a consequently reduced margin between prices ,at Chicago and at British ports. During Octo- -1 ber, November and December, 1913, the aggre- Igate imports of dressed meats were approxim- -1 ately 33,000,000113., not to speak of purchases of stock on hoof from Canada p,nd Mexico, while from January 1, 1914, to March I, 1924, the direct shipment of beef from Buenos Aires to New York totalled 99,000 quarters, the equiva- lent of practically 25,000 cattle. Since the new tariff law removing the duty from meat went into effect these thousands of tons of Argentine beef arriving at the port of New York have caused some confusion on the railroads, as the long habit of hauling meat eastward and not westward is thus reversed. Some authorities are apprehensive as to the effect of Free Trade in cattle and meats upon cattle growing in the United States, but American cattle growers are far more prosperous now with some Argentine beef coming in than they were in the nineties with their own beef going out. Up to the pres_ ent time free beef has, failed to relieve the shortage and lessen prices. On the other hand, the price is sure to go up, because the popula- tion is increasing faster than the domestic supply.--" The Times."

===== 0002601_19140530_art0033.txt | included=true | included =====
IN THE GARDEN.

Hoe alongside all crops. Sow Forget-me-nots, Pansies, Violas, Silenes, Aubretias, and the big Dusseldorf Daisies. Sow dwarf French Beans, Scarlet Runner Beans, Turnips, and finish planting Potatoes. Draw a little soil up to Potatoes showing above the ground. Earth up Peas (dwarf and tall) and put stakes to in good time. Roses will be benefited by a little guano or nitrate of soda, stirred into the soil and watered in.

MAKING CELERY TRENCHES. For a single row of plants, mark out a trench 15in. to 18in. wide. Then dig out the soil to a depth of 18in., throwing the soil on each side equally. For two or more rows a width of from 2ft. to 4ft. will be needed. accordingly. After taking out the soil in either case to the required depth, the subsoil must be well broken up. Then put in about four inches of fairly well-decom- posed manure and any green rubbish there is about. Now put in four inches of the top soil, which will finish off the trench portion. The ridges must now be made firm, levelling the top so that lettuce and other crops may be grown until such time as the celery requires earthing. The beginner is advised to start with a single row, which will be so much easier to manage, until experience for a wider trench is gained.

THE OLD BULBS. In most cases the beds occupied by the spring flowering bulbs have now to be cleared for the summer flowering plants, and what to do with the bulbs after flowering often a vexed ques- tion. The best plan is to lift the bulbs carefully and lay them in in rows in some spare piece of ground. This allows of the foliage dying Off, the i nutriment contained n the leaves being gradu- ally transferred to the bulbs. When the; foliage has died down and become quite dry the bulbs can then be relifted, cleaned and stored away in some dry place until required in the autumn. A word of warning may well be given here, and that is that such bulbs are of little value for prominent beds, and when replanted should be grown in shrubberies or any place in the vege- table garden where they will afford a supply of flowers for cutting.

SWEET VIOLETS. Everyone may grow Violets, their culture being very simple. A suitable site, where thee plants may receive sunshine for a portion of the day, but not in the fierce glare of the sun, should be selected—facing east to west via north. If a southern aspect is chosen the position should be partially shaded. If possible, raise the bed a few inches above the ground level, which should be composed of good loam, leaf mould, and a good sprinkling of charcoal dust or soot. Before planting give the bed a good watering, and allow the soil to settle. In dividing the old plants that have finished flowering, select the strongest suckers and runners, with a bit of root attached if possible. Plant in rows, one foot apart each way, placing them in the soil up to the part where growth commences. Make each one firm, and give water frequently during the summer months, so that they may become established before the heat of the summer. Remove all runners during summer to prevent useless growths.

MOTOR NOTES.

Competitions are "the thing" nowadays.

Go where you will you come across the com- petition face,, flushed, strangely serious, the eyes fixed upon the far-away; half-closed eyes with that dull glaze possessed also by the optics of a fish deceased. If the competing one is a friend of yours you will probably be rushed at, almost before you can lay your hat and gloves aside; a series of weird pictures will be ,thrust under your nose and a voice hoarse with pent-up feel- ing will beseech you to supply offhand a solution for number 23, failing which you will be the horrible cause of the loss of a ten shilling cash prize and half a hundredweight of some frightd fully useful line of domestic goods.

. - A These are symptoms of the keenest enjoyment. There is nothing to be alarmed at. Your failure to translate into Pool Quay a picture of a small lake and a gentleman whose preference does NOT appear to be water trying to find the slot in his front-door yale, will only spur your hoarse friend to greater efforts, to secure correspond- ingly trivial results. But what about running a competition your- self, for. a prize not so trifling? This is an idea which has occurred to Michelin and they offer it to you. You will have no brain-waving to do, no coupons to collect; you leave all the work to the tyre and simply record the'results in a book, specially prepared for the purPose, a copy of which the Michelin Tyre Co., Ltd., will send you gratis on application at their address: 81, Fulham-road, London, S.W.

• Michelin want you to prove by actual trial that Michelin 'Tires will pay you best. The Record Book is giVen.you in order that you may calculate exactly how much each cover, of all the makes you use, costs you per mile. It 'wi,ll. enable you to get at your tyre-mileage cod to, a, penny and will show you which make .of .tyre yields you the greatest return on your outlay. This is a competition among tyres that is very well worth 'your while; 'for, when you come'to consider ,the fact, it will enable you to•deCide, once and for all, which tyres:to:buy and Which to avoid.

A postcard bearing your name and addres and the word "Record Book" is sufficient to secure you a complimentary copy by return. BIBENDUM.

The eYeLISTS' NOTEBOOK.

THE WYE VALLEY.

To those who have a week's holiday this Whitsuntide, I can recommend the Wye Val- ley, both for its beauty, its comparative ease, and its handiness to Warrington cyclists. The first stage is Tarporley, 18 miles, and Whitchurch, 15. The direct road to Shrews- bury would be best avoided, the 27 miles round by ‘Bllesmere being so picturesque and prefer- able to anyone who loves lake scenery. Between Shrewsbury and Ludlow (29) are Church Stret- ton, the home and the burial-place of the Rev. Sylvester Horne, and Stokesay Castle, which should certainly be visited. Ludlow is unique as a medic-al town, and a day can be spent there easily. The hop country extends to Here- .

_ Mr. 11. Smith, Bridge-street, showed me this week one of the neatest accessories to the bicycle I have ever seen. It is called the "Brit- ish Byer-Ready" lamp, and is operated on the flash lamp principle. It burns for 18 hours with one charge, and a re-charge costs is. It is made in nickel-plate, and costs Bs. 6d. with the first charge. It turned exactly 17 ounces on the scale. The light is very powerful for its size, and on the whole the new lamp should be very useful to those Nyho do much night riding.

CYCLE CARRIERS AGAIN. It does one good to see that a motor cyclist has been got at by the police for carrying a lady on his carrier. Perhaps some readers noticed in Friday's paper how it was accomplished. He

As a whole the carters and drivers of War- rington are "horse proud." Indeed, one cannot come to any other conclusion after witnessing Saturday's horse parade, which was the 14th annual event of its kind in the town. It is always a splendid sight to see the well-groomed, brightly harnessed animals in the parade round the principal streets, and despite the fact that this year umbrellas were in greater demand than sunshades, large crowds lined up along the route and cheered the various successful competitors. There was a record number of 138 entries, which was an increase of about 20 on the previous parade. The judging took place in front of the Town Hall, and it was easily noticeable that the competition is having the desired effect of im- proving the general condition and treatment of horses in the borough; encouraging drivers to take a humane interest in the animals under their care, and encouraging the use of horses suitable and fit for heavy work in the streets. Each animal was critically judged, not a single point escaping the notice of the following gen- tlemen: The Hon. J. E. Cross (High Legh), Lieut-Colonel Lyon (Appleton Hall), Messrs. W. D. Bullock (Altrincham), G. Cotton (Tar- parley), V. Davies (Steckton Heath), R. nem-

JUDGES INTERVIEWED.

Ur V IL

.._----- ------• An "Examiner" representative had inter- views with the judges, one of whom expressed the opinion that the Corporation horses were the hest, although the various private firms had excellent turnouts. Great care was shown by all drivers, and despite the fact that there was not much improvement on last year, the show was a very good one. Mr. Bullock's views were that the show was quite up to the, average, perhaps a littlebit above the average. The harness was better kept, the drivers appearing to be more particu- lar in keeping the harness clean. Generally. speaking, the horses were very clean and cam( fully groomed.

" 'do you think the show compares with that of other towns?" our representative asked, to which Mr. Bullock replied, " Oh ! it is quite as good generally, and in some cases a great deal better. Of course, the Warrington show has been going longer than some of the others. You compare very favourably 'indeed with other towns."

CYCLING AND MOTORING.

An Advertising Device That Caused Uneasiness in Ireland. THE PROFESSIONAL RIDER.

Just at present the powers that be in the cycling world are somewhat perturbed over the subject of professionalism. It is really the old question over again—Is professionalism in sport a good or a bad thing? Local debating societies have waxed eloquent over it, newspapers have devoted columns to the subject, with the net result that we are in exactly the same position as when we started. One thing, however, is certain—professionalism has come to stay, whether we like it or not. Once the principle is admitted there seems to be no reason why any particular sport should be hailed as an exception. We have professional footballers, cricketers, golfers, &c. Then why not profes- sional cyclists? In this connection Mr. F. Percy Low, the well-known cycling journalist, has made a very interesting suggestion and he asks why not divide competitors in racing events into three classes P—amateurs, who receive noth- ing except actual prizes; semi-professionals, if such a term be permissible to describe those who receive expenses only; and, thirdly, the out-and-out professional. The idea is certainly a novel one, but whether anything will come of it remains to be seen. A Maxim in Trouble.

It is not often that one expects to find humour in the dealings of our large business houses, but a most ludicrous event has happened recently with a large forwarding firm. It appears that a well-known firm of cycle makes declare that their cycles are "made like a gun," and to emphasise the fact on the public they make use of an old Maxim gun for advertising purposes. Their plan is to send this for exhibition to their various agents throughout the country, when it is placed in the shop window. It is a very good dodge, and its appearance usually excites a large amount of interest. The gun was on view in Southport recently, and from there it was consigned to an agent in Belfast. Without making any inquiries, the forwarding agents refused to carry it, and, as far as I know, it is still lying in the warehouse at Liverpool. The funny part of the whole proceeding is that prior to the purchase of the gun the principal parts were destroyed; therefore it is useless for any war purpose.- The Maxim is excellent as an advertisement, but for service with the Volun- teers in Ulster it is useless.

Wake Up, British Manufacturers! I n haveot fully oft marketssurely cycle makers are home_ foreign' trade of foreign their machines. Certainly the is a good one, but the very fact that hardly any machines find their way into Great Britain, notwithstanding the entire lack of legis- lative restriction, is itself an indication of the excellence of British bicycles and their possi- bilities abroad. Only a few days ago official figures were issued relating to the imports of cycles and cycle parts into Russia. From these it appears that no less than 17,681 complete machines, valued at .6165,883, were imported into that country. It has been known for some time—says the "Cycle Trader "—that the trade is largely in German hands, but it comes as a surprise to learn that the German share of the trade was so high as 14,774 machines (X 136,115 leaving only 2,907 machines (X 29,768 for all other countries. When over in Germany several years ago, I was very unfavourably impressed with the average cycle in that country, and registered a vow that if ever I had to live there my bicycle should go as well, tariff or no tariff. With an obviously superior article, surely the cost of freight to Russia will not deter our manufacturers. •

"UP WITH THE LARK."

The "Joy" of Possessing a Bird Tower of Babel.

MRS. STIRLING'S CURTAIN LECTURE.

I I. EkAMNEA 13t-CIAL. Poets have sung of and authbri haVt penned prosy c'oluning about thn *nthling of the birds in the first few hours which succeeds the dawn. This feathered vocal concert is described as one of the "joys" of semi-rural life. At present it is my happy, or unhappy, lot to each morning have to wake up at 2.30 and listen to the serenade of the birds. Behind my house, on the outskirts of Warrington, are gardens, with fields still further beyond, and in these about all the known species of local birds, except sea galls, gather and make melody which prevents sleep. Perhaps this is the penalty of too liberal feeding during the winter. Whatever it is due to it is scarcely a "joy." As the first streak of dawn arrives in the eastern sky, the lark shoots up with his blithe- some song. A short distance away he has a rival. It is a race between them each morning to see which can warble the first note. Now the lark is not so bad if the others would keep quiet, but his song is like the bo'swain's whistle on ship board, all hands are called upon deck for the piping practice.

===== 0002601_19140530_art0055.txt | included=true | included =====
THE PERKY SPARROWS.

... There ar perky sparrows. My experience of them is they like to be "up with the lark." They, li. .-ever, do not soar heavenwards, but come to the window ledge and "chirp, chirp" incessantly. Desiring to live the simple life, I leave the bedroom window open. One cheeky sparrow hops every morning on to the window chirping his loudest, and then pops into the room as if he was the landlord. You can "hush" at him as much as you like, but he will not budge until he has made a survey of the room. Satisfied with what he has seen, he hops out to join the rest in a continuous twittering, which is kept up until they get hungry and descend to the gardens and fields in search of food.

MOTHER ROOK ARRIVES.

Mater Rook next comes along with her brood, young cawing creatures. They alight in the field without and such a din they make as they wrangle over dainty morsels of food. For some hours they keep up their noise, but at length depart for pastures new. Ere this the thrush has got to work. There are several of them, and a conversation some- thing like this goes on in the shrillest tones 1 "Where are you? where are you?" "Here, here, here!" "Where, where, where?" "Where are you?" &c. The blackbirds are doing their best to drown the notes of the thrush, and these two, along with the sparrows, finally succeed in drowning the voice of the lark.

===== 0002601_19140530_art0056.txt | included=true | included =====
THE PHEASANT'S CHALLENGE.,

The cock pheasant by this time has wakened up, and he hurls a challenge to fight the whole world in his deep, guttural notes. Every morn- ing he challenges for an hour, but evidently the other members know him too well to bother about his desire to fight. The cuckoo in the fast growing corn is "cuck-cooing" for all his worth, and the cornerake is making a sound like a file rasping a tough piece of steel. The humorists are a pair of starlings nesting in one of the eaves of the house. Where the "head of the house" goes at night I cannot say, but when he comes jauntily back soon after dawn he catches it from his spouse.

===== 0002601_19140530_art0057.txt | included=true | included =====
MRS. STARLING'S LECTURE.

Scarce has he gently settled down on the spouting than the lien opens her anger upon him. It only requires a little imagination to convert the conversation which ensues into something like this: Hen (in deep voice): Where have you been all night? The Cock: (meekly): Detained at offish.

Hen: Story-teller, story-teller. Cock: True, true, true. Hen: Nice beauty you are. Cock: Nonsense, nonsense. Hen: I won't have it, won't have it. Cock (more meekly): Sorry, sorry. And so on goes the talk, the poor male bird getting about as powerful a curtain lecture as ever Caudle received from night-capped Mrs. Caudle.

The " joy " of the morning goes on unceas- ingly for about an hour and a half. Fortun- ately for we poor mortals, birds have appetites which must be satisfied, and it is only when they feel hungry that they cease their clamour, and the country sinks into comparative .peace, which brings sleep to those disturbed by the morning chatter of the birds.

JOHN WESLEY.

Extracts from his Journal—Continued

Christian Prudence!

At Oxford I was grieved to find that prudence had made them leave off singing psalms. I fear it will not stop here. God deliver me, and all that seek Him in sincerity, from what the world calls Christian prudence

The World My Parish." Suffer me now to tell you my principles in I this matter. I look upon all the world as my parish; thus far I mean, that in whatever part of it I am, I judge it meet, right, and my bounden duty, to declare unto all that are wil- ling to hear, the glad tidings of salvation. This is the work which I know God has called me to; and sure I am, that his blessing attends it.

Oxford Prisoners. I had a little leisure to take a view of the shattered condition of things here. The poor prisoners, both in the castle and in the city prison, had now none that cared for their souls, none to instruct, advise, comfort and build them up in the knowledge and love of the Lord Jesus. None was left to visit the Workhouses, where also we used to meet with the most moving ob- jects of compassion.

Describes a Book. In riding to Bradford I read over Mr. Law's book on the New Birth; philosophical, specula- tive, precarious; Behimish, void and vain!

The Beginning of Class Meetings. Many met together to consult on a proper method for discharging the public debt; and it was at length agreed: (1) That every member of the society who was able should contribute a penny a week.

(2) That the whole society should be divided into little companies or classes, about twelve in each class; and (3) That one person in each class should re- ceive the contribution of the rest, and bring it into the stewards weekly.

Quietens a Mob. In the evening as I was preaching at St. Ives Satan began to fight for his kingdom. The mob of the town burst into the room, and created much disturbance; roaring, and striking those that stood in their way, as though Legion him- self possessed them. I would fain have per- suaded our people to stand still, but the zeal of some, and the fear of others, had no ears; so that, finding the uproar increase, I went into the midst, and brought the head of the mob up with me to the desk. I received but one blow on the side of the head; after which we reasoned the case, till he grew milder and milder, and at length undertook to quiet his companions.

Irish Massacres. I procured a genuine account of the great Irish massacre in 1641. Surely never was there such a transaction before from the beginning of the world! More than 200,000 men, women and children butchered within a few months in cold blood, and with such circumstances of cruelty as make one's blood run cold! It is well if God has not a controversy with the nation. on this very account, to this day.

Irish Misgovernment. I stayed at home and spake to all that came; but I found scarce any Irish among them. At least ninety-nine in a hundred of the native Irish remain in the religion of their forefathers. The Protestants, whether in Dublin or else- where, are almost all transplanted lately from England. For is it any wonder that those who are born Papists generally live and die such, When the Protestants can find no better ways ti convert them than penal laws and Acts of Par- liament?

Ireland's Voluntary Poor Relief. In the afternoon I walked through all the parts of the workhouse, called, in Ireland, "The House of Industry." It is pleasantly situated on a rising ground, near the river, and I believe would contain about three hundred persons. That at Dublin contains six hundred. At pre- sent there are about eighty persons there, the contributions falling short.

Electricity. I went with two or three friends to see what are called the electrical experiments. How must these also confound those poor half- thinkers, who will believe nothing but what they can comprehend? Who can comprehend how fire lives in water, and passes through it more freely than through air? How flame issues out of my finger, real flame, such as sets fire to spirits of wine? How these, and many more as strange phenomena arise from the turning round a glass globe? It is all mystery; if haply by any means God may hide pride from man !

A Light Day's Work! • Some gentlemen of Yarm earnestly desired that I would preach there in the afternoon. I refused for some time, being weak and tired, so that I thought preaching thrice in the day, and riding upward of fifty miles, would be work enough. But they would take no denial. So I went with them about two o'clock, and preached at three in the Market place there, to a great multitude of people, gathered together at a few minutes' warning.

Preaching a Healthy Exercise. This being my birthday, the first day of my seventy-second year, I was considering how is this, that I find just the same strength as I did thirty years ago? That my sight is considerably better now, and my nerves firmer than they were then? That I have none of the infirmities of old age, and have lost several I had in my youth? The grand cause is the good pleasure of God, Who doeth whatsoever pleaseth Him. The chief means are: (1) My constantly rising at four for about forty years; (2) my generally preaching at five in the morning, one of the most healthy exercises in the world; (3) my never travelling less, by sea or land, than four thousand five hundred miles in a year.

Human Slavery. I preached, about noon, at Warrington, and in the evening at Liverpool, where many large ships are now laid up in the docks, which had been employed for many years in buying or stealing poor Africans, and selling them in America for slaves. The men butchers have nothing to do at this laudable occupation; since the American war broke out there is no demand for human cattle; so the men of Africa, as well as Europe, may enjoy their native liberty.

A Princely Salary. What I receive of the fund yearly is neither more nor less than thirty pounds.

===== 0002601_19140530_art0061.txt | included=true | included =====
THREE BAGS IN ONE.

A lady who has tried one for some little time gives an enthusiastic account of a novelty known as the " Surprise" folding bag, made by the Folding Bag Manufacturing Co., of 79, Turn- mill-street, London, E.C. She declares that she would not be without it now for ten times its original cost, indeed it has grown to be an abso- lute necessity to her. It so often happens that a lady when out for a walk catches sight of a bargain which she cannot resist, and in the ordinary course the result is that she becomes loaded with a number of small paper parcels, consequently presenting a decidedly unsightly appearance. The alternative is to carry wher- ever she goes a huge marketing bag, a trial which few ladies care to face. By the use of the "Surprise" folding bag she now escapes all this annoyance, as when carried in the ordinary way to contain purse, handkerchief, and the like, it is only loi by 6 inches. Should small purchases be made, by a simple arrangement the depth is increased by an additional three inches, thus affording room for many a little oddment that may be purchased. As the shopping progresses and more goods are bought the bag is again ea3ily enlarged until it attains the capacity of 16i inches deep, 10i inches wide, opening to a breadth of 7 inches.

lobbies of the House of Commons, but when it is a question of the moral authority of the Gov- ernment to pursue their policy, there is, as Mr. Samuel says, no ground or principle on which it can be contended that the judgment of the 20,000 electors should be preferred to the judg- ment of the 236,000. Do we, then, infer, it may be asked, that all will be well for the Govern- ment if, when a General Election comes, the contest is fought on the same lines as at these recent by-elections? Very far from it. We think, on the contrary, that unless the Liberal and Labour differences can be adjusted there will be great risk of a most serious disaster. These differences do not apply to any present question of policy; they are differences either about the distribution of seats or about ques- tions which are not yet within practical politics, land may never be so, unless the two parties can

Punch.":

combine to make good the ground they have won by their joint efforts. If they cannot do that, they may see a reactionary Government returned by a minority vote and put in a position to erect entrenchments against further progressive poli- cies which it may take years to pull down. According to all the evidence now before us, there is an abundant force to carry the Govern- ment forward with a fresh impetus, if it can only be organised and concentrated on the work in hand. The contrast between seats and votes in the three elections which have just been concluded is an object-lesson to the Liberal and Labour parties of what may happen on a large I scale, unless they can set their house in order between the present time and the appeal to the I country. If it is taken to heart, it will be a lesson well learnt even at this cost.—" The Westminster Gazette."

THE COALITION TOUCH

LIBERALS AND LABOUR.

Combination Needed to Make Good Ground Won

BY JOINT EFFORTS.

Mr. Herbert Samuel, in a letter which he writes to the Press, makes up a sum for all the constituencies, 42 in number, which have been polled since the Home Rule Bill was introduced in 1912, and shows that in these 236,000 electors have given their votes to candidates pledged to Home Rule and 201,000 to candidates opposed to Home Rule. Split candidatures have pre- vented the Government from reaping the fruits of this substantial majority in the division

"A SOLDIER IN THE MAKING."

British Tirmy Film Creates a Sensation at the Star Kinema House.

ARR: GREAT

About twelve officers and 90 of the Territorials paraded at the Drill Hall on Monday night, and, accompanied by the band and drummers, marched to the Star Kinema House, Church - street, to vie* the Army film. The Mayor headed the procession in an open carriage, and had a splendid reception at the picture house from the large number of civilians who were present. During the evening a flashlight photo- graph was taken, and Major Green presented a long service and good conduct medal to Sergt. E. Shone, of the South Lancashire Regiment. The MAYOR said that the British Army was often slandered by people who knew nothing about it; therefore the management happily ar- ranged for the exhibition of the film, which had been shown before the King and Queen, and many celebrities. The daily life of the soldier was illustrated, and he was sure that after the audience had seen the pictures they would take no notice of the absurd statements made about recruiting the services. He hoped that the re- sult of the exhibition would he a great increase in the number of recruits. (Applause.) Major GREEN thanked the management for ex- hibiting the film, and added that the medal which he presented to Sergeant Shone was just as difficult to get as the Victoria Cross, mid to obtain it a soldier had to go through a certain routine at home. He had to serve at least 18

EFFECTIVE WILD FLOWERS. While passing a wood the other day we were very much struck by the effect obtained from our common Bluebell mixed with the Greater Stitchwort or Satin Flower, Stellaria. Holostea The wood was a mass of blue, while on the edge of it were masses of the Stellaria growing among the Bluebells. The pure white of the Stitchwort formed a striking contrast to the deep blue. This is only one of the numerous instances how many of our native flowers could be used with good effect, particularly in some of the wilder parts of the garden.--" The Garden!!

===== 0002601_19140530_art0067.txt | included=true | included =====
MANUAL TRAINING.

The New Cult of Instructing School

Children.

PREHISTORIC MAR'S ART.

Prehistoric man seemed to be more adept in the use of his hands than modern man. This was the impression one formed after listening to two addresses delivered at a meeting of the Educational Handwork Association, a national body of teachers, held in the Co-operative Hall, Warrington, on Saturday afternoon. One lecturer, Dr. Thomas, of the London Edu- cation Authority, alleged that the lack of com- bining manual with mental instruction spoiled the true effect of education. Mr. Legge, Direc- tor of Education, Liverpool, showed, mainly through the aid of a lantern, how extremely clever the worthy people who lived in the Flint Age were in the use of their hands, although they had the crudest stone instruments with which they had to execute their work. A large audience were much interested by the addresses. AN ANNUAL CONFERENCE. •

The second annual conference of the associa- tion was held earlier in the day in the 'Town Hall. Delegates attended from all over the country. Mr. Sermr, secretary and treasurer, Hudders- field, reported that great interest in true educa- tional methods was now taken by teachers, and that was reflected in the formation of many new branches of the association. In 1910 there were 12 branches. This year there were 60 branches, with a total membership of 6,930. The income was £614, and there was an adverse balance of .e2OO. The report was adopted and the following offi- cials were re-elected President The Right lion. H. D. Acland, a former Minister for Education; secretary of the General Council, Professor Green, Sheffield; director of Summer Schools, Mr. Tipping, Bradford; editor of journal, Mr, Johnson, Liverpool; secretary and treasurer, Mr. Spittle, Huddersfield. MAYOR'S EARLY DAYS. The MAYOR presided at the public gathering, and, in welcoming the delegates, commended the objects of the association. He was sure their body was doing a great work on behalf of the future race. He wished there had been such a scheme in his young days. If there had he would have done his carpenter's work, of which he was extremely fond, at the school bench, instead of on the chairs and sofa at home. (Laughter.) He hoped their deliberations in Warrington would prove beneficial to the country, and he regretted there was not a larger attendance of 1 local teachers to hear the addresses. Unfortu- nately for that meeting there was a thanksgiving service at the Parish Church, and he understood that amongst teachers there were some very religious ones. (Laughter.) He hoped, however, their deliberations would be successful. Dr. C. J. TnomAs dealt with education of the brain, aided by the hands. It was only recently they realised that one depended upon the other. Education consisted of bringing about actual physical changes in the brain. Every lesson made an adjustment in the child's brain, and that was strengthened by repetition and prac- tical lessons made the great impression. The play of a child demonstrated that fact. In one the child used its muscles to prepare itself for man or womanhood, and that was a natural education. There were two kinds of play: (1) the boisterous exercise of the funda- mental musbles; and (2) the exercise of the brain. • It was the latter they wished to develop from the educational standpoint. That 'orm of play lay at the root of all education. (Hear, hear.)

===== 0002601_19140530_art0068.txt | included=true | included =====
Lessens from Defectives._

It was impossible iCt-ed-u:a-te defective children except through the muscles, but why should that mode of education be restricted to the defectives? Why should they not use it to develop normal children? (Hear, hear.) There was a certain amount of instability in

_ the more highly educated not found in the artisan. They got more hysterical manifesta- tions from the highly educated, and, dealing with that fact, one of their greatest brain specialists had given it as his considered opinion that educated brains had, within the past fifty years, lost fifty per cent. of their staying power.

===== 0002601_19140530_art0069.txt | included=true | included =====
Neurasthenia.

Amongst elementary and other teachers in London he found a great amount of neuras- thenia. Among some teachers it was extremely great, but he had yet to come across a single instance of neurasthenia amongst teachers of manual training. (Hear, hear.) There were many more breakdowns amongst art teachers than amongst science teachers. The deduction was that those teachers who combined their hands and brains enjoyed better health than those who worked with their brains alone. That brought them to the point where it be- came imperative that manual and mental in- struction should be combined. If it was so much better would it be for children and teachers.

PREHISTORIC MAN'S ART. Mr. LEGGE, Liverpool, delivered a lantern lec- ture on the art of prehistoric man. Some re- markable pictures of carvings on bone and ivory, found in caves in France and Spain, and dating back perhaps 30,000 years, were shown. Those men, said Mr. Legge, showed remark- able skill. A peculiar feature was that they started first to carve with their flints the figures of human beings. In a later age they concen- trated upon animals. It was believed that to have an animal carved upon their stone weapons cast a certain spell over the animal and made it fall an easier prey when they went hunting. To have carved a human figure would have meant bewitching that person and anyone who, at the later period, attempted to do so would have been promptly felled with a stone club.

Proved by Photography. They showed animals in attitudes which were not believed possible until the advent of instan- taneous photography. Scientists doubted the movements of animals depicted in these engrav- ings until they were proved absolutely accurate by the camera. These engravings were all the more remark- able from the fact that primitive man during his hunting expeditions in the summer studied the movements of the animals, retained the im- pressions of what he saw in his mind until the winter, and then reproduced them in his caves. In proposing a vote of thanks to the speakers, Professor GREEN contended that manual train- ing should be adjunct to mental training. Afterwards the delegates were entertained to tea by the Mayor in the Council Chamber. Mr. LEGGE proposed, and Mr. W. A. T. BROWN seconded, a vote of thanks to the Mayor, which was enthusiastically tarried. AN INTERESTING EXHIBITION.

An interesting and instructive exhibition of handwork was held in the Cairo-street Rooms during the sitting of the Conference. The general object of this is to secure the introduc- tion into the schools of practical work. The children, it was pointed out, could understand theories more easily after having seep practical examples, and the lessons also were rendered more interesting. Splendid exhibits of woodwork, sewing, paper- tearing, clay-modelling, painting and basketry were sent from Coventry, Leyland, Warrington, Widnes, and other towns. The local specimens of work were of a high standard, being the ap- parent outcome of considerable thought and painstaking. Mr. F. Daintith acted as honorary secretary to the Exhibition Committee.

===== 0002601_19140530_art0070.txt | included=true | included =====
ELEPHANT'S CLEVERNESS.

"Speaking of animals, in my opinion the ele- phant is the cleverest of them all," remarked a well-known circus trainer to some Pressmen the other evening. "I remember one in my charge who showed the he could read." "Oh, come now," said a young reporter. "I'll prove it to you in two minutes," replied the trainer. "The elephant had a scrap with a Bengal tiger, and before we could get them separated he had his trunk badly mauled. Immediately after he broke loose and tore down the street. 'He's going wild,' somebody shouted; Don't you be- lieve it,' I cried. Now, where do you suppose he went?" "To the doctor's," retorted the youngster. "Can't you spin a better yarn?" "No he didn't," said the old circus hand. "He went straight up to a little shop in Tottenham Court-road, where a sign said 'Trunks repaired while you wait.' Of course he had made a mis- take, but what could you expect from a poor dumb brute?"

THE EXAMINER, SATURDAY, MAY 30, 1914.

Land Reform Campaign Begun in

Warrington.

different nations to act as sane individuals, rather than crazy fanatics, in defiling with these questions. The day will come, and, when that day comes, it will mean not only better provision for old age, but it will mean further progress along the path of democratic reform and national advancement. (Applause.) We are fighting the battle of the entire demo- cracy, and Tories, certainly Tories amongst the middle and working classes of England stand to gain by our victories just as much i as we Liberals do. (A Voice: "They don't deserve it.") But they get it. We are generous folk; we share and share alike with them. (Laughter and applause.) I would rather put it simply, and say that the entire nation of England stands to gain by this work of constructive social reform. And so we ask for your firm, resolute, unflinch- ing, unflagging determination to strengthen the hands of the Government which is carrying through this great and blessed work; to strengthen the hands of the far-sighted, sagacious and resolute statesman who is lead- ing us; and to back up the man who himself sprung from the people, and who, feeling in- tensely, sympathetically and deeply with them, is striving, working and fighting with all his genius, inspiration and ability for the welfare of our community. (" Lloyd George" and cheers.)

SERIES OF EPITHUSIASTIC jffETINGS.

Mr.

Crosfield Leads New Start Struggle for Freedom.

in the

IVithin a few hours of the passing of the Rome Rule Bill on Monday the note for the next political fiffht in %%arrington was sounded at the first of a series of Land Reform meetings.

Mr. Crostield was present to start the fight, which he did most effectively, ringing .out the old and ringing in the new programme in most felicitous fashion. H.- The campaign has been continued each night during the week with will go on through next week. tr. urst meeting of the campaign was held ildge Foot on Monday, when an exception- . Dresi,4tge audience assembled. Mr. C. E. Parker and the speakers were Mr. A. H. Cros- ,' 111...,kwh0 was given a hearty reception) and f. 4 }never Todd, of Manchester. THE CHILD.

Tice moment was most opportune. The old struggle of thirty years' standing had become a thing of the past, and the nation passed on to strive for those betterments in the con- dition of the masses, the way to which has been blocked by the demand for home Rule.

a

There is no great business in the kingdom where it is so impossible for the principals efficiently to follow the work done, let •alone supervise its detail. You are bound to unload, and no one knows it better than the more statesmanlike half of the Unionist party themselves. CONSERVATIVES AGAINST THE OLD ROTTEN SYSTEM. They took this anti-Home Rule—anti-devolu- tion—position up 30 years ago, and, 30 years ago, let us be frank, they had a majority of the nation with them. What about majorities now? They have had three elections in the last eight years, and at every one of them a large majority has been returned in favour of this policy of devolution, which we call Home Rule, and which is bound to develop, as sure as you and I stand here, to the advantage of every Man Jack amongst us, into a federal system for the United Kingdom, not as a mere matter of ex- pediency, but as a matter of prime necessity. And what about majorities? I see they have been boastfully sticking up posters about Ips- wich. (Laughter.) It only shows how liar. put to it the Tory party are in these clays that they dare to talk about by-elections, because, for a Government that has been in power as we have been for eight years past, the record of by-elec- tions and the figures shown at these by-elections in favour of the policy of Home Rule is quite extraordinary. I should have thought that was a fact that might weigh with our Tory oppo- nents, and I will tell you why. If there is one thing more than another that you hear on Tory platforms, whether they be Tories speaTcing from the House of Lords or the House of Com- mons, it is their constant declaration that they will always bow to the will of the people. (Laughter.) They ignore all that now, and instead, you see them prepared to-day to go to all lengths, however unconstitutional, however disorderly, however revolutionary they may be— ready to stick at nothing if they think they have a ghost of a chance of getting their own way. (Hear,. hear.) What is the cause of it all? Now, does all this violence in the Tory party spring from their devotion to some great principle? Are they so confirmed in their belief in principle that they think it is out of the question to listen to any proposals in the nature of com- promise or anything of that kind? Our Tory friends can plead nothing of the kind, because their whole record on the matter is —against them. I am sure I see friends of mine here who have as vivid a recollection as I have of the little hanky-panky work that went on in 1885. (Hear, hear.) I am glad to see there are those here who have not forgotten it. After the Nationalists and the Conservatives had ousted the Liberal party, there was a conference be- tween Lord Carnarvon and Mr. Parnell. Opinions differ about that conference, because there does not happen to be any record in black and white. One thing is certain, that a great deal was said during that conference that shows how far Lord Carnarvon himself wanted to go in the direction of governing Ireland accord- ing to Irish ideas; the Tory party were backing him up at the time, repealing the Crimes Act, and doing what they could to creep up Parnell's : sleeve. All I say is this: That is not the action of those who are high and dry opponents of , Home Rule at any price; those who won't listen to devolution or compromise of any kind. In 1902 the then Conservative Lord-Lieutenant of 1 Ireland, Lord Dudley, making his tour through ;Ireland, said he was in favour of putting an end to the old rotten system, and of governing : Ireland according to the intelligence and ideas of Ireland. (Applause.) At the same time you had Mr. George Wyndham working for devolu- tion. With experience gained in Ireland, these men, like Lord Carnarvon before them, were strongly in favour of the policy of devolution. and was not Mr. Balfour sanctioning it? Of course, he knew what was going on.

BIGOTRY APPLIES A CHECK. Then a little bigoted group took fright and offence, and through their mouthpiece, through their leader, Sir Edward Carson—(Voices : "Oh! Anarchist! Don't mention him! and 'Call him King '")—I am too loyal to call him King. (Cheers.) lam exceedingly glad to know, from the way these remarks are received, you have jolly well taken his measure. He went to Mr. Balfour with the Orange group, and they put a pistol to his head—(Voices: "A bad job it did not go off" and "Was it a wooden gun?")—l don't know which way our friend wanted it to go off. (Laughter and a Voice: " The pair of them are no good.") Well, I'd rather leave violence to the other side. We don't know the ins and outs, no one outside the inner circles does; it has never been published, but it is a most important and significant fact that these leading and responsible Tory statesmen preached devolution and the government of Ire- land according to Irish ideas, the only sensible way in which you can carry on a vast govern- mental concern like the United Kingdom. (Cheers.) After referring to the Constitutional Confer- ence three and a half years ago, when half the influential Tory organs were in favour of devolu- tion, Mr. Crosfield dealt with a fourth instance, saying: It is very significant at the present time that the most reputable, weighty and worthy section of the Tory party are themselves in favour of devolution, but the " Die-hards " (laughter)—l have mentioned no names, but you know who constitute that elegant and edifying group who go by the name of "Die- hards" in Parliament. Those gentlemen have got the upper hand in their party, and if Ipswich encorages them to go on with their tricks I am sorry for them, and from a national .point of view it is to be regretted. They are not going to succeed. (Applause.) I dare say there are not a few political friends of mine here, in Warrington, who think that the Gov- ernment have stood the nonsense of some of these people a little too long—(hear, hear)—and who think that we have heard about enough of concessions. (Hear, hear.) Well, I am one of those who, as years go by, more and more, from the bottom of my heart, can say in all sincerity that I trust Asquith. (Hear, hear, and ap- plause.) A leader of more sagacity, with more calmness, foresight, quiet determination and superb intellectual capacity I do not think we have ever had. (Applause.) I think it is a good thing, therefore, that the olive branch is held out to the last moment. Of course, there is a limit to it. (Hear, hear.) The Prime Minister has got patience, but he has also got determination, and I say to Mr. Harold Smith, and any other Member who encourages these practices, that there is a limit to the amount of their nonsense we will stand— (hear, hear)—because we want to see Ireland get its chance. We want to see self-government —so long a reality in this,country, never as yet, except for a brief period in the 18th century, a reality in that—made a reality for the reason

increasing success.

that whatever drawbacks it may have, if it is self-government under modern democratic forms it does help to spread and extend equality of opportunity and gives to the man of prudence, energy, industry and capacity his best chance to get for himself and his family a fair reward for his labour. I say that of Ireland and for ourselves. Well, have we not got our hands full with work to be done at home here?

===== 0002601_19140530_art0078.txt | included=true | included =====
A MINIMUM WAGE.

We have got a great work to do, and a great deal has been accomplished. During the last eight years we have put measures on the Statute Book and done work that will for all time be looked back upon as a wonderful period in English history. (Hear, hear.) Referring to "one land-mark, one great mile- stone in the National journey we are all tread- ing "—the Trades Boards Act—Mr. Crosfield said: That is an Act which for the first time in our history introduced the principle of the minimum wage. Parliament began, as it ought to begin, with the most crying cases of sweat- ing, in the chain-making and box-making in- dustries, and in certain branches of tailoring, &c. It has been extended to several more trades. You have got the principle approved, and you ' have got to put it into practice. I don't say that the Wages in these trades are anything to boast of now, but I do say that they have gone up 50, 60 and more per cent, above what they were be- fore. (Applause.) If you were with me at these conferences on labour questions, which I attend abroad nowadays, you would be struck by the intense interest with which democratic repro- , sentatives of foreign countries discuss and pon- der on the work of the British Parliament in passing the Trades Boards Act; it strikes them 'as being so immense a departure. Since then we have had another very striking illustration. You have had /the minimum wage applied to one of the greatest industries in the country —the coal mines—which is probably not one of the industries where it was most essential, but still, here is my point: you get that principle adopted in the case of an industry which is not a sweated industry. Now we come to the agricultural labourer, and, alas! in many a county in Eng- land his is a sweated industry. There are a few places in the North where you could not accurately describe it so. as compared with the rest of the country. It is not entirely, but still largely a sweated industry, and so we are going to apply that principle of a minimum wage to the agricultural labourer. (Applause.) I am sure you feel that is another great and terribly needed step in advance. (Hear, hear.) The Chairman has spoken to you about decent housing accommodation. It is esti- mated that we are short of proper cottage accommodation in Britain by something like 120,000 cottages. We are going to have these cot- tages built. When that is done; when you get this pressure on housing accommodation—which is, of course, the inevitable result of a shortage-- relieved there will be a tendency towards the eas- ing of rent. To begin with, rents will not be arti- ficially forced up as they are at present, but ' the tendency will be for them to come to a more natural and more healthy level. The agricul- tural labourer will have a wage which will enable him to pay an economic rent for his cottage. The change, with that and the rest of the programme, is so enormous that you are bound to stop the excessive flow of agricultural labourers from the country into the towns. I need not tell you what sort of effect that will have upon the wages of the town labourer. Have you heard that in Oxford City they can get tram drivers for 2s. 6d. per day? Do you think that has no relation to the fact that Oxford happens to be a county in England where agricultural labourers receive the poorest wages? Why, the one thing is a con-1 sequence of the other. (A Voice: " How is it your firm is buying property, pulling it down, and there are no houses in which to go and live?") I don't want you to be under a misapprehension, but I am not responsible for what is taking place at the pre- sent time. I am quite ready to answer for any mortal thing I have ever done, and for which I am responsible. (Hear, hear, and applause.) If you stop the unnatural, evil flow of agricul- tural labourers from the country districts to the towns; if you help to build up a larger population on the soil, in the fresh air of the country, it will be a God-send to the labourer in the town. It will be, also, a good thing for Old England. (Applause.) WHY TORIES ARE IN A FRENZY. . . .

Mr. Crosfield continued: That is not the only thing we are doing. I am going to say a word about what has happened since the passing of the last two Budgets. It is 25 or 30 years now since I first discussed, with my political friends and more recently supporters in Warrington, this great question of graduated taxation. In the days of the 'Bo's and '9o's we were powerless, but now we are in a position to give effect to the work we are doing, and that is why the "Die- hard " section are in such a violent frenzy. They see, under the Parliament Act, that our Statutes approach completion, and their frenzy reaches boiling point. When they get like that you have to treat them like spoiled children, and, if they get into a passion, give them a whacking. (Laughter.) We are at last masters in our own house—(hear, hear)—and the work that is being done concerns every man and woman. The triumph of our work will be bene- ficial to all the working class and middle class. —to Tories and Liberals alike. It will be a bless- ing for the whole nation. (Hear, hear.) To get the means to develop our great scheme of social reform, we have put into practice what we have talked about for 25 or 30 years past. By the provisions of the Budgets of 1909 and that introduced the other day, taking the increase on death duties, the graduation of income tax and the increase on the super tax—l am giving you a fair and reliable estimate when I tell you that, by that principle of graduated taxation, so boldly ap- plied, the nation's resources have been increased by £24,000,000 a year. (Applause.) Just think what £24,000,000 applied to better education, better housing, better provision against sick- ness will do now. I want you to consider what we could do if, while we gain £24,000,000 by that increased taxation, we were able to cut down this monstrous expenditure on, arma- ments, which has reached .Q 50,000,000 per year for the,Navy alone. Just think what we should be able to do in the future when we have cut that down, and it won't be many years before we can do it. - -

WHY IRELAND SHOULD GOVERN HERSELF.

An Old-Age Pension Illustration.

The CHAIRMAN said he was glad Mr. Crosfield had spoken of Lloyd George. He would advise all those present to read Harold Begbie's book, "The Lady Next Door," which was better than all the pamphlets on Home Rule. A doctor had said to the author: "I hope when he dies the Devil will get his (Lloyd George's) soul." (Loud laughter) Asked why he had such ilapple. If they antipathy against the Chancellor, the doctor said "I am rung to go and see, old at all hours of the night have a pain in the toe, or are short of breath, I am rung up at all hours. The care taken of those old people in order that they may get a pension is astonishing." (Laughter and applause.) In told that an old-age Pension of ss. per week for epoenall:leersoa:ieornn with a Bishop, Mr. Begbie was alstoo much, and the Bishop explained it in the following words: "Our scale of living in Ireland is a good deal lower than yours•in England. It is out of proportion to the earnings of these people to give them a ss. pension when they are 70. It would be far bet- ter if, instead of giving them ss. at 70, you started it at 60 and gave them about 3s. 6d. That is where the awkwardness of your govern- ing Ireland by the same laws as the English conies in. Our conditions are entirely different, and if we had to, we could do it, far better by adjusting our laws to our conditions. (Ap- plause.) You are a wealthy people and try to govern, by the same laws and regulations, people a good deal poorer than yourselves, and that is proving disastrous." (Applause.)
