A TOLERANT GOVERNMENT. -

Mr. TODD raised much enthusiasm when he announced that the Home Rule Bill had passed in the House of Commons at afternoon. There was still an peace in Ulster, because the Liberal Government was sufficiently toler- ant to recognise that the people of Ulster had Amending thirdeno,dcil nocgrkeßatiiliilanttgo d give prejudices, and was willing to give concessions in order that the Ulsterites might look on the Home Rule Act as not quite so bad as they thought it. Under those conditions Home Rule for Ireland would be likely to meet with more success, because it would be met with keener enthusiasm by the greater number of people throughout the length and breadth of Ireland, instead of only one part of it. (Hear, hear.)

THE DROWNED CAT.

What were they th fight the next election on ? (A Voice: "Tariff Reform.") Tariff Reform on one hand—(A Voice: "It is a drowned cat.") Mr. Crosfield had been saying that Tariff Reform was on its death-bed and now someone suggested it was drowned. (A Voice: "It has had a resurrection.") They got inside information from the "Central Unionist and Conservative and Liberal Unionist Association," which, in a recent pamphlet, gave the Conservative policy against the Liberal policy in matters connected with land. In the whole of that pamphlet Tariff Reform was not mentioned, except in very small print in a footnote. Tariff Reform, in the opinion of the Conservatives themselves, had come down to a footnott3. (A Voice: "Tlicy don't think so at Ipswich.") But they thought so at the offices of the party if they were to judge from their own pamphlet. (Hear, hear.)

===== 0002601_19140530_art0082.txt | included=true | included =====
BACK TO THE LAND.

_ After referring, at length to the general and beneficial effect of Liberal legislation the speaker dealt with the land question, instancing at the outset the increase of 10 to 15 per cent. increase in agricultural rents in the Midlands in the last 10 years. The price of agricultural land had gone up 30 per cent., but the agricuitural worker got no benefits at all. The Liberals were going to make sure that the agriculturil lab- ourer had some share in that increaseti pros- perity. The farmer was going to have security of tenure, compensation for disturbances, and compensation for all money he had put into the soil. The land which had not been used was going to be put to the lest possible uses for the good of the community. REMEDIAL MEASURES.

The meagre pittances of agricultural labour- ers in certain districts game in for caustic criti- cism, as also did the disgusting housing condi- tions of the present day. There were two reme- dial methods, said the speaker, one was to deal with rating in the towns, and the other was to do with the price of land. The principle in the new Budget was that there should be a division between the rates levied, on houses, factories and improvements of all kinds on land, and, on the other hand, on the land on which the im- provements were erected. Those who owned land were making too much out of the enterprise and expenditure of the towns, and the Liberals said those people should pay a larger portion of rates than they did. (Hear, hear.) The effect of the Budget proposal would be to reduce the rates of certain towns by anything from Is. and is. sd. HOUSING AND TOWN PLANNING. The Town planning Scheme was also detailed by Mr. Todd, who said they wanted instead of 80 houses, 12 houses to the acre. They wanted more open spaces, more public parks, recreation grounds, Sx., but for all these improvements they needed" land. The local 'authorities had a great amount of difficulty in getting the neces- sary land for improvements, but that was going to be minimised. The local authorities were going to be enabled to go into the market and buy land at a reasonable price, without all the enormous cost surrounding arbitration proceed- ings, not only for present, but future require- ments. There would be no confiscation in the Liberal proposals and the landowner would be given a fair price for his land. The cost of officials for the land scheme would cost as much as a torpedo boat, and that was comparatively a small amount for officials who were going to see wages raised, better housing conditions and reductions in rates. (Hear, hear.) VOX POPULL

The people and not the Government would be responsible for the election of those who would govern in their own towns, and they should put into office in Warrington people whom they could trust to carry out Liberal legislation in the spirit in which it was intended. (Loud applause.) COUNCILLOR BROADHURST'S QUESTIONS Councillor BROADHURST (who was amongst the crowd) said he would like to ask Mr. Crosfield (who had by this time left the meeting) if it were not a fact that on the appeal to a demo- cratic country on the 1909 Budget the Liberal Government lost 100 seats? • The CHAIRMAN said that Mr. Crosfield would be at other meetings, and the questioner would then have an opportunity of asking his question. Councillor BaoArmunsr : The speaker corrobor- ated a statement as he has been dealing with public authorities. (A Voice: "Shut up!") Is it not a fact that the Liberal Government stopped every British working man who was thrifty from getting his own house through the Corporation by defeating Boscawen's Bill? Mr. TODD: Yes; because that Bill makes hous- ing a charge on either the town or the State, and that is simply a method of subsidising wages out of rates and taxes. (Applause.) The Liberal Government's proposal is that: " That the industries in which men are engaged should bear the cost of paying living wages, and then the workers themselves will not be in the posi- tion of paupers, but will be able to pay for the houses themselves out of the living wage." (Ap- plause.) Mr. SANDERS: We shall be pleased to see Coun- cillor Broadhurst or any other member who wishes to ask questions at the meetings.

===== 0002601_19140530_art0083.txt | included=true | included =====
TO REVERSE THE MISTAKE OF THE LAST ELECTION.

At the outset the CHAIRMAN said that by the passing of the Home Rule Bill the previous day, by a majority of 77, the way had been cleared for the great and vital social reforms which were so much overdue. Evils at present con- nected with the land would be swept away. Agricultural labourers who were now in receipt of only 18s. 4d., on an average, per week, would be given a living wage, and that would greatly affect the wages of labourers in the towns, where, because of the poor wages in the country the agricultural labourers were flocking. By reason of the fact that the agricultural labour- ers were paid sweated wages they could not live in decent houses, and, therefore, slums were created. Slums were a thing to be cleared away, for, where slums existed, there was over- crowding, and, as a consequence, immorality. A fact to be very much regretted was that in every town in England there were these plague spots. The Liberal party said it was not right that people should be huddled up in towns or cities when the problem could be solved by tackling the land question. (Applause.) He asked them to support Mr. Crosfield at the next election, and so reverse the political mis- take they made in Warrington at the last elec- tion. By returning their Liberal candidate they would strengthen the hands of the Gov- ernment, who were going to give the land back to the people, in order that they could reap its produce for their own good, and also for the benefit of the populatioa at large. (Applause.)

HOUSING AND WAGES.

Vital Questions that the Gonernmeu will Tactle.

LOWER RENTS; HIGHER WAGES.

£l,OOO AN ACRE.

What Warrington Paid for Land for ScHool in Lovelg Lane.

===== 0002601_19140530_art0084.txt | included=true | included =====
SLUM PROPERTY TO GIVE WAY TO HEALTHY DWELLINGS.

Mr. JONES, after a passing reference to the " curious time we had recently been passing through in politics," said there were plenty of men in the Tory party who were glad to see Home Rule was out of the way, for they knew it was good for England, Ireland, and the Empire, to have that question settled once and for all. (Hear, hear.) They knew that when they got the two great factions, the Protestants and the Roman Catholics, joined together in the task of governing themselves, Ireland would be a happier and more prosperous nation. (Ap- plause.) The Government were going to deal effectively with the land question, and in regard to the housing question, dependent on a solution of the land question, he could not help wondering, as he came into Warrington through the outskirts, what percentage of the people lived in slums. He did not know Warrington as a town, good or bad, but he did know that if they' took the towns of England as a whole they would find 10 to 12 per cent. of the people lived in slum property. They had all those people living under unhealthy conditions from the point of sanitation and morality, which could only re- sult in the gradual deterioration of the nation. The sooner that awful problem was tackled the better it would be for England and the world as a whole. (Hear, hear.) The Housing and Town Planning Act had provided a large part of the necessary machinery for tackling that festering sore of the nation. Slum property would be cleared out, and houses, with plenty of air space, would be built in their places. (Applause.)

Iniquitous Rating System. It was, however, not the mere question of slum property they had to consider, but the question of rating, which was amost iniquitous thing. A man was rated on his own improve- ments. If a man had a little shop and worked the business up so that enlargement and im- provements were necessary, if he made it more attractive, the rating overseer came round and the energetic shopkeeper's rates went up. He was penalised for his own industry and success. Next to the shopkeeper there might be a plot of vacant land, and the owner of that idle plot did not contribute to the rates. Ile was only waiting until he could get an outrageous price for his land, before he would sell. That differ- ence was to be equalised. In the future some of the rates would fall on the site values. (Hear, hear.) Under the present system the hard-working, industrious person had to pay, whilst the idle rich got off scot free. (Hear, hear.)

===== 0002601_19140530_art0085.txt | included=true | included =====
Slums Infested.

If they could keep the agricultural labourers to their own ground in the country, it would, to a large extent, relieve the tension in the town labour markets. Agricultural labourers came to the town and infested the slum property, because they were not sharp enough in the towns to get big wages. They also made the conditions of wages and houses far worse for the town workers. The Liberal Government was going to remedy such coliditions by making it compulsory that a certain rate of decent wages should be paid in each particular dis- trict. When the agricultural labourers had got the minimum wage, the Liberals were going to turn their attention to their houses. There i were thousands of cottages n the country which were not fit for pigs to live in. Houses would be built that would be healthy in every way. (Applause.)

Vote Liberal and be Healthy. Some people might ask from where was the money to come. As a result of Liberal legisla- tion reserve funds had been set up on the Insur- ance Act, and, by having less sickness as a re- sult of better housing, the reserve funds would be increased, and so the good work would go on. The land scheme was like a great river, which i did good as t flowed along, improving the con- ditions of everyone. He would ask them to think that matter over very seriously, and to think whether they would not be wise to give their whole-hearted support to the Liberal partY• He could understand a rich man voting Tory, because, after all, the Liberal party had made it hard for those with the big incomes, by mak- ing them pay their fair share towards the national taxation. He could not, however, understand how it was that any working man who thought and reasoned for himself could, at any election, place his vote opposite the name of a man, other than a Liberal. The Liberal party had done as much as possible up to now for theworking classes, and they were going to do more for the great democracy, the great wage- earning classes of the country. (Hear, hear.) If the people gave the Liberal party their trust, confidence and the power they could make War- rington, and all the other towns, healthier, wealthier, and better than they were at the 'present time. He urged themi all to do their best to ensure the return of Mr. Crosfield, and send Harold Smith back to Liverpool, or from wherever he hailed. (Applause.)

A GOOD AUTHORITY. A QUESTIONER: What guarantee have we that there will be a minimum wage for agricultural labourers? Mr. JONES: I have it on the authority of the Government, as stated in Mr. Lloyd George's speeches. Boards will be set up in each dis- trict something oti the lines adopted under the Trades Boards Act. There will be a different Board for each district, for the rates of pay will be different, say, between places in Oxford- shire and elsewhere. Ido not think any agri- cultural labourer in the country will be paid less than 225. per week. On the proposition of Mr. SANDERS a hearty vote of thanks was accorded the speaker and the Chairman, the latter replying in suitable terms.

===== 0002601_19140530_art0086.txt | included=true | included =====
Methods by which theg are to bring

about these Reforms.

The chief aspects of the question dealt with by Mr. Enever Todd, at a well-attended meeting at the corner of Hardy-street and Scott-street on Wednesday evening, were those in relation to wages and housing. Councillor llenshall pre- sided and briefly opened the meeting.

===== 0002601_19140530_art0087.txt | included=true | included =====
RIVAL METHODS FOR RAISING

WAGES.

Mr. TODD said, as he had been told that he was going to speak in a Conservative quarter of the town, he would point out that in regard to the land question, there were many Conserva- tives in the House of Commons and the House of Lords who had already expressed themselves in favour of some of the Government land pro- posals. In investigating such subjects as hous- ing, the proper control of the land monopoly, the conditions under which farmers were tenants on the land, and the conditions under which shop- keepers plied their trade, the Government had been driven from one point to another, until it had come to the final decision that nothing sub- stantial or final could be done without tackling the question of wages first of all. They could not get decent houses so long as a very large pro- portion of wage-earners in the country and in the towns were unable to pay a reasonable rent for the houses that were built, and the cost of the land. The Tory scheme for raising wages was Tariff Reform. Although they were not saying much about it now, lie did think that if a Tory Government was returned they would find that perhaps the first thing they would do would be to put Tariff Reform into practice. ' He entirely failed to see how Tariff Reform, by putting an import duty on foreign produce com- ing into the country, was going to compel em- ployers to cease paying sweated wages. The ing of decent wages under a new fiscal system would depend on the goodwill of the employer, or whether he was made to pay a decent wage. Tariff Reform, in the opinion of the Tory leaders, was an indirect means for raising wages. The Liberal Government, how- ever, was determined to go straight to the point and bring direct action to bear on the matter. (Hear, hear.) Some people said it was beyond the power of Parliament to raise wages, but Parliament had proved that to be an erroneous statement.

===== 0002601_19140530_art0088.txt | included=true | included =====
Help for the Weak.

The Liberal Government said that those workers who were receiving under £1 per week could not help themselves, and they were de- termined to see to it that those workers re- ceived such a wage as to giv'e them a bargaining power as against the bargaining power of the employers. The first thing the Government wanted to do was to see that the minimum wage should be paid to the agricultural labourers, and the second thing was to see that the mini- mum wage legislation should be more rapidly extended to all other industries in the towns, where, upon inquiry, it was found that any- thing approaching a sweated wage was being paid. The minimum wage would be sufficient to cover the cost of decent food and the rent of a decent habitable house. (Hear, hear.) The agricultural labourers had not been able to form a trade union up to the present. By giving them a minimum wage and better houses, they would also help on the trade unions by the mere fact that those workers would be in possession of a bargaining power for themselves. (Hear, hear.) That proposal would be carried out in the next Parliament, if the Liberal Government were returned. (Applause.)

===== 0002601_19140530_art0089.txt | included=true | included =====
To Cheapen House Rent.

But it was no use getting higher wages for the workers if they had got to pay more for what they bought, and the charge for rent took a big sum out of £1 a week nowadays. Before they could clear away slums they had to build.other houses in which to house the people, and before they could build houses to let at a reasonable rent to the workers, the cost of the dwellings must be cheapened. The Gov- ernment were going to tackle the rating system and the land question as a solution to the pres- ent difficulties. For a man paying 6s. per week, for rent in Warrington, it meant that he was paying is. 6d. or 2s. of that in rates. The working men were paying about Is. out of 20s. for the rates, but what were the wealthy people paying? It had been calculated that a man who paid -super tax, a man with .£5,000 per year income, was paying Is. out of every 100 s. in rates. It was not fair; it was obviously un- just that a working man should pay is. out 205., whilst the wealthy people only paid is. out of every 100 s. The Chancellor was going to see to it that the taxation was placed on a more just and equitable basis. (Hear, hear.) They pro- posed that where land had been improved, built upon and made available for industrial pur- poses, relief would be given in that direction by transferring the rates to where The land had not been improved or built upon. That, in Birmingham, would mean a reduction of Is. sd. in the .6 on the rates of the working men. (Ap- plause.) That was a beginning, and anyone could see that if they reduced the rates on houses by Is. sd. in the .6 they were going to give a stimulus to builders of houses.

Control of the Land. No longer was the enterprising town to be handicapped by the landowner, for local author- ities would have the power to acquire the land for their requirements, such as improving the houses of the working classes and their general conditions. Judicial Commissioners would be set up to act in cases where a dispute arose be- tween the authority and the landowner. The object of the land campaign was to control by fair and legitimate means the land monopoly of the country, and to control it in just those ways where injustices and grievances were recognised as existing under the present system by mem- bers of all political parties. It was a business- like campaign to do something sure and certain for the health and welfare of the lowest paid classes in the whole community. (Hear, hear.) The health of the community would be at the highest possible pitch so far as legislation and the Liberal Government could make it, and if the country was to maintain its manufacturing supremacy amongst the countries of the world; if it were to go on increasing its industry and its wealth in the future, at the same or a greater rate than it had done in the past, then the first consideration was the health of the community, which the Liberal Government was going to improve. (Applause.)

===== 0002601_19140530_art0090.txt | included=true | included =====
PLURAL VOTERS TO CO.

Mr. SANDERS reviewed the political situation of the last few weeks, and said that before the next election was fought plural voting would be a thing of the past. The settlement of that great injustice to the working man had been too long in abeyance, and the Government were determined to remain in power until the Plural Voting Bill had become an Act of Parliament. There would not be a repetition of Ipswich, where there were 1,700 plural voters.

There would not be such a thing as one man having six votes against the working man's one. Such a state of affairs was unjust and unfair, and the Government had made up its mind that the working man should be on an equal footing with all his fellows at the ballot box, despite snap divisions, gun-running, lodgers coming to Warrington at the beck and call of Harold Smith, or any of the silly Tory dodges. The working men would benefit by such an Act, and it was their duty to do all that lay in their power to return the " Government for the people." (Applause.) He proposed a vote of thanks to the speaker and chairman, which was carried.

===== 0002601_19140530_art0091.txt | included=true | included =====
LAND FOR WORKERS.

Mr. Martindale and the Liberal Proposals.

There was a large attendance at thve meeting held at the corner of Lovely-lane on Thursday, to listen to the able and eloquent exposition of the land proposals by Mr. J. B. Martindale (Liberal candidate for the Widnes Division).

COUNCILLOR HAWTHORN AND TIIE "MANTLE OF GLADSTONE."

Councillor HAWTHORN, who presided, said they ought to congratulate themselves that in Mr. Asquith they had a leader upon whose shoulders the mantle of Mr. Gladstone had surely fallen; he had carried to the final the Act which "the Grand Old Man" was unable to do. (Applause.) Now that Home Rule and Welsh Disestablish- ment were out of the way, they could devote their attention to the other pressing questions, one of which was in regard to land, which everyone was agreed ought to be faced with the least possible delay. (Hear, hear.) They all sincerely, hoped that Mr. Martindale would be returned for Widnes, and when plural voting was a thing of the past, as it shortly would be, he would have a far better and fairer chance. (Applause.)

===== 0002601_19140530_art0092.txt | included=true | included =====
EMIGRATION.

Mr. Martindale Blames the English _

Land System. Mr. MARTINDALE, in his racy manner, familiar to Warrington audiences, dealt with the various aspects of the land huestion, but at the outset said they were quite confident that if the people in Warrington realised what Liberalism meant to them and the whole country, they would set a lead to Widnes at the next election by turn- ing Harold Smith out and putting Mr. Cros- field in. (Applause.) Now the Liberal Government had settled great issues, the Tories were asking, "What is going to be your programme?" The Liberal party had got plenty of programmes to last a life time; plenty of work to do before the country was a satisfactory place for the people. (Hear, hear.) They turned now to what must be the demand of the whole country—a demand in favour of some alteration of the land system, which would give them greater opportunities and more free- dom than they had at the present time. There was very little land in this country, and that was all the more reason why they should put it to the best possible use. Land was not being put to its best economic use at the present, and, as a result, labour and the masses of the people lied to suffer. When, as they intended, they had given the agricultural labourers a fair and decent wage, it would not be long before the wages of the workers in the towns were raised. (Hear, hear.) When they had agricultural labourers getting low wages, the whole indus- trial system was thrown out of balance, and men were driven across the seas, not by Free Trade, but by the present land system. (Hear, hear.) LAND FOR LABOUR, NOT PHEASANTS. They said to the wealthy landowners: "-You can have your sport in the country, but not at the expense of the country, and if that land can be useful for cultivation purposes it must be used for that purpose, rather than for feed- ing rabbits and pheasants." The Duke of Marl- borough said that pheasants were food. "Well," continued the speaker, "I suppose people do eat pheasants, but if you went into an agricultural labourer's cottage and Bair the family eating pheasant for the Sunday dinner, you would say that man was ready to do seven days' hard labour without the option of a fine." (Laughter and applause.) Pheasants, continued Mr Mar- tindale, were food for those who could afford to kill them, but they were going to see that the workers did not suffer through the sport of the idle rich: (Applause.)

===== 0002601_19140530_art0093.txt | included=true | included =====
"CASTLES IN_THE.AI.R," BUT NO LAND

FOR HOUSES.

In Warrington there was a housing problem, as in most other towns, and the reason was that they could not get land. They could build "castles in the air," but when they came to build houses in the town they needed land upon which to build them. (Hear, hear.) But when towns wanted land, the owner discovered that it had the value of a gold mine. That state of affairs would not exist long, for under the new scheme, if Town Councils required land, they would be empowered to buy it at a fair and reasonable price. The Liberal party were going to see to it that the landlord's pocket was not the first consideration, but the working man's health and the health of his family. He would like to see hundreds of the workers in Widnes, who were now living under unhealthy condi- tions, living out at Farnworth, where they would be healthier and happier. (Applause.) It was the intention of the Liberal party to build houses for the working classes in healthier dis- tricts, away from the smoky towns in which they lived. They wanted more open places for recreation grounds, and they were not going to pay through the nose for the land for that and other purposes, as they were doing now. (Ap- plause.)

WILL LANDLORDS EMIGRATE? After dealing with the Liberal proposals in regard to a more equitable rating system, Mr. Martindale said they were told that when they had brought land into use the landlords would emigrate. If they did that they would have to work a jolly sight harder than they had ever done before. (Laughter.) It was not the plan to deal unjustly with these men, give them a fair price for the land which wbLuiabtseto nraeelded for the general welfare of the people. Justice would be given to the industrious shop- keeper, who was now penalised for his industry, and everything would be so controlled as to make the people of the country happier, more contented and wealthier. (Applause.) —lf the people would give the Liberal party a further mandate they could do all those things and more besides, always having the ideal before them that the people constituted the State, and that the State's greatest benefit could be attained by looking after the welfare of the great masses of the people. (Loud applause.)

===== 0002601_19140530_art0114.txt | included=true | included =====
THE EXAMINER, SATURDAY, MAY 30, 1914.

THE PASSING WEEK.

THE FIRST PLANK.

The first plank in the Liberal programme now is the reform of our land system, and the battle for freedom, in its newest phase, was begun locally this week by means of a great land cam- paign, which has spread itself out over all the districts of the borough. The object of this -campaign is to give the people a fuller entry to their natural rights, than they have hitherto enjoyed. We may expect strenuous opposition. People who have been in a privileged position, and whose ancestors have bred into them the idea that theirs is the land and the fulness thereof, will not. readily succumb to the rightful claims of the other participmits. The claims of long descent have been stronger in this country than the claims of Nature, and " succession " has ranked higher than labour, worth and the claims of Nature. But those who maintain the wealth of a nation are demanding with increasiag strength that they shall enter upon the enjoyment of the fruits of their labour —they are growing tired-of the arrangement by which one man sows and another reaps, in so glaringly unequal a fashion as now obtains. During the debate on the Budget it was hinted that the rich should submit to -pay "ransom fontagxlyes rfeomr " berlain's militant Radical days, when he coin- iNnv htihceh f oisrmstor itnhiesicrepnrtivoiflegmesr,. acllvamord. pared the rich with the lilies of the field, which toil not, neither do they spin. Fortunately the possessors of wealth are coming to see the need for a more equitable arrangement. At the annual meeting of the Lancaster Liberal Association on Saturday Sir Norval Helme, M.P., said he had asked Lord Ashton how he liked the Budget, and he replied: "It hits me hard, but the principle of taxing the well-to-do in order to save the pockets of the poor is very just and proper." To do this "very just and proper" thing by the poor is the task the Liberal party has set itself to perform.

===== 0002601_19140530_art0115.txt | included=true | included =====
BOW IT IS TO BE DONE.

The set of speeches Which have been deliv- ered this week, as reported in "The Examiner" to-day, cover the grounds of the proposed reforms with tolerable completeness. Briefly, It is that land will be made to contribute a fairer share towards the happiness of all classes; rents are to be lowered and wages in- creased. For the benefit of those who meet such a programme with the customary Conser- vative negation and say that it cannot be done, it may be well to draw attention to what has actually been done, not in the old reforming days, but in these later days that are fresh in all our memories. Ten years ago the House of Lords was practically supreme in this Country; it had the privileges politically such as many of its members now enjoy economic- ally. Their political power has gone and heredity counts for little. Ten years ago the Congervatives pretended that they would like to give old-age pensions, but it was impossible to find the necessary money. The money has been found by Mr. Lloyd George. It was thought to be impossible to set up a national system of insurance against sickness and unem- ployment, but both are accomplished facts. The minimum wage has actually been applied to some industries, and we take it that what has been successfully applied may be extended.

===== 0002601_19140530_art0116.txt | included=true | included =====
THE TWO METHODS.

There are rival schemes for dealing with the condition of the people problem The Tory scheme is to raise wages by means of Tariff Reform. That is, they will put on taxes that will administer an industrial " shock " as well as increase prices. Both these processes re- strict demand, and setting up a condition of less work would be a queer way to raise wages. Profits might be raised by the making of rings, but profits go to the masters, not the men. A similar process of reasoning may be applied to the housing problem, which Councillor Broad- hurst, by asking a question threw into a very clear light, lie asked whether Liberals had not opposed the Conservative Honsing Bill. Of course, they did. The Bill provided that houses would be partly paid for out of the rates and taxes. As in the case of Tariff Reform the masters would benefit, so in the case of State aided housing the landlords—not for the first time—would get a dole at the public expense. The tenants are not to be pauperised in this way. Land and rating will be so treated as to bring rents within the comfortable reach of the workers, who will be further aided by the regularisation of wages in the lower grades. It is a great policy with fine ideals, the uplifting of humanity as a whole—and we hope our Conservative working men will see the advisa- bility of joining forces to help it along. It is often said that certain policies should be car- ried through independently of party politics. Here is one that has the greatest claim of any cause upon the whole people—a practical effort for their own uplifting.

===== 0002601_19140530_art0117.txt | included=true | included =====

WESLEY AND ENGLAND AND IRELAND.

the following significant which we publish in another column, appear In the quotations from Wesley's Journal, passages I procured a genuine 'account of the great Irish massacre in 1641. Surely never was there such a transaction before from the beginning of : the world! More than 200,000 men, women 1 and children butchered within a few months in cold blood, and with such circumstances of cruelty as make one's blood run cold! It is well if God has not a controversy with the nation, on this very account, to this day. I stayed at home and spple to all that came; I but I found scarce any Irish among them. At least ninety-nine in a hundred of the native Irish remain in the religion of their forefathers. The Protestants, whether in Dublin or else- where, are almost all transplanted lately from England. For is it any wonder that those who are born Papists generally live and die such, when the Protestants can find no better ways to convert them than penal laws and Acts of Par- liament? The nation certainly does appear to have suffered somewhat for the transgressions of the past.

EVERYWHERE,

"Queen Victoria —," the Guardians were startled to learn, was now under their charge.

Humour of the Country. "My dog took first prize at the cat show." "How was that?" "He took the cat."

Always in Bed. " I'd like to be that 'ere flower," quoth a Weary Willie to his pal as they passed a War- rington garden. " Why?" " 'Cos I'd always be in a bed." Looking Backwards.

"Mr:— was stipendiary," said an evening paper circulating in Warrington, "1879-1819." This was reversing the order of nature with a vengeance. Like a Leech. i

"I tipped Miss Leitch all through the year as the future lady champion golfer," said a Warrington golfer. He, in fact, stuck to his opinion like a leech.

" Breeds Fever."

Superstition dies hard. There is a genial Warrington dame who will not permit a bit of hawthorn blossom to be brought into her house, as she says it breeds fever."

===== 0002601_19140530_art0120.txt | included=true | included =====
In Proper Style.

The Rev. A. M. Mitchell suggests that the Guardians should ride to Padgate Homes "in proper style." We suggest a coach and four, with a red-coated guard tooting his post horn. That Is It.

" Snowdon, the mountain in Wales," wrote a Warrington school boy in an essay, "is so named because the snow never gets done there." Verily we live in an age of discovery. Pause or Paws._

Speaking at the Handwork Association meet- ing in Warrington on Saturday on the value of using the hands, Prof. Green said the time had come for pause (paws). He wondered why the audience laughed. The Mayor's Joke.

The Mayor wishes handwork had been taught when he was at school. Then he could have done his carpenter's work at the school bench, instead of damaging the sofa and chairs at home.

===== 0002601_19140530_art0121.txt | included=true | included =====
Heard on the Tram.

" Why," queried a Warringtonian on the upper deck of the tram, "is a bird the greediest creature?" The one questioned failed to reply, and came the answer: "Because it takes a peck at a time." Then followed deadly silence.

Hunger Belts. A lecturer at the Handwork Association meet- ing told how savages tightened their waistbelts when hungry. Surveying the sumptuous tea provided by the Mayor for the delegates, a wag remarked: " Unloosen your hunger belts." The "savages" did and enjoyed the tea. Suffragettes Warned.

In a Warrington office the young "knute" have arranged a gallery of pictures clipped from magazines, and they have finished all with the topical notice: "Suffragettes are strictly prohibited from entering this art gallery—By order." Sarcastic, isn't it?

Railwayman's Yarn. A Warrington Great Central Railway man says that during a flood in the Eastern Counties an official wired along the line, "Send full par- ticulars of the flood." A wearied hut witty stationmaster replied: " See Genesis."

" K in-ee-matograph." Apropos of Mr. Kingdon's pronunciation of the word cinematograph as above at the Educa- tion Committee meeting, a picture-house man- ager was asked whether it should be pronounced " Kinematograph "or " Cinematograph." "I always pronounce it bioscope," he replied. "It's easiest and it's the proper name." Cricket Fever.

It is reported that the cricket fever is so bad amongst some Warringtonians that they cannot think or speak of anything else. 'Tis said one dentist, thinking of the willow and the ball, put up the notice, "Stumps drawn at 8 o'clock."

Phew, the Heat!

A native home for a holiday after a long sojourn in the tropics complained of the pres- ent heat spell in Warrington. "But you should be able to stand heat," said his friend. " That's it. I can stand being roasted, but I cannot stand being stewed as well."

===== 0002601_19140530_art0122.txt | included=true | included =====
Pioneer gutter.

A Western paper says dealers in butter classify it as wool-grease, cart-grease, soap- grease, variegated, tesellated, cow-grease, board- ing-house, breakfast, inferior tub, common tub, medium roll, good roll, and gilt-edge roll. The terms are strictly technical.

"A Plate Layer." Two young sparks met in a local hostelry and got talking. "What are you?" asked one. "A plate layer," came the answer. "Do you work on the C.L.C. or the L. and N.-W.?" "Neither. I work in hotel." "Then you're not a plate layer!" "Ain't I? Isn't a waiter a plate layer?"

Still Slaughtering. A country doctor discharged his coachman on account of his unsteady habits. The coachman took service with the village butcher, and one day when driving a number of beasts to the slaughter-house he met his former employer. " Well, Tom," said the doctor, patronisingly, "you are in a .different sort of employment now?"

"Not at all, sir," said Tom, who bore his former employer a grudge. "Not at all; I'm still in the slaughtering business."

WIDNES N.U. CLUB. Signing-on Agreements.

Important business was transacted at the an- nual meeting of the Widnes Football Club, held on Monday night. Mr. J. H. Smith, in present- ing the balance-sheet, said they could congratu- late themselves on a very successful season and a profit of £3OO. Alluding to the amount paid to players (1677), he said only one man, G. Aspey, had played in every match, and he had received the largest amount ever paid by the Widnes Club to a player. A resolution which would have serious import for the Widnes Club would be presented at the annual meeting of the N.U. a fortnight hence. It provided that players should be paid a sum to be mentioned on the signing-on agreement and no more. It had been the custom of the Widnes Club to sign their players on for a nominal sum, the players having confidence to leave the amount of their remuneration in the hands of the committee, who paid them according to the position of the season's finances.

Mr. F. Smith was re-elected secretary, Mr. J. Barber financial secretary, and Mr. J. H. Smith hon. treasurer. There were 19 nominations for nine places on the committee, and the following were elected: Mess. G. Aspey, A. Chalmers, T. Gregory, R. Jones. W. Lawrenceson, T. Min- ton, T. Murphy, R. Wailing, and J. Younge.

A GREAT WEEK FOR LIBERALISM.

The House of Commons on Monday read the Home Rule Bill foi' the ninth time during the lifetime of the present Parliament ; that is, three times the measure has run its full course in three successive sessions, and so becomes law, automatically, as it has out- lived the power for mischief of the cur- tailed veto of the House of Lords. Those who have thought the PREMIER weak in the length he has gone along the road of conciliation would do well to note that his original Bill will go on the Statute Book, and that he • has thrown the onus upon the Lords of receiving, or leaving, as the case may be, the amendments that may be intro- dueed by way of an Amending Bill. Such a case of sweet reasonableness, accompanied by firmness of purpose, has seldom been seen in British poli- tics; and Mr. ASQUITH'S reputation as the Statesman who gave freedom to the Irish people will be enhanced by the manner in which he achieved his object. The Opposition have been virtuously indignant at the determina- tion of the Government to pass the Bill. They have stood aghast at the sight of a Liberal measure being passed over the 'heads of the Lords, the ancient obstacle that the Parliament Act has Partially cleared out of the way. Really it is the Liberals who ought to be indig- nant at the fact that in free England, in the enlightened Twentieth Century, they, as a party, have to wait three years before they can get a measure through, and not only so, have to run the gauntlet for those three years of threats of civil war, of riotous conduct in the Rouse of Commons, and of tam- pering With the Army, all nicely cal- culated to take the desired effect before the three years are up. If this Bill had been introduced by a Liberal realseimTsbc eeaehngrn il 1 /ft hg h BillnTory Government, and carried by a majority of one in the House of Com- mons, it would have taken its place on the Statute Book two years ago. Mem- bers who sat in the Parliament which ended in 1905 have clearly and vividly engraved on their memory cases in which legislation, novel, never sub- mitted to the electorate, condemned by them on the first occasion on which they had an opportunity to pronounce it, passed through the House of Com- monsthe in a single session, and, with the lo.aLtnodrd.tsh,elattisTonothrcyee minority, which to-day suffers from a le parties. resultagre eT of This Government will behobpreougthhat in a t the s the betweenpreliminary pi e s th that tthiena n conversationseces anddifferent- sarily long interval further efforts will be made to arrive at such an agreement as can be translated into the terms of the measure. The second reading of Amending en law majority the Home Rule Bill in the Lords will not be set down for any date before June 22nd. On that date, then, the House of Lords will be definitely at grips with a Bill which they no longer have the power to kill, nor even to delay beyond a few days or weeks. What the Lords do, although they have a opportunity in the cause of pea not matter very much. ucnei, qudpoes What Liberals feel is that this has been a great week for them. They have re- deemed a thirty-year-old pledge, hon- oured GLADSTONE'S message of hope to Ireland. and vindicated their claim to be masters in their own house. What has been done cannot be undone. In the eloquent words of Mr. REDMOND, who sought to make peace with his opponents. "the Union of Pitt and Castlereagh can never be again set on its feet, and the assembling of an Irish Parliament. under the provisions of the Home Rule Bill, is as certain as the rising of to-morrow's sun."

SUNDAY CLOSING. Mr. Harold Smith, M.P., and the Temperance League. THE MEMBER'S GIBES

To the Editor of "The Examiner."

Sir,—Enclosed You will find a copy of corre- spondence which has passed between Mr. H. Smith, M.P., and myself, and I should be pleased if you published same.—Yours truly, BENJ. T. PARKES, Hon. Secretary Warrington and District Citizens' Temperance League.

===== 0002601_19140530_art0129.txt | included=true | included =====
[COPY.]

4, Elm Court, Temple, E.C., May 21. Mr. Benjamin T. Parkes. Dear Sir,—l have to thank you for your letter .of the 14th. I observe the only reference you make to the enormous growth of illicit drinking dens in areas where Sunday closing is in force is a totally incomprehensible suggestion that "The Trade" is responsible for these illicit methods. This, of course, requires no answer, except a respectful but firm contradiction. I pointed out in my letter that, comparing the Sunday closing areas with the non. Sunday closing areas, the reductioli in the number of offences is greater in tho latter than in the former. I asked you tu compare Monmouthshire with Glamorganshire, Scotland with England, Irish towns with Irish rural areas. The only attempt to answer these unanswerable facts is a series of figures showing that since the year 1906, when the hours of opening in the Irish cities were reduced on Sunday, there has been a diminution in offences. You..omit to tell us, however, that this diminution started several years before 1906, thereby proving that it is attributable to some cause other than the reduction of hours in 1906.

I notice that "all temperance organisations are agreed that clubs ought to be subject to the same regulations as public-houses." I can only reply by asking when did the Temperance party ever no anything to bring this reform about? They have never ceased to attack the publican and they have never started to sup- press the drinking club. This reform has been left to myself and some colleagues in the House of Commons, and we have received no assistance from the Government or the Temperance party. When members of the United Kingdom Alliance in the House of Commons succeed in the ballot for Private Bills they produce a Bill for closing public-houses—they content themselves by a sham lip service to the necessity for dealing with the drinking club. I am afraid that you or I will never convert each other; each must rely upon public opinion. For myself, I gladly leave my case in the hands of this tribunal, and I trust that, in the mean- time, we can each respect the opinion of the other, and I thank you for this opportunity of exchanging views.—Yours very truly, HAROLD SMITH.

DRINK THE ONLY CAUSE OF DRUNKENNESS. 8, Willis-street, Warrington, May 28th, 1914. Dear Sir,—ln reply to your letter of 21st, I do not think my suggestion is " totally incompre- hensible," because if " The Trade" would not supply the illicit drinking dens, how could they be carried on?

As we were dealing with Sunday limitation of hours, the figures quoted were quite in order, and all that were necessary, but I have exam- ined the figures for years previous to 1906. True the diminution had set in, but the further restrictio-i increased the rate, as we may see from the following figures, which are the totals for the five cities :- 1897-98. 1899-10. 1902-03.

1907.

1910 1912.

2,060. 1,972. 1,-706.- 1,017. 616. 589. You would claim to be a pioneer in relation to the reform of the club, but I must remind you that before you were elected to Parliament the Temperance Bill of 1908, which contained clauses relative to clubs, passed the House of Commons by a substantial majority, and the Sobtch Temperance Act, the third reading of which I am informed you voted against, con- tains clauses which will make it much more difficult to establish a purely drinking club, and also limits the hours of sale in clubs in Scot- land. As far as members of the U.K.A. are concerned, I am sure they are quite capable of using private members' days to the best advan- tage. _ _

have left one charge you make, namely, "the Temperance party have never ceased to attack the publican." We do not attack the publican-- he is, in many cases, to be pitied. We attack the Alcoholic Drink, which is a destroyer of manhood and womanhood, a curse to the chil- dren who are helpless. Having had a very close contact with the effect of drink I know of some of its evil. In the "Workers' Onward," we have the following quotation from F. E. Smith, K.C., M.P. : "having had no small experience of Criminal Courts, he was satisfied of the essen- tial truth that drink was responsible for a very large proportion of the crime statistics of this country." As I saw a copy of your letter in the "Guardian," I will forward to them a copy of this, and will ask "The Examiner" to publish both, as I consider the columns of the latter equally important for "matters of public interest" to the columns of the former. As the opinion of the ,people must always eventually settle these matters, I will join with you in your closing words respecting each other's Opinion. but hoping that you may some day be i converted and help us n our efforts.—Yours truly, To 11. Smith, Esq., M.P.

BENJ. T. BARREN.

" SANITAS."

A Dividend of Seven and a Half Per Cent.

At the annual general meeting of the " Sanitas" Company, held at their Limehouse (London) factory, Mr. C. T. Kingzett, • F.1.C., F.C.S., the chairman, presiding, the usual divi- dend of seven and a half per cent. (which has been for many years) was again of £3,000 being placed to declared,en paid the sum regularlyum reserve account, £l,OOO to contingency account, and £2,756 13s. 4d. carried forward. The Chairman directed attention to the very satisfactory increase in sales of the company's manufactures. The value of " Sanitas Fluid" for preventing oral sepsis by its regular use as a wash for the mouth and teeth was being more and more endorsed by the medical profession and the public at large. In this respect it ranks highest of all available antiseptics, in addition to which its unique and non-poisonous character renders it the only disinfectant suitable for general toilet and household employment. With respect to " Sanitas Powder," the pub- licity that has been given to its value in the garden as a preventive and protective against the ravages of slugs, snails, insects and other soil pests, together with its continued popular- ity as a disinfectant powder for general use, has resulted in a largely increased consumption. The importance of pure air was also remarked upon, and reference was made to the report recently issued by Dr. F. E. Scrase (Medical Officer of Health for Hampstead), pointing out the importance of daily subjecting picture palaces to the sterilising effect of sunlight. As this, however, is not always possible, it was pointed out by Mr. Kingzett that the same or better result can be obtained by spraying the air of such places with " San4as Fluid," bearing in mind that—as the " LaTfcet " pointed out some years since—" there is no factor more favourable to the successful invasion of the human body by disease-producing organisms than when the air is rendered stale, warm and musty."

Postmaster Promoted.

Mr. J. Wyard, postmaster of Warrington, has been promoted to the position of postmaster of Middlesbrough.

Weather for the Holidays.

_ Last night the weather prospects improved, and with a steady and somewhat high barometer there were signs of pleasant weather for the holiday-makers.

Rush to the Seaside. Yesterday a considerable number of Warring- ton people travelled to the seaside, but the rail- way officials did not expect the rush to begin until to-day. Bookings for Ireland, the Isle of Man, &c., were reported up to the average.

" A.8.C." Railway Guide. The " A.8.C." Guide for June, published to- day, contains the train services remaining in force until July 11. On the following day, July 12, the extended summer services will begin. The July issue of the Guide, showing all addi- tions and correctidns, will be published Thurs- day, July 9.

Territorial Officer Honoured.

= Among a number of Territorial officers upon whom the King has conferred the Territorial decoration is Lieutenant-Colonel Brereton Fair- dough, commanding 4th Battalion Prince of Wales' Volunteers (South Lancashire Regi- ment), of Warrington. The Colonel is an Hon. Lieutenant in the Army.

===== 0002601_19140530_art0138.txt | included=true | included =====
The Missionary Exhibition.

As a thanksgiving for the success which at- tended the recent great Missionary Exhibition in Warrington, a special service was held in the Parish Church on Saturday afternoon, when an address was given by the Rev. E. H. Day, of Liverpool, organising secretary of the S.P.G. and a former missionary in Burma. Thd -Rector conducted the service.

Meat and Food Inspectors' Examination Of the two Warringtonians who passed the above examination at Liverpool, Mr. Monks is in office as a Sanitary Inspector at Warrington; Mr. Lloyd is employed as a plumber by Messrs. Thomas Allen and Sons. Both these students are fully qualified as Sanitary Inspectors, and have the certificate of the Royal Sanitary Institute.

===== 0002601_19140530_art0139.txt | included=true | included =====
Fire at*Naylor's Works.

Shortly before four o'clock on Thursday after- noon the Fire Brigade received a call to Howley Timber Yard, in the occupation of Messrs. R. A. Naylor. An electric box had fused in the joinery shop and set fire to some wood. A few buckets of water and sand were thrown on the flames by the workmen. The motor fire engine, in charge of Superintendent Turner, arrived on the scene in a few minutes after receipt of the call, but there was no need for the services of the Brigade.

===== 0002601_19140530_art0140.txt | included=true | included =====
Brabazon Society Sale.

The members of the Brabazon Employment Society were the promoters of a sale of articles of sewing, NV ood-ca Tv n g and rugs, made by the inmates of the Workhouse. The sale was held on Thursday in the gardens at "The Hollies," Latchford, the residence of Mrs. Broadbent, and Miss Broadbent, the local secretary of the society. A large number were present, and the games and competitions were attended with con- siderable success. The proceeds of the sale total about £22.

===== 0002601_19140530_art0141.txt | included=true | included =====
Cheshire Lines Staff Trip.

The Cheshire Lines stores office staff held their fourth annual picnic on Saturday. The party, numbering 26, travelled from Bank Quay to Windermere, and, on arrival, walked to Bow- ness, where a steamer took them to Ambieside. Dinner and tea was partaken of at the White Lion Hotel, Ambleside. A drive to Rydal and Grasmere, arranged for the afternoon, proved a success, the scenery round this part of the Lake District being very beautiful. The party left Windermere by the nine o'clock train. Warrington was reached at 11.45 p.m., after spending a most enjoyable time. The arrange- ments were in the hands of Mr. W. H. Sanders.

===== 0002601_19140530_art0142.txt | included=true | included =====
Increase for Tramway Men.

It is understood that the Electricity and Tram- ways Committee, at a meeting on Tuesday even- ing, agreed to an increase of a halfpenny per hour on the wages of motormen, conductors, cleaners, and " handy " men employed by the department. The committee recently decided to recommend an increase of one farthing per hour to compensate the men for the alteration in the Sunday service. This, however, was not considered adequate by the employes and some members of the Council, and at the last meet- ing of the Council the matter was withdrawn, so that a deputation representing the men could wait upon the committee. The deputation met the committee on Tuesday, when they were informed that the increase of a halfpenny per hour would be granted.

Boy's Remarkable Recovery. A remarkable recovery is reported in connec- tion with a severe accident which befel Harry Clay (10), only son of Mr. Clay, of the Liberal Club. On Saturday morning the boy was found unconscious at the foot of the stair leading to the first floor of the club, and had, apparently, fallen down the steps. He was taken to the Infirmary, where it was thought he would not recover, as he had sustained severe concussion of the brain. He lay unconscious until Tuesday, when he recovered and spoke. Since then he has made slow, but remarkable progress. Mr. and Mrs. Clay wish to express, through "The Examiner," their thanks for the sympathy they received from all parts of the district, as it is impossible to reply individually to the numerous inquiries.

===== 0002601_19140530_art0143.txt | included=true | included =====
Scotch Ladles' Bowling Main and Drive

The members of the Warrington Caledonian Association, with their wives and their friends, had their annual ladies' main at the Hermit Hotel, Newton-le-Willows, on Saturday. A bowling drive was played, which, after some keenly.contested games, resulted in Mrs. W. Murray being placed first and Mrs. W. Stirling second. The company then adjourned for tea, which was again up to Mrs. Copple's high stand- ard of catering. After tea bowling was resumed for the ladies' consolation prizes. Here some fine games were witnessed and the enthusiasm of the spectators was great. Result: 1, Mrs. T. Tennant; 2, Mrs. G. Marshall; 3, Mrs. F. Love; 4, Mrs. J. Robertson. Mr. W. Murray acted as M.C. for the bowling drive and referee in the bowling match, to the satisfaction of all the ladies.

As is usual at these outings, an impromptu concert was held in the hotel. Mr. Murray occupied the chair. Songs were given by Messrs. G. Valiance, J. Robertson, J. Aitken, A. Love, W. Stirling, W. Murray, and F. Love, and the remarks made by Mr. Skilling were greatly appreciated. On the proposition of Mr. Murray a vote of thanks was accorded the Secretary for the carrying out of the programme; also to Mrs. J. Tennant for playing the accompaniments to the singers.

===== 0002601_19140530_art0144.txt | included=true | included =====
Training College Re-union.

Former students of the Training College, members of St. Katharine's Guild, assembled under the old portals on Saturday for the annual re-union. Memories were rekindled by the sight of "old" familiar faces, and happen- ings of years ago were enacted again—in thought. For various reasons there were not so many present as usual, although about 350 attended. The Principal (the Rev. Canon Morley Stev- enson), in extending a welcome, gave an account ' of various events, of interest to them as former students, which had occurred during the year. Reference was made to the progress of the Mil- lion Pennies Fund and the Principal proposed that various objects should be aided by the Guild Fund; a course which was unanimously agreed to. The Million Pennies Fund received a flip from the sale of sweets, flowers, &c., and from the entrance money taken for the perform- ance, given by 30 second-year students, entitled "Mrs. Jarley's Famous Waxworks." More than usual interest was taken in a number of beautifully attired dolls, whose dresses had been prepared by the second-year students, as part of their needlework examination. A Past v. Present Students' tennis match had to be abandoned on account of the bad weather.

===== 0002601_19140530_art0145.txt | included=true | included =====
L.G.O. Rifle Club.

There is on view in the window of Kellett's, Tailors, Bridge-street, a very pretty solid silver challenge shield of uncommon design, given by the Mayor to the Local Government Officers' Rifle Club. It has been supplied by Mr. F. Hallett, the silversmith, Bridge-street.

The Leather Trade. Trade during the week (says "The Leather World ") appears to have been on a fairly high level, and all spoken to report a much livelier tone about the inquiry, which is all the more promising, as it springs up just on the eve of the Whitsun holidays, when usually business begins to slacken off. As trade has been largely confined to prompt sales, output has been rather heavy, and at the moment tanners' stocks are decidedly small in everything other than stout bends.

Land Campaign Meetings. The land campaign under the auspices of the local Liberal Association will be continued as follows :—To-night, on land at bottom of Cath- erine-street; Tuesday, corner of Knutsford-road and Thelwall-lane; Wednesday, Orford Village; Thursday, Corporation-street; Thursday after- noon, ladies' meeting, Co-operative Hall; Friday, Dalton-Bank. All the night meetings start at Fight o'clock. The report of last night's meet- ing will appear in next Saturday's "Examiner," whilst a full report of this week's meetings will be found on Page 5.

Town Hall Scheme Dropped. The General Purposes Committee, on which every member of the Warrington Town Council serves, considered the scheme for the provision of new municipal offices, at a cost of £50,000, on -Monday. evening. It was proposed by the special committee appointed to deal with the question of extending the Town Hall that that building should not be altered, but that a block of offices which would accommodate all the Cor- poration departments should be erected on the large plot of land between the east drive and Legh-street. The General Purposes Committee were recommended to sanction the scheme and to authorise the appointment of an assessor and the preparation of conditions for the obtaining of competitive designs. After considerable dis- cussion both upon these proposals and the alter- native of enlarging the Town Hall, it was decided that the time is inopportune to proceed with the question at all. Some members pointed out that questions such as that of sew- erage were more important, and would doubtless demand attention at an early date.

Sunday School Union: Annual' Ramble 'The annual Spring meeting and ramble of the Sunday School Union took place on Saturday. Starting from Latchford, the members enjoyed a walk to Thelwall, the grounds of Mr. Jones- Parry being visited. A lecture on "Memories of Thelwall and Latchford" was given by Mr. Arthur Mounfield, which was much enjoyed. Tea was served in the Independent Methodist Schoolroom. At the public meeting which fol- lowed, the Rev. W. Reid, president of the Union, presided. An address was given by Miss Cros- bie, of Liverpool, which embodied the results of a long and wide experience in Sunday school work, and Mr. Ernest Mellor reported upon a visit to the Swanwick Sunday School Conven- tion. The meeting was most successful and was thoroughly enjoyed and well attended. Mr. Mounfield's lecture dealt with the doings of the Romans, Saxons and Normans, in the neighbourhood of Latchford, and included an account of the ancient fords and bridges across the Mersey. It is hoped that the entire lecture may be printed in "The Examiner" in the near future.

Empire Day in Warrington. On account of Empire Day falling on Sunday, this year's celebrations were divided, so far as the schools were concerned. The majority of them held their special features on Monday, although, as was announced in the last issue of "The Examiner," the Secondary and Fair- field Schools, both made Friday of last week their joy day. On the tops of the Municipal offices, business premises and works chimneys, flags fluttered, waving a welcome to the day, whilst the scholars in their various schools en- thusiastically sang patriotic songs and listened to the lessons on the subject. In the afternoon thousands of children made their way to the Warrington Football Club's ground for the ath- letic festival. The boys at the Boteler Gram- mar School were interested in an address from Major Green on the meaning of Empire Day, and following cheers for the King and the visit- ors, the school was closed for the day. On Tuesday evening the children from the Fair- field School successfully repeated their pro- gramme before an admiring audience of parents and friends.

RICHMOND'S AND THEIR MEN.

Good Feeling That Exists Displayed at Football Dinner. THE SEASON'S SUCCESSES.

High tribute was paid to the fine spirit which exists between the firm and its workmen at a dinner at the Lion Hotel, given to commemorate the success of the Richmond Gas Stove Com- pany's Football Club in the recent Works Com- petition.

===== 0002601_19140530_art0147.txt | included=true | included =====
LOYALTY AND DEVOTION TO GOOD

EMPLOYERS.

Mr. J. R. BURNETT, works manager, who pre- Director (Mr. J. A. Ransome), Pro sided, and, in the absence of the Managing toast of " The Richmond Gas Stove and Meter Company, Ltd.," said: No doubt the minds of posed the many of you here will go back to occasions on which this toast has been honoured with enthu- siasm before my appearance on the scene, so whilst I am proud of being in the position to make such a popular and worthy proposal, I must ask you to excuse any falling off in the ' framing of the words. Since I came amongst you it has always been a pleasure to me to notice the fine spirit of loyalty and devotion to the service of the company, which animates all the old hands, and which, by inevitably influ- encing the new blood introduced amongst us from time to time, makes directly for the con- tinuous prosperity of the company. I like to feel that we are workers, real pushers, not hangers-on, or mere wage-lifters. (Hear, hear.) On the other hand, we have to admit that we have a good company to serve, one that is splendidly organised and controlled, and always deals with the workers in a just and generous spirit. (Hear, hear.) With a company of this kind, backed up as it is by the brains and brawn of the workers, it is difficult to see how it can do otherwise than maintain and advance the proud position it has already won. (Applause.) The toast was enthusiastically drunk.

PROPOSED ATHLETIC CLUB. The next toast, "The Richmond Gas Stove and Meter Company, Ltd., Football Clubs," was also in the able hands of Mr. BURNETT, who intimated that he would like to see a good all- round Athletic Club in connection with the works, a club able to carry all before it. He would , support any effort in this direction, and he could also promise the support of the directors to any well-organised scheme. Mr. T. CoNNou suitably responded on behalf of the players. "The Health of the Committee and Officials of the Football ,Teams" was proposed by Mr. BURNETT, special reference being made to their able secretary, Mr. C. Cox. The toast was well received, and responded to by Mr. P. GAMMON, supported by Mr. MELTON. Mr. BURNETT proposed a vote of thanks to the artistes and friends who had provided such an excellent programme, and to Mr. W. Pemberton for presiding at the piano. Mr. P. °ARLEY (assistant works manager) sup- porting, suggested the formation of a committee for next season and that they should get to work right away, so that they might meet again 12 months hence as winners of the "double event." He also supplemented the remarks of Mr. Bur- nett regarding the Athletic Club, the formation of which he would welcome and support. Mr. GALLIMORE responded to the vote of thanks, Mr. C. Cox supporting. PRESENTATIONS. During the evening an excellent musical pro- gramme was submitted, in the early part of which a pleasing presentation was made by 'Mr. J. Adkiss on behalf of the members of the two teams to their Honorary Secretary (Mr. C. Cox). Mr. Cox, in accepting a smoker's cabinet, ex- pressed himself as fully satisfied with the manner in which the teams had held together, and almost won the "double event." Other presentations were made to the reserve men, Mr. R. Taylor receiving a medal, and Mr. J. Brown a watch.

PUBLIC AMUSEMENTS.

ROYAL COURT THEATRE. "An Indian Girl's Devotion."

Large audiences are attracted to the Royal

Court Theatre this week, where a stirring romance of the West is presented in "An Indian Girl's Devotion." It is a story of love and adventure, where, above all, the strife, enmity, jealousy and cunning, the power of whole-hearted, sympathetic devotion prevails. There are many exciting incidents and a splen- did appropriate climax is witnessed in "Across the Rockies," when the hero, fastened to a horse, is dashing to destruction, when the Indian girl mounts another horse and effects a sensational rescue. Messrs. Glenville and Osmond's Co. give an admirable presentation of the play, Mr. Reg. T. Fox infusing plenty of dash and vigour into the part of Broncho Jack. Mr. Cecil E. Melton is also successful as the •unscrupulous rival. Miss Lillian Malvern suc- cessfully elicits the sympathies of the audience, as also does Miss Margaret Hobart as Bessie Hope. A rare fund of comedy is provided by Mr. Fred Osmond as Who Ray, the faithful negro.

MR. JOHN GLENMINTNING. As Father Kelly, in "The Rosary."

===== 0002601_19140530_art0150.txt | included=true | included =====
has made a tremendous hit in America, where it has been toured by six companies during the last four years, and although it has caused a great amount of controversy in every town visited, it has invariably proved a success, as repeated visits to the different towns proved. It is one of the very few plays of a relizions subject which does not hurt the susceptibilities of the audience, and it readily appeals to both Roman Catholics and Protestants alike. It has been Mr. Glendinning's great pleasure to notice that every audience numbers all classes and denominations, who have been unanimous

===== 0002601_19140530_art0151.txt | included=true | included =====
Scene between Skeeter and Lesura in "The Rosary."

===== 0002601_19140530_art0152.txt | included=true | included =====
in their approval. This speaks volumes for the author's construction of the play, as it is one of the most difficult problems to steer clear of offending the religious feelings of a mixed assembly. Altogether "The Rosary" is a most interest- ing play, written in a vivid style and enlivened by some truly delightful comedy, and should prove a great attraction on Whit-Monday at the Royal Court Theatre.

===== 0002601_19140530_art0153.txt | included=true | included =====
" BLUEBOTTLES " AT HIPPODROME.

"Bluebottles" is the somewhat striking title of a dramatic comedy sketch, presented so ably by Mr. Walter Bird's West End Company. There is a decidedly novel touch about the pro- duction, and as the comedy develops the audi- ence cannot suppress their laughter, and it develops into one huge outburst at the finish. Two ladies and a gentleman constitute the trick comedy Peostocflaring tricks in tball match is most us impersonations of Laa cyclists,mo ctulte osvi,ne clever whoimloa Innanpn ehedirri sf 00mrtmritao‘ marvellous relfolooimoi men who have helped to make the nation, Ranschle is decidedly popular. Barnard and Bunny are extremely clever comedians, their imitations of the cat causing much laughter. As instrumentalists the Two Canadian Ladies are past masters in their art. Oakes Duo appear with success in "Making a Man of Her." Florrie Elliston is a popular vocalist, and Lily Cole also scores a success as an illustrator of popular songs.

FIRST TURNS NEXT WEEK. TIIE PICTURE HOUSE.

This popular place of amusement is now under the management of Mr. G. D. Sinclair, who has, we understand, had a vast experience in such a capacity all over England and abroad. He assures patrons that only the best of pictures will be shown, and he has booked some big films. Next week "The Betraying Mark," in four parts, will be the principal .film for the first three nights, and for the remainder of the week "The Hidden Witness" will occupy premier position on a good bill.

"A Neglected Wife" will be the great attrac- tion at the Star Kinenia in the first part of next week. "The Cage" ought to appeal to the audi- ences on the last three days of the week.
