Annual Meeting of Members and Purchase of , the Ground.

WHY NO WARRINGTONIANS WENT TO AUSTRALIA.

Thera was a stirring debate over the report at the annual meeting of the Warrington N.U. Football Club in the Co-operative Hall on Thursday evening, Complaints were made at the Warrington players being ignored by the N.U. Committee in connection with the Australian tour, and questions were asked about what the club officials had done in the matter. One speaker, dealing with the proposal to foster schoolboy football, said he would rather see £l5O spent on that purpose than spent on committee men going butterfly-catching round the country and returning home with moths in their treacle cans. The Secretary, Mr. Bradshaw, effectively re- plied to the criticisms. Councillor Osborne was unanimously elected president, and out- lined the scheme for schoolboy footballers. Mr. Bradshaw was elected secretary. He got 354 votes against the 79 given for Mr. W. Mason.

Prior to the election of the new President, Mr. T. Locker, the retiring president, occupied the chair. He stated that would be the last meeting of his presidency, a position he had held for three years. He was pleased to retire under such happy conditions as at present pre- vailed, and he hoped the club would be still more successful in the coming season. He urged the members to assist the management in every way. He thanked the committee for the man- ner in which they had supported him. He was sorry to say that Mr. F. Barber would not be present, as he had met with a slight accident— sprained his leg playing golf, not football. (Laughter.) The report of the Secretary (which was pub- lished in "The Examiner" last week) was pre- sented. Mr. H. Rogers proposed and Mr. G. F. Foster seconded the adoption.

strike on the invitation of the committee. They were then in an unfortunate position. 'Although the committee were quite capable of dealing with the matter themselves, Mr. Houghton volunteered his services, and they were accepted. No expenses were paid, except Mr. Houghton's railway fare. (Hear, hear.) It was very im- portant that a settlement should have, been arrived at. If a complaint had gone forward from Hull Kingston Rovers, the matter would have been out of the hands of the Warrington Committee, and the players would have been dealt with by the N.U. Committee and sus- pended,, while the olub, would have been.. heavily fined.. * .

Got Out. Cheaply. Considering that they had- got out of such liabilities without costing the club a penny piece he thought_ they had-done remarkably well under the circumstances. (Loud applause.) It might have been an easy matter for the club to have been let in for X 150: or 1:200. Mr. Houghton promised the players that the strike would have no effect on the choice of the Selec- tion Committee of, the Northern Union._.

To show iieiniere—s-t the Warrington Commit- tee took in the team, added Mr. Bradshaw, and to show that the effects of the strike had been wiped out, the committee nominated four men for positions in the team for Australia. These names were sent and were brought forward in the usual manner, but the N.U. Committee decided that, under- the circurastances, they would not be considered. We pressed the claims of the four umeni.not only by letter, but through our represintative, Councillor Osborne, but without effect We could have done noth- ing more than we did:

===== 0002601_19140530_art0157.txt | included=true | included =====
THE CRITICISM.

Mr. W. Mason congratulated the secretary and committee on the report, and said it was a worthy successor to the reports that had gone before. There were many things in it he was glad to see; he was glad to see the services of the ambulance people recognised; glad to see that the services rendtred by the teachers were not overlooked, for the future of the club de- pended upon the present youngsters, and 'the teachers were leading the youngsters' interest in the proper direction. He was glad the com- mittee were endeavouring to produce a new rank of local players, and that an intermediate league was to be formed, so as to bridge the present gap between schoolboys and senior foot- ball.

===== 0002601_19140530_art0158.txt | included=true | included =====
Butterflies_ or Moths.

I would prefer, added Mr. Mason, to see our committee spend £l5O to £2OO in financing ten junior clubs—it can be done for that money— than I would see the money spent on committee men going through the country butterfly catch- ing and coming home with moths in their treacle cans. (Laughter.) Ther4 were some terms in the report he was sorry to see. He referred to the paragraph about the strike. He also drew attention to the paragraph which stated that the forwards were the mainstay of the team. When he read the two paragraphs together he found out the con- tradiction. As would be remembered the for- wards were the strike leaders, yet the strike did not affect them, for the report stated that all through the season they were the mainstay of the team. The report added that the players in the back division were very unsatisfactory, and he wanted to know what disciplinary measures the committee had taken to bring about a better state of affairs. He thought the failure in the second part of the season was not so much the fault of the team as the fault of the committee.

===== 0002601_19140530_art0159.txt | included=true | included =====
Needed Strengthening.

. ------ A passage stated that the team needed strengthening after the strike. If it needed strengthening then, it surely needed strengthen- ing before the strike as well. They met another , contradiction later when it was stated that the ' men did not play well after the strike, because of the lack of unanimity. In regard to the defeat by Barrow in the Lancashire Cup Com- petition, the committee believed that Barrow were undoubtedly the better side, and that they thoroughly deserved to win. Yet after the strike the Warrington men beat Barrow at home and on the Barrow ground. Then in the N.U. Cup, the men who lacked unanimity were praised for the fight they made against Batley. These paragraphs should never have been put into the report. There was a casual reference to the men who had got county honours. It would have cost only 21c1. more to have printed their names in the report. They had done scant justice to these players by not placing their names in the report. The Australian Tour.

There was no reference to what happened in connection with the Australian tour. They had two men with splendid claims for a position in that team, and a third with a very good claim. Clare and Cox were fit to go into any football society. (Applause.) With regard to Jolley, he thought his claim was not second to the best back in the kingdom. (Applause.) The members did not know how far the com- mittee supported the Warrington candidates. It would have been a proper thing to state in the report what steps were taken by the committee to support the claims of their own men, and to state whether the N.U. had entirely overlooked that claim.

"A SCANDALOUS SHAME." Mr. Madden asked if Mr. Houghton had said there would be no grievance over the strike, and it would not count with the N. 17. Committee in selecting the Australian team. If so, why were the Warrington men barred? He thought it was "a scandalous shame." (Hear, hear, and laughter.) Mr. J.W. Plinston thought the N.U. had cast "a terrible slight upon the club and players." He suggested that to keep in touch with the old school boys they should run a Works League. mi.. 3", P. Foster asked who invited Mr. Hough- ton to meet the players during the strike, who brought him, and whether his expenses were paid by the club? He thought it showed a great deal of weakness on the part of the coniinittee in having to bring in an outsider to settle the strike. (Hear, hear.) Mr. S. Wright said it had become recognised that if anyone wanted to get on the club man- agement they must first criticise the committee. (Laughter.) He congratulated the secretary on the excellency of the report, and he did not think it should be criticised as it had been. (Hear, hear.) THE SECRETARY'S REPLY. Pointed Answer to Criticism. Mr. T. P. Bradshaw said he could understand how people who for ten days sat in an armchair studying the report could find some little matter for criticism and upon which they could draft a speech. (Laughter.) It was far more difficult to perform the duties of secretary. They had been congratulated on one hand and Pulled to pieces immediately after. Mr. Mason said he was glad the work done by the masters in War- rington for schoolboy football had been recog- nised. These teachers had been on the field every Saturday, but Mr. Mason had taken no interest in the Schoolboys' League. (Hear, hear.) The committee gave credit to the teachers who worked the competition, and at the sports festival last Monday every headmaster was there except Mr. Mason. Mr. Mason: Is this in order? The Chairman: You brought it up on the report. (A Voice: "Hear, hear," and "Give it him.") Order, gentlemen, this is not a Town Council meeting. (Laughter.) The criticism can be met fairly and squarely by the com- mittee. . _ . _

Divided Backs. Mr. Bradshaw, continuing, referred to the question of the back division. The forwards during the strike came out in a body, but the back division were divided, some sticking in and some coming out, with the result that the men who stuck in were not treated by the others as they should have been. In regard to the defeat by Barrow, the fault was that the team went on the field over-confident, and Barrow took advantage of the chance. It was true Mr. Houghton came during the

mediate league. In 1912 Mr. Plinston brought a proposition that a third team be formed, but he suggested that the better plan would be to form an intermediate league for youths of 15 to 17 years. Now his opinion was that a league for boys between 14 and 16 would be better, and when it was made a success form another for youths from 16 to 20. Everybody recognised how the boys had taken to the game, and the Warrington scholars were considered the best in S.-W• Lancashire. Some of the ideas of the schoolboys could well be copied by their seniors. (Hear, hear.) They knew how to side-step and swerve, and went about the game in a business- like manner. (Applause.)

At Present Neglected. , The lads when they left school were at pres- ent neglected. When they had been in a factory for two or three years and tried the game they had practically to re-learn the play. The in- tention was to get the 14-16 old league working, and then form another for the older youths. He thought the suggestion by Mr. Price excel- lent. The teachers were hest able to keep in touch with the old boys. He had suggesthd to the Lancashire Com- mittee that they should present the shield for competition amongst teams in the intermediate league. The shield competition was dying a natural death, and it would be great encour- agement if the shield was given to the intermedi- ate lague. (Hear, hear.) Mr. Houghton's Visit. He was sorry to hear Mr. Mason speak as he did of Mr. Houghton. He thought Mr. Mason's remarks were very unkind to Mr. Houghton, because if there was one man on the N.U. Com- mittee who devoted himself wholly and most heartily to N.U. football it was Mr. Houghton. , Referring to the strike, the President said he told the players that if they persisted in the strike he felt sure they would not be considered for the Australian tour. , Purchase of Ground. Referring to the purchase of the ground, Councillor Osborne said they had a ground which was the envy of many other N. 17. clubs. They had spent considerable sums on improve- ments, and in face of that it was felt advisable to ascertain whether the owners would be pre- pared to sell. Mr. Bradshaw—no one worked harder for the club than Mr. Bradshaw—saw the directors of Messrs. Greenall, Whitley and Co., and a very tangible offer was made for that most valuable piece of land. One of the Founders.

• We pleaded with the committee to see if they could see their way to let, if not the four go, to allow two to go, and we pointed out that War- rington was one of the founders of the NAJ., and had assisted clubs from time to time, who would have gone out of existence but for our financial assistance, and we asked that they should give us one man to represent the town in the Australian team. The matter was taken out of our hands, with the result you know, but now that cannot be helped. Mr. Mason spoke about, the strike not having been , settled in a satisfactory manner. No penalty was imposed upon any player. As a committee, we have forgotten the incident We hope the players will work as one body and obtain a position amongst the first four in the League next season. (Applause.) Mr. Price thought the suggested Intermediate League should not be connected with the works. The teachers, who were in touch with old scholars, would work to greater advantage. (Hear, hear.) Councillor Osborne: Without the hearty co- operation of the teachers this Intermediate League would not be a success. (Hear, hear.) The report was unanimously adopted, as was also the financial statement submitted by Mr. Pemberton.

===== 0002601_19140530_art0161.txt | included=true | included =====
LINER SUNK.

Anxiety Concerning Warringtonians on Board.

MR. ERNEST HAYES FATE.

A shipping disaster, not equalled since the Titanic foundered after striking an iceberg, occurred early yesterday morning, when the Empress of Ireland, homeward bound from Canada, was rammed by a German collier dur- ing a fog in the St. Lawrence and sank in ten minutes. The disaster occurred 55 miles from Quebec. _

During last evening contradictory meesages - were received. One stated that out of the 1,200 souls on board 800 had gone down with the ill- fated vessel. Another put it at 700, and a story from Montreal stated that all were saved, but this was not credited.

Capt. Kendall, the man who led to the capture of Crippen, who is reported saved, is said to have wired that there was a heavy loss of life.

WARRINGTONIANS ON BOARD.

Much alarm was occasioned in Warrington yesterday afternoon when news of the disaster came through. It is feared that at least three men belonging to the district were members of the crew, but particulars as to who they were could not be had.

It is known, however, that Mr. Ernest Hayes, ' of Warrington, second purser, was on board, but up to late lfist night no news had been re- ceived from him.

He is a promising young man of 24 years, a son of Mr. Hayes, of Fletcher-street, and is a native of Warrington. His father, yesterday, should have sailed on the sister ship, the Empress a Britain, for a holiday in Canada to visit another son residing in Montreal. He got to Liverpool when he heard of the disaster and decided not to sail as he iirtended yesterday afternoon. Last even- ing- lie was still in Liverpool, awaiting tidings of his son.

The relatives, seen by "The Examiner" re- presentative last night, said they were hopeful that Mr. Ernest would be amongst the saved. They were anxiously awaiting a cable, and thought if safe he would send a message at the earliest possible moment. Anxiety was in- creased as the hours passed and no message arrived.

Amongst other Warrington names amongst the crew are A. Delaney, H. Perry and W. Clare. It was announced in a later message that both assistant pursers had been rescued, and it was thought that this included Mr. Hayes.

LAST NIGHT'S OFFICIAL MESSAGE. The C.P.R. issued the following official state- ment late last night:—

Only 400 have been saved. The vessel was ripped clean to the stern and sank in 14 minutes. There was no time to call pas- sengers from their beds. This message seems to confirm an earlier one that at least 800 persons have lost their lives.

FOOTBALL CLUB'S SMART DEAL

THE EXAMINER, SATURDAY, MAY 30, 1914.

a. I,• • DISTRICT riEw3

STOCKTON HEATH

TOWNSHIP TIITLE-TATTLE.

The firemen had a " dry " drill. So stated the report of the Fire Committee At the meeting of the Parochial Committee. A waggish Councillor now wants to know •If the firefighters had a "wet" afterwards. Electricity is once more in the air. Warrington is prepared to supply the "Heathens" With electric light at a cheap rate. Whether the scheme will mature Remains to be seen in the future. A little girl of the Heath has a little lamb. It is a very tame creature and can be seen Following her about the roads in the district. The modern 80-Peep is proud of her pet. As yet it has not followed her to school. The lighting of the Swing Bridge is under consideration. We shall have to wait and see whether we are To have a lamp in the middle of the bridge. The Ship Canal Company are to decide the matter.

Allotmeht gardens for Stockton Heath are still in abeyance. It is now nearly nine months since the scheme Was first suggested at the Parish Council. They have not got much further with the matter. They are waiting for a reply from the land- owners. Some Councillors think it is time the Parish Council Began to hustle around a bit more. The old men's " Parliament " at the Swing Bridge Have decided that the Government need not dissolve. A proposal has been made that shelter seats Should be provided for this" Parliament," And the veterans appreciate the idea. A building boom has set in and many New dwelling-houses are to be erected. Yet we were told that Lloyd George finance Had completely killed building in Stockton Heath. After all, that is a "terminological inexacti- tude," So far as Stockton Heath is concerned.

Waggons Collide.—Od Wednesday afternoon two waggons which were being shunted on the service railway to the Wilderspool Brewery came into collision, with the result that one was thrown off the line and slightly damaged.

Underground Telephones.—Rapid progress has been made with the laying of the underground telephone cable track, and this week the work has been centred in London and Grappenhall roads. On part of Wednesday the trams could not run to the terminlts, owing to the line being " up" for the laying of the telephone conduit.

Victoria-road Paving.—At the Parochial Com- mittee meeting on Tuesday, Mr. Ashton (the clerk) reported that half of the owners in Vic- toria-road had decided to have cement flags sub- stituted for chequer tiles in the pavement. He had received no reply from the other owners, and the meeting decided to adjourn the matter.

Swing Bridge Lighting.—At the monthly meeting of the Stockton Heath Parochial Com- , mittee on Tuesday evening, Mr. Ashton (the clerk) said he had received a letter from the Ship Canal Company acknowledging receipt of the resolution passed by the Committee about the lighting of the Swing Bridge, and stating that the matter was receiving attention.

Fire Brigade Humour.—The members of the Parochial Committee were startled by a report that the Fire Brigade had held "a dry prac- tice."—A Member: What's that?—Councillor McDermid said the new couplings for the hose had been delayed in delivery through a strike. —Councillor Mclntyre: A strike! What is a strike? (Laughter.)

Allotments.—Councillor C. Dukes, at the meet- ing of the Stockton Heath Parochial Committee on Tuesday night, asked if they were doing any- thing in regard to the provision of allotments. —The Clerk (Mr. Ashton): The Parish Council has taken the matter in hand.—Councillor Mclntyre: We are waiting to hear from the landowners.

Members Present.—The members present at the meeting of the Parochial Committee on Tuesday were: Councillors Pining (in the chair), Westbrook, Clarke, Warburton, Pearson. Mclntyre, Dukes, C. Hallows, Rose, McDermid and J. Hallows. The officials in attendance were: Mr. Ashton, clerk; Mr. Wright, surveyor; and Mr. Bradshaw, clerk to the Parish Council, was also present.

Swimming and Rowing.—Those of the "Heathens" who desire to swim and row can have the full enjoyment at the lake, which was officially opened on Saturday. There was not a very large number present, but this was due to the inclement weather. The surroundings have been considerably improved by Mr. Cooper, the new attendant, for in addition to the re- moval of weeds the fencing has been renovated. It is hoped that during the coming season the association will have better support than they received last year.

A Sewer Bargain.—At the monthly meeting of the Stockton Heath Parochial Committee on Tuesday, a letter was read from Messrs. Greenall, Whitley and Co., asking that the six- inch sewer to be put in Brackley-avenue should be made into a nine-inch one, so as to carry the drainage from new property they proposed erect- ing. If the Committee agreed to this, they would pay the difference in the cost.—The Build- ing Sub-committee advised the Parochial Com- mittee to accept the offer, and this the Com- mittee agreed to do.

New Houses.—A building boom seems about to commence, for it is some considerable time since the Parochial Committee have had to pass so many plans for new houses as at their meet- ing on Tuesday evening. The plans passed were for four villa houses in West-avenue, eight in Egerton-street, six in Brackley-avenue, and two in Alfred-street.—Councillor Dukes asked if they were to be provided in connection with the natural development of the locality or to at- tract people there.—A Member: For private speculation.—Councillor Pilling (the chair- man): It will tend to improve the village.

A " Fishy" Subject —At Stockton Heath Par- ochial Committee on Tuesday a letter was read from Mr. Nathaniel J. Morris, London-road, stating that there had been some complaint about the fish refuse which had been deposited at the Cobb's Farm tip. He had asked several farmers to allow him to deposit it on their manure heaps, but all to no avail, and he could see no other way but to send it to the farm, as before. He would, however, undertake to see that the refuse was buried.—The Clerk said the committee as a sanitary authority were not called upon to remove trade refuse, except by agreement under which the work should be paid for. It was for Mr. Morris to arrange with the nightsoil contractor or somebody else for its removal.

Empire Day Celebrations.—This day was cele- brated at St. Thomas' School by the singing of the school song, an address by the master, un- furling and cheering the Union Jack, and the singing of the National Anthem. Mr. Rose urged the claims of the British Empire to the consideration of every child belonging to it. British heroes and heroines were noble men and women, whose examples of public service were worthy of high praise and sincere imitation. The children must, however, remember that there was still a greater empire, viz., the Christian Empire, of which the kings of the earth, as well as they, were citizens. There was a third and greatest Empire—the Empire of Self. Without self-government neither the British nor the Christian Empire could prosper. It was incumbent on every child to think and act nobly, despising mean thought and action, for only thus could they hope to be true ' citizens ufloth anatarial-tuid spiritual empires.

The Hoardings.—Complaiut was made at a previous meeting of the Stockton Heath Paro- chial Committee that the hoardings on the banks of the Ship Canal at the corners of Elles- mere-road and Fairfield-road, obstructed the view of motorists and made the crossing dan- gerous. The Committee passed a resolution about the matter, and at the meeting of the Committee on Tuesday a letter was read from the Ship Canal Company acknowledging receipt and stating that the complaint would receive attention in due course.—Mr. Ashton (the clerk) said he had drawn the attention of the County Council, who were the authority to deal with hoardings, to the matter, and they were considering it.—Councillor Clarke thought one of the hoardings was loose and in a dangerous condition. He suggested that the surveyor should inspect it and report to the County Coun- cil.—This was agreed to, and the question was adjourned.

REST FOR OLD MEN.

Need of a Shelter Near Ship Canal. A plea for a shelter and rest for the old men of the village was made at the meeting of the Parochial Committee on Tuesday night, but the meeting decided to take no action in the mean- time.

Councillor Rose said there were several old men in the village, and it would be of great convenience to them if a screened rest could be provided near the Swing Bridge. Tie suggested as a suitable site the pavement at the corner of Ellesmere-road. _ _ _

--Councillor Westbrook: Could they not have a seat in the church porch? Councillor Rose: Yes, with the permission of the clergy and wardens. But they would see nothing there. (Laughter.) Councillor Westbrook: Yes, they would. (Laughter.) The Chairman (Councillor Pilling) said the Committee had taken action to have hoardings pulled down at that corner. A screen would be a further block to the highway. , Councillor Rose: It need not be a high screen.

===== 0002601_19140530_art0184.txt | included=true | included =====
SANITATION QUESTION.

Row the Scheme is Progressing. Progress is steadily being made towards the improvement of the sanitation of Stockton Heath, and it was reported at the Parochial Committee meeting on Tuesday that a survey qf the district was in progress with regard to the ?onversion of the pail system into the water carriage syAem. At that meeting Councillor Dukes asked whether they were any nearer the conversion of the sanitary system? Were the Sanitary Com- mittee doing anything? The Clerk (Mr. Ashton) said Messrs. Diggle and Son were engaged to make an examination of the sewerage system and report whether it would be adequate for the converted property. They had made an inspection and were obtain- ing the data to enable them to present a report. The Surveyor to the District Council had that morning received a letter from the firm asking for further information and stating that they Wished to make their report by Whitsuntide. The question was therefore coming to a head now.

The Chairman (Councillor Filling): Is that satisfactory? Councillor Dukes: Yes. Councillor Westbrook: I suppose we all under- stand that when we are converted we shall be saved? (Laughter.) The Chairman: It depends upon how you are converted. (Laughter.)

===== 0002601_19140530_art0185.txt | included=true | included =====
NEW ILLUMINANT.

Warrington Prepared to Supply Elec- tricity for Domestic Lighting.

Stockton Heath is seeking to have electric light and thus become up-to-date. Warrington Corporation is prepared to supply the illumin- ant for domestic lighting at the same price as charged in Warrington. The charge in War- rington is Id. per unit. The matter came before the monthly meeting of the Parochial Committee on Tuesday even- ing. At a previous meeting the Clerk (Mr. Ashton) was instructed to write to the Warring- ton Corporation, asking the terms they were Prepared to supply electric light to Stockton Heath. Mr. Ashton stated that he had received the following letter, dated May 26th, from the Town Clerk of Warrington (Mr. J. Lyon Whittle): "The Electricity Committee consid- ered Your letter of yesterday's date at their meeting this afternoon, and, in the event of Powers being obtained, are prepared to supply Stockton Heath with electricity for lighting purposes upon terms similar to those in force in the borough." Councillor Mclntyre: What is the price. The Clerk: The same as in Warrington. He thought before the Corporation could supply it they would have to obtain powers. Councillor Pearson: I thought we wanted to know the price for domestic lighting as well. The Clerk said the Corporation's offer would , apply to domestic purposes. The Chairman (Councillor Pilling): They have not Power to come into the district. If YOU get the electricity you can use it for any- thing.

Councillor McDermid suggested that they would require electricity for power as well as for lighting purposes. The Chairman: We shall require it for cook- ing purposes, &e, The meeting decided to inform the Corpora- tion that they would assist them in an applica- tion for powers to supply Stockton Heath with electricity.

===== 0002601_19140530_art0186.txt | included=true | included =====
PENKETH. EMPIRE DAY.

Doings at the Council School. For about an hour on Monday, in genial weather,. the school children held high carnival, giving a series of martial songs and clever dancesgreatness and beauty of our Empire, also their impressing on their youthful minds the respons'ibilities in connection with it. The programme, which was thoroughly appreciated by a large number of parents, friends, and the members of the Education Committee, was as follows :—Song, "Follow the drum," national airs, ball dance told English), speeches and presentation, "Rule, Britannia," daisy chain (dance), minuet, National Anthem, march-past and saluting the flag. The infants, in their bright-coloured costumes, did great credit to themselves and their teachers. Before the Chairman (Dr. Murray) began his speech, a pleasant surprise was sprung on Mrs. Murray. A splendid bouquet of roses, sub- scribedy the master and teachers of the school, for Iv by presented to her by Margery

The Chairman reviewed the work of the last year and said he was pleased to notice a decided improvement all round. He complimented the children on their clever display, and the parents on the children's exceedingly nice appearance. The Headmaster (Mr. D. G. Griffiths), in pro- posing a vote of thanks to Dr. Murray and the ladies and gentlemen of the committee present, said that he had not intended to mention the scholarship, but the Chairman had mentioned that they hoped for two scholarships next year, but what he would really like to do was to sweep the board. He considered the children of the Penketh School were mentally and phy- sically as fit as any school in England, and quite capable of holding their own with anyone. The teachers, however, must have the co-operation of the parents. It was a question of sacrifice-- parents must be willing to sacrifice themselves for the good of the children. If they were not willing to do so the teachers were very heavily handicapped.

Airs. i3e-11 seconded the vote and endorsed Mr. Griffiths' opinion of the children under their care.

-- Mrs. Murray thanked them all for their kind thought. The children were given a half-holiday in the afternoon to attend the school sports at War- rington, where, to show their physical fitness, they captured eight prizes. The dances by the older scholars were arranged by Miss Winn and Miss Newbould. Miss Routledge, Miss New- bould and Mr. Middlehurst played the accom- Oanitnents.

GREAT SANKEY.

G.F.S. in Liverpool—A journey to Liverpool was made by about 40 members and associates

of the St. Mary's Church Girls' Friendly Society on Saturday, when visits were paid to the Cathedral, the Walker Art Gallery, and other interesting places. Following tea a visit was paid to New Brighton.

Empire Day.—The children at the Great San- key Council Schools celebrated Empire Day, and amongst those present were the Rev. J. R. Jones and Miss Jones, Mr. and Mrs. Furness, Mr. and Mrs. Smith, Mrs. Clarke, land Mr. Long.. Ad- dresses were given to the children, who sang suitable songs, and the medals given annually by Mr. Furness were presented to George Hod- son and D. Jackson.

===== 0002601_19140530_art0188.txt | included=true | included =====
WIN WICK.

Bishop of Liverpool's V:sit.—The Bishop of Liverpool paid a visit to the schools and school allotment. He afterwards received the church workers and parishioners in the Rectory grounds. A special service was held in the evening. The Bishop said he was going about amongst the churches in his diocese taking a close interest in their—work- and meeting the church workers and paitishioners. He thought the people of Winwick had much to be thank- ful for- In the first place, thankful to God that their Rector had been able to labour amongst , them for 24 years. The Rector sometimes said he felt the time was drawing nigh when he would have to make way for a younger man. He (the Bishop) urged him to continue as long as he could. There would be a great blank in the diocese of Liverpool and in the parish when the time came for him to retire. To him and Bishop, first came amongst them, his family .they owed the deepest gratitude. When he, as 14 years ago, Canon Penrhyn was working hard raising money to restore their church, and now, P at the age of 86, with the courage of a younger man, he was endeavouring to raise the sum of £1,200 for the extension of tho school.

===== 0002601_19140530_art0189.txt | included=true | included =====
PADGATE.

A Road Incident.—A strange incident hap- pened on Wednesday on the main road from Leigh to Warrington at Fearnhead. Tar-spray- ing operations are being carried out, and a lighted coal fell from the engine which pulls the tar-spraying machine and set fire to the tar. The fire caused quite a consternation in the village, the roadway and machine being in flames. After Some difficulty the fire was ex- tinguished with dust and water.

Tennis, Bowls and Whist.—The Padgate Tennis and Bowling Club have erected a hand- some pavilion, and this was opened by Mrs. J. Duckworth on Saturday, in the presence of a large gathering.—The President, Mr. J. Duck- worth, congratulated the members upon their enthusiasm, whilst the Vice-president, Mr. E. Lunt, gave a resume of the six years' history of the club. The heavy liability which was incurred in laying out the courts and grounds had been wiped out, and now they had launched upon more expenditure, but he was sure the members would not allow themselves to be in debt very long.—Mrs. Duckworth formally un- locked the door with a handsome silver-gilt key, which was presented to her by the club.—ln the pavilion, on the proposition of Mr. .T. Burn, Mrs. Duckworth was thanked for her services. —ln the evening a whist drive was held, when the winners were Mrs. Anderson and MTS. Duckworth, and Messrs. W. Slater and W. G. Wood.

RIXTON.

Sunday School Services.--The Sunday school sermons at the Rixton Wesleyan Church were held on Sunday, when the preacher was the Rev. J. C. Harrison, of Pendleton. Special music was rendered by the children and choir, and the collections aggregated over ‘,£l3.

RISLEY.

A 252nd Anniversary.—The 252nd anniversary in connection with the Risley Presbyterian Church took place on Sunday. The preacher in the morning was the minister, the Rev. J. J. Glover, and afternoon and evening, the Rev. John McNeil, of Manchester, preached. There were good congregations at all the services. Special hymns were sung, and solos very taste- fully rendered by Mrs. J. 11. Lee and Mrs. A. H. Clarkson. Mr. A. H. Clarkson was the accompanist. The collections, on behalf of the church funds, realised over £l2.

Independent Methodist Church.—The Sunday school anniversary sermons were preached on Sunday. Mr. Drurart, of Latchford, was the morning preacher, and an address was given to the children. In the afternoon Mr. George Hunter, of Leigh, preached from the text, "All the Saints salute you, but chiefly they of CEesar's household." The children sang very nicely, and a little dot of ten sang her solo very sweetly. The choir rendered the anthem "One thing have I desired." In the evening Mr. Hunter again preached from the subject of the "Unjust Steward?" The choir rendered the anthem "Sun of nay soul" with taste. The collections amounted to £lO.

THEL WALL.

Memorial to Late Vicar.—At the Thelwall Church vestry meetinw on Saturday evening a letter was read from the Rev. W. J. Stanton, giving the reason for the delay in starting work on the memorial to the late Vicar, the Rev. T. Pym Williamson. It appeared that the esti- mates for both the designs, which were sub- mitted to the meeting of subscribers, exceeded the amount of money available. A revised plan had been made, which, while retaining the principal features a the original schemes, will not exceed the money at disposal. The matter was left in Mr. Stanton's hands. The Vicar thanked the parishioners for the kindly way in which he and his wife had been received, and in a brief reply 3Slr. Jones Parry extended a warm welcome to Mr. and Mrs. Tiarks. The following officers were appointed: Wardens, Mr. T. H. Jones Parry (vicar's) and Mr. Edwin Hewitt (People's); sidesmen, Messrs. Shenton 14, 9d., leaving a balance on the right side of and Hampton; lay delegates, Messrs. Parry and Hewitt. The wardens' accounts showed that the income for the year had been £297 15s. 10d., while the expenditure had amounted to £219 7. £8 ls. id. Mr. Stanton made an offer to pay for the necessary fittings for supplying the g church with water, and the offer was accepted.

MOORE.

Death of Well-known I nhabitant. —Mrs. Cart- wright, who, together with her husband, has resided in Moore for the last 46 years, passed away on Saturday, after a comparatively short but painful illness. Although 78 years of age, she had enjoyed fairly good health up to about six weeks before her death. Mrs. Cartwright was very well known and respected in the vil- lage, and the news of her death came as a sur- prise to all but the few friends who had been visiting her at the last. The funeral took Place on Tuesday afternoon and was preceded by a service in the Wesleyan Church, Moore, of which the deceased had been a member and active worker for nearly half a century. The service was conducted by the Rev. John Gunnell, who spoke of Mrs. Cartwright's life-long con- nection with the Wesleyan Church and the great loss which the village, the church, and her husband and family had sustained by her decease. Handsome floral tributes were sent by: Mr. Cartwright (husband), "All at Fern Bank," "Mary and Charlie," Mrs. W. Cart- wright and "Jack," "Daniel Towers and family" tilaydock), and the Stewards of the Moore Wes- leyan Church.

===== 0002601_19140530_art0195.txt | included=true | included =====
LINES FROM LYMM.

-

"Monday ladies" are not wanted. The Lymm Councillors do not like them, Because, it is alleged, they drive in With barrels of beer on the char-a-bancs. This is what some of the "ladies" call Enjoying a great beano on the Mondays. When these "ladies" got merry, One Councillor said, they seize the men of the Village And insist upon them doing a dance. Evidently they believe in a Tango tangle. The Chief Constable of Cheshire is to be Asked to stop the scenes occasioned by trippers. Sleepers-out are not allowed to live The Simple life, for the police are making Periodic raids, and they get plenty into the net. We shall be May-queening on Thursday. The preparations are now well in hand. The committee have hustled with a vengeance And everything will be ready for the " Coron- ation." A large crowd of spectators is expected.

Station Alterations.—The work of raising the platform at the Lymm Railway Station is now nearing completion, and the alterations will be much appreciated.

Cricket.—The Lymm first team journey to Arley to-day, when they will oppose the local eleven. This fixture is always much appre- ciated.

Pastor's Progress.—The Rev. A. J. Penaliggon, of the Lymm and Bucklow Hill Congregational Churches, is making satisfactory progress from his protracted illness, although he will not be able to resume his duties for some time.

Not in the Dictionary.—At the Lymm Police Court on Monday morning, before Messrs. A. Watkin and J. T. Ridgway, John Wilson, a native of Lymm, but of no settled abode, was charged for using obscene language and fined ss.

Leave of Absence.—The Lymm District Coun- cil on Monday night approved of the action of the Gas Committee, who proposed that the Gas Manager be allowed leave of absence from June 16th to 19th inclusive, to enable him to attend the annual meeting of Gas Engineers.

For the Children's Sake.—The house-to-house collection in Lymm on behalf of the National Children's Home concluded on Saturday, and it is expected that about £5 has been raised. Since the collection has been an annual one in the village, about ~t 23 has been raised.

Councillor Parker.—As was exclusively an- nounced in the last issue of "The Examiner," Mr. George Parker was returned unopposed as member of the District Council for Statham Ward, in succession to Mr. W. F. Dixon, retired. He will take his seat at the meeting of the Council to be held on the last Monday in June.

Land Near the Dam.—At a meeting of the Lymm District Council on Monday evening a minute of the Roads Committee was read, in which it was proposed that the Surveyor be in- structed to interview Mr. James Gould and ascertain on what terms he had agreed with Mr. Davies to deal with the land near the Dam. —The Council approved of this.

Present at Council Meeting.—There were pre- sent at the meeting of the Lymm District Coun- cil on Monday evening: Councillors Warburton (chairman), Lockyer (vice-chairman), Gotobed, Kershaw, Cowper, Holt, Ingham, Groves, and Critchley; the Clerk (Mr. William Mullard); Assistant Clerk (Mr. Timperley), and Messrs. Barnes (water manager), McDonald (gas mana- ger), and Mort (surveyor). Former Minister's Visit.—The Rev. S. R. Woodall, formerly minister of the Primitive Methodist Church in the Lymm Circuit, paid a visit to this neighbourhood on Sunday, the occa- sion being the Sunday school anniversary at the Primitive Methodist Church. The Dunham Massey services in the afternoon and evening were exceedingly well attended. The collections amounted to between £8 to .1:9.

Welcomed.—At the meeting of the District Council on Monday, the Chairman (Councillor Warburton), in welcoming Councillor Ingham, the newly-elected representative for Heatley Ward, said they were pleased to see him present. —Councillor Ingham, in reply, spoke of the pleasure it gave him to take a seat on tho Council. They might have differences of opinion, but they all had one common object, and that was the welfare of the ratepayers they represented. (Hear, hear.)

Death and Funeral of Mr. William Bayley.— The death of Mr. Win. Bayley occurred at his residence in Statham on Saturday. Mr. Bayley, who was in his 80th year, had lived in Lymm all his life and was particularly well known. The funeral took place • on 'Tuesday afternoon in the Lymm Churchyard, when the Rev. F. C. Bozman (son-in-law) conducted the last rites. Amongst the mourners were Messrs. Herbert and Horace Bayley (sons), Messrs. Beckett, C. Cowper, F. Swallow, H. S. Saville, J. T. Milne, and W. Allman.

The Culvert Near the Old Mill.—lt was reported at the meeting of the Lymm District Council on Monday evening that at a recent meeting of the Roads Committee a letter was read from Mr. Clarke, agent for Lymm Develop- ment Company, with regard to the culvert under the roadway near the Mill, and it was decided that the Clerk be instructed to write to Mr. Clarke in reply, stating that if the Council are to be held responsible for the condition of the culvert they proposed to have the same filled up.—The Council endorsed this proposal. Delegates to Conference.—At a meeting of the Lymm District Council on Monday the dele- gates to represent the Council at the conference of local authorities in London in July next were chosen.—Councillor Gotobed proposed that their senior member, Councillor Groves, attend this year, in company with the Clerk k Mr. Mallard). It was a good many years since Councillor Groves had been sent to the conference.—Coun- cillor Lockyer seconded, and the proposition was carried.—Councillor Groves said it was 19 years since he went, and then the conference was a very small affair.

Shop Assistant Footballers.—The Lymm shop assistants are making endeavours to raise a foot- ball team, and as was announced in last week's issue of " The Examiner," they made application to enter the Second Division of the Manchester Wednesday League. A reply has been received, and it is found that they are not eligible for that League, a six-mile from Manchester radius ride preventing them joining. However, a further meeting of the local football enthusiasts is to be held, when they will consider the advisability of throwing in their lot with the First Division of the Manchester League. The subscriptions are most encouraging.

Cyclists at Fault.—At the meeting of the Lymm District Council on Monday evening. Councillor Gotobed called attention to the danger caused by cyclists riding at great speeds down narrow footpaths. If publicity was given to the subject possibly it might have a good result. There was danger along the path from Higher-lane to the Grammar School, and also along the canal banks. He did not know whether they had any power to deal with it. The Clerk: No.—Councillor Kershaw said he hoped steps would be taken to stop such a dan- gerous practice.—lt was ultimately decided that the police be written to with regard to the matter.

===== 0002601_19140530_art0196.txt | included=true | included =====
MAY. QUEEN PREPARATIONS.

The arrangements for the Lymm May Queen Festival are now practically complete. All con- nected with the event have been electrified with enthusiasm, which accounts for the tremendous amount of work that has been accomplished in so little time.

_ The children have taken great interest in their training, and "The Examiner" representative who saw them dancing this week was surprised at the progress made.

The queen's bouquet is being provided by Mrs. W. Houghton, of West Leigh, Lymm, and will be presented to the queen by Miss Winnie Houghton.

The Queen-elect Miss Dorothy Pearson, is a great favourite, and is sure to uphold the dignity of the throne. The tradesmen and householders can materi- ally add to the gaiet:' of the scene if they would make some slight decoration of their premises by either exhibiting flags or streamers.

===== 0002601_19140530_art0197.txt | included=true | included =====
HEATLEY BY-ELECTION.

Enthusiastic Crowd Awaits Result

Declared.

MR. INGHAM'S SUCCESS.

Mr. F. Ingham (I.) Mr. W. H. Finch (T.)

Majority

- An enthusiastic crowd gathered outside the Lymm Council Offices on Saturday night await- ing the declaration of the result of the election in Heatley Ward. The result was no great sur- prise, and as it was announced by Mr. W. Mill- lard, the returning officer, there were loud cheers.

===== 0002601_19140530_art0198.txt | included=true | included =====
The New Councillor's Thanks.

Councillor Ingham said he stood before them as the winner of the contest, and he thanked all who had given him their support. Mr. Finch, his opponent, had fought a straightforward and manly fight, and the good friendship between them, he was sure, would continue. He appre- ciated the confidence the electors had shown in him, and he publicly thanked his committee who had worked so energetically and straight- forwardly. To his knowledge nothing dirty had been done by either side. Mr. Finch said he could supplement every word Mr. Ingham had said. They were all scholars, and during the campaign he had been a schcflar and had learned a lesson, but what it was lie was not going to say. (A Voice: "Every bad mistake means a lesson.") Mr. Finch said that gentleman had made an interjection that was uncalled for. He had the satisfaction of knowing that he came out with an object, at the request of certain gentlemen in the district, and that object had been fulfilled. (A Voice: "It is so.") He would have resigned in favour of Mr. Ingham if his committee would have allowed him before a certain date. (A Voice: "Not in favour of Mr. Jolley?") "No; distinctly no," he replied. He thought as much of the electors then as he did when lie entered that campaign, for he still looked upon them as fair sportsmen, and he was as big a sportsman as any of them.. He thanked all who had taken part in the elec- tion, especially those who had voted against him. That seemed curious, but his committee knew he did not want to go on the Council. Remarks which were made by Mr. Finch in con- cluding brought forth strong protests from various parts of the audience, and cries of "You are not a sport" were heard on every hand.

BOUNDARY QUESTION.

Disputed Territory Near Lytom. If the Local Government Board confirm the Lancashire County Council's proposals for the alteration of the boundary between that county and Cheshire, Bucklow would be deprived of a very important part of territory, whilst Irlam would gain considerably. At a meeting of the Bucklow Board of Guar- dians on Wednesday a copy of a letter which had been sent to Mr. Potts, of the Cheshire County Council, by the Local Government Board was read, intimating to the County Authority that as regarded the proposals which were pro- ceeded with at the recent inquiry in Manchester the Board were sa-isfied they might properly be put into effect. It appeared desirable, how- ever, that before any order was issued the two County Councils should have an opportunity of coming to some agreement with regard to the alteration of a certain portion of the boundary line (between the county borough of Warrington and Warburton high-level bridge). If such an agreement were arrived at, and the Board were satisfied as to the desirability of the agreed alteration, a provisional order could be issued dealing with the whole matter at one time. Mr. Potts' letter in reply was also read.

FATAL COLLISION.

Cadishead Man Killed in Trap Accident A shocking fatality occurred in Mill-lane, lleatley, near Mr. Oldfield's farm, on Tuesday afternoon. The victim was a eadishead coach proprietor, named James Jackson, who was thrown out of a float and killed immediately. The inquest was conducted by the Coroner, Mr. T. Ridgway, of Warrington, on Wednesday afternoon, at the Burford-lane Farm, occupied by Mr. Paul Peters.

-Richard Jackson, of 75, Bankfield-avenue, Cadishead, said the deceased, who was his father, was 49 years of age, and had been enjoy- ing very good health, especially so during the last few weeks. Deceased left home shortly after five o'clock on Monday morning on a journey to Burslem, Staffordshire, with a load of explosives, and would be on his return when the accident occurred. lie would have travelled 32 miles that day.

===== 0002601_19140530_art0201.txt | included=true | included =====
COLLISION EXPLAINED.

..... John Blackledge, farmer, Agden-lane Farm, said he was driving a governess car down Mill- lane, and first saw the deceased 50 to 100 yards away. The accident happened about 150 yards from Mr. Oldfield's. Witness, who was accom- panied by his father, was travelling at about six miles an hour on his way to the Heatley Station. Deceased was on his correct side, but nearer to the centre of the road. When they were immediately opposite witness's pony swerved and the two vehicles collided, with the result that the float was overturned and de- ceased thrown on to his head and killed instan- taneously. It was a narrow road. The Coroner: What made your pony shy?—l cannot tell. How old is it?—Four years. Did you notice anything bright about the float?—No, sir. I cannot tell what caused it. In answer to further questions, Witness said that both deceased and himself altered their pace. Deceased could have got about a yard nearer his own side, but if the pony had not shied nothing would have occurred.

A REMARKABLE THING. The Coroner: It seems remarkable that the governess car should knock the float over, which, of course, is heavier.—lt does, sir. One would have thought that the lighter vehicle would have turned over.—They would. sir. Later it was stated that the two horses fell and perhaps that was responsible for the float overturning. Deceased appeared to be driving all right. John Blackledge, sen., father of the previous witness, said he was with his son at the time of the accident, and he corroborated the son's evi- dence. The pony in the governess car might hesitate at some things, but there was nothing he knew which made it swerve on the occasion of the accident. Witness could throw no light on why the float was overturned. Perhaps when the horse fell it turned over. A carter named Thomas Grant, who knew the deceased, said he last saw him at " The Kilton," Hoo Green, about 1.30 on Tuesday. They had a glass of beer together, but deceased was quite sober and quite able to drive his float. They left the public-house together and went the same way. The Coroner: Where did you separate?—De- ceased went on in front. I was driving a lurry. Was his horse fresh?—No, sir, not after com- ing so far. How far had he come?—lt would be about 26 miles.

DEAD ON ROADSIDE. P.C. Jeffs, stationed at Heatley, said he was notified of the accident about three o'clock. He found deceased lying dead on the side of the road. Dr. Thorpe said Jackson's skull had been fractured. There was a pool of blood about two or three feet from the kerbstone. Witness examined the governess car and found the splash-board and the lamp-socket had been damaged, whilst there was a scratch on the wheel of the float and marks near the rim, which, he thought, suggested how the heavier vehicle was overturned. The Coroner said that was probably the cause. Witness, in reply to further questions, said the rciad was 16 feet wide. Those in the trap were the only people to see the accident, al- though there were several who heard the crash.

THE VERDICT. The Coroner, summing up, said the only thing that surprised them was the fact that the float should have been turned over, but probably that was explained by the evidence of the constable. Probably there would have been plenty of room if the pony had not swerved, but it seemed to him that it was one of those unfortunate acci- dents which could not have been prevented. A verdict of " Accidental death" was re,. turned.

TABLE TALK.

The World's Tribute of Interesting

Items of News.

QUAINT AND AMUSING.

The smallest part of a motor car that is abso- lutely essential to normal action is the rubber washer on the core of the tyre valve, it weighs only one hundredth of an ounce. A penny will pay for enough washers of this kind to equip ten motor cars, yet, when lost, it is one of the most difficult pieces to replace on the whole car. Fine thread soaked in rubber cement and wrapped around the valve core at just the right amount will provide a temporary repair.

A Clock of Glass. Glass has disadvantages as a material for machinery, anct for using it for the works of a clock, a Bavarian glass polisher found it neces- sary to make some parts as many as 40 times. In the end, after six years of effort, a clock entirely of glass was produced. Not only is this the material of the plates and pillars form- ing the framework, but they are bolted together with glass screws, and glass pins and wedges are used as fastenings, with glass dial, hands, shafts and gears.

===== 0002601_19140530_art0206.txt | included=true | included =====
Old-Time Surgery.

Writing on the old methods of surgery, a medical man says that even the torture of the rack could not have been much worse than the old method of reducing a dislocated shoulder. Less than 200 years ago the way of surgeons with a man who had put his shoulder out was to hang him by the armpit over the top rung of a ladder, and let strong men pull at his arm whilst others pulled at his feet. It was always said in those days that men soon lost their power to feel, and modern physiology confirms the idea that torture soon loses its power.

Finding Rare Metals by Spectroscope. Mineral waters are easily analysed by means of the spectroscope, as shown by M. Jacques Bardet, and this is likely to prove one of the best methods for this work. lie sends a beam of light through the water to be analysed and thence through the spectroscope prism, in order to permit of examining the spectrum, this method revealing very minute traces of metals. He finds the most varied metals in different samples of mineral water, and even the rarest metals, such as germanium and gallium, which are very rarely found in Nature.

Unsinkable Lifeboat Hu Novel Features. Recently an English inventor exhibited upon the Thames a lifeboat which ig,3 claimed to be unsinkable. It is a cylinder pointed at both ends, with sliding doors which may be tightly battened, and affords a 7fuge in case of emer- gency for 80 people. This strange-looking craft may be launched in a few seconds, according to the inventor, and is buoyant in the extreme, carrying its large load easily and safely in a heavy sea. Air is supplied to the interior by a small pump. A picture of the boat is a feature of the May "Popular Mechanics Maga- zine."

===== 0002601_19140530_art0207.txt | included=true | included =====
Bed Enclosed by Folding Screen Panels.

_ Folding screen panels to protect beds and cribs from flies and mosquitoes are the interest- ing part of a patent recently taken out. The screen arrangement is designed especially for babies' cribs, but it can be built on a larger scale for any bed. It consists of two screened panels fastened permanently above the sideboards of the bed and another screen panel which serves as a cover. An ingenious hinged-lever arrange- ment is fastened to one side of the cover so that it can be folded down on one side, out of the way. A picture of a bed protected in this way appears in the May " Popular Mechanics Maga- zine."

===== 0002601_19140530_art0208.txt | included=true | included =====
Eat Lettuces and Sleep Well.

The lettuce plant has had a reputation as a sedative from very ancient times, says "Science Siftings." The Romans were in the habit of partaking of it at the evening meal to insure peaceful slumbers. In later days many cele- brated physicians prescribed it as a soothing herb, but it was not until the end of the 17th century that a Dr. Cox experimented exten- sively with the latex, or milky juice obtained by making incisions in the stem. This crude juice, when dried into a solid mass, he named lactu- carium, and be, as well as other doctors, pro- claimed it virtues as a substitute for opium.

A Deserted Town of Blackmailers. Only seven people now live at Dyea, which in the days of the Klondike had a population of 20,000. Of the seven, it is said only two are whites. The townsite has been filed on as farm- ing land by three homesteaders, one of whom is an Indian woman and another a young man who is founding a fox ranch. Dyea, whose post office was established in 1896, became the next year the most crowded, lawless settlement in the world. The residents took toll from .the men going in to seek gold and those coming out with pouches of the precious stuff. After completion of the White Pass- and Yukon Railway, with Skagway as its sea terminus, Dyea collapsed and was abandoned.

Fruit Diet•to Stop Strikes. Dr. J. Oldfield, the advocate of a fruitarian diet, lecturing in London, said the present labour unrest was really a dietetic. trouble. "Feed men," said the doctor, " on meat diet and they develop mental as well as physical un- easiness. The solution of the whole labour trouble is a dietetic one." Dr. Oldfield's argu- ment was that every set of animals has its diet —there are herbivores, carnivores, and so on. Every sub-class has its individual diet—for in- stance, acorns are poisonous to cattle, and not to pigs; and again, each species has its particular fancy in food. The human race, by all the evidence, is closely allied to the anthropoid apes, and therefore a fruitivorous diet is indi- cated for us, for the apes are fruitarians.

Humour in Engine Names. Humour may be found in the most unlikely spots by those who keep a look-out for it, and a contributor to the April issue of the "Railway and Travel Monthly" has even discovered it in the naming of locomotives. Certainly, some of his examples are funny enough, though there was an element of the gruesome in the naming of an engine, after a prominent locomotive engineer, Slaughter. This was afterwards altered to Avonside, where the gentleman with the ferocious cognomen presided over the locomo- tive works. A similar case was the naming of an engine after S. R. Graves, and on this engine qualifying for the subject of its name, another burdened with a similar name was sent out. Again, imagine a Dachshund travelling sixty miles per hour. •

Marriages of the Deaf. An examination has been made of the records of 4,500 marriages of the deaf, and it has been found, says the "Family Doctor," that, while taking the marriages of deaf persons as a whole, nearly nine per cent. of the offspring are deaf, and contrasted with less than one-tenth of one per cent. of dead children as a result of the. marriages of normal persons; a very different and much more favourable result is obtained if it be found that the deaf parents had no trace of previous deafness in their families. Marriages of deaf persons without deaf relatives is no more likely to result in deaf children than any marriage in the community at large, while marriages of hearing persons who have deaf relatives is just as likely to result in deaf chil- dren as the marriage of the deaf.

===== 0002601_19140530_art0209.txt | included=true | included =====
THE EXAMINER, SATURDAY, MAY 30, 1914.

WARRINGTON WORTHIES.

Passages in the Local Life of Philip P. Carpenter.-11.

HIS I NDUSTRIAL SCHOOL AND THE "OBERLIN PRESS."

BY ARTHUR

Terrible as were the ravages of hunger and disease in Warrington during the famine year, one cannot read without a degree of pleasure the story of the gallant efforts made by Philip Carpenter to stem the current of misery and give the hunger-bitten toilers the opportunity to eke out an existence. Practical genius is worth much at such a time, and Philip was the man for the hour. His great endeavour was to establish an industrial school, in which the ignorant might be taught to read, write and work, and in which apt workers of any kind might do something to earn their food. The scheme was shaped, but a serious check came from the opposition of the clergy, who would give no support if reading or writing were to be : taught. But, as its pioneer understood it, an industrial school was not an institution to ex- tract manual labour and leave men and women no more intelligent than it found them. And so he launched his project alone.

===== 0002601_19140530_art0211.txt | included=true | included =====
INDUSTRIAL SCHOOL "WITHOUT BENEFIT OF CLERGY." The spirit in which he began is well revealed in a circular letter which he addressed to a number of his friends.

Warrington, 1847. My dear Friend,— I am very sorry that it has been quite out of my power to write to you before; and now, as you see, I am writing, not with a pen, but with a composer's stick. As this is my first effort in printing you must excuse errors. My time is entirely taken up with managing the ' Industrial School, which we have opened for the benefit of the unemployed factory operatives. Owing to the bigoted conduct of the clergy, who would not have reading or writing taught, we were not able to organise the school under the General Relief Fund. But our Mayor [Mr. Beamont] (although a Churchman and a Con- servative) handed me £5O, and requested me to establish and superintend a school without "benefit of clergy." Of course, I undertook it; and I have now about 150 boys and young men to look after, of whom seventy work at different trades. They have their dinner, if they come in time; and many of them get nothing else in the day, unless we give them some Indian meal to take home, which we do as often as the dona- tions we receive from friends will admit. I have also a night school, with fifty young men, Ito look after. I might be called the Town Nose, froM my sanitary inquiries. Then there are all sorts of extras, too numerous to mention. All this is in addition to my ordinary ministerial duties. So you will not expect to hear much from me. My sister is just as busy with her Industrial School. If you can help us with orders, materials, or cash, we shall be very thankful. Yours faithfully, PHILIP P. CARPENTER. His sister Susan, though frail in body, did great things in her school for the female opera- tives, and in the first five months (so Philip says) had taught sewing to 269 of them.

===== 0002601_19140530_art0212.txt | included=true | included =====
THE OBERLIN PRESS.

It was in connection with the Industrial School that he established his "Oberlin Press," so called in honour of the philanthropist Ober-

- _ lin. Here again we cannot do better than quote his own words, written a year or two later in his paper " The Helper." We began the Industrial School hastily with the means immediately within reach. Having some bookbinder's tools, we collected all our old books, and set several to work, mending. folding, sewing, &c. The younger ones made all the waste paper into spills, for lighting candles (not pipes !). All were under the schoolmaster's care half of their time. Soon we got an empty house for a workshop. In one room, a dozen bookbinders were assembled; in another, a dozen tailors; in another, a dozen shoemakers; in another, paper bags were made for grocers. At regular periods, these attended the school, and another set took their places at the workshop. The lads who came in tattered clothes, and could not afford to get them mended, visited the tailoring and shoemaking rooms in turns, and came out well patched, and darned, and " crapped." All the pieces of old carpet we could muster were made into slippers; and the torn books from the cottages were brought to the bookbinders' shop, and, having been "fettled," they made the commencement of many a good library. "But what has this to do with the Oberlin Press?" _ -

-I-t-i;appened, dear reader, that in the midst of these bad times an unexpected bonus was an- nounced on some railway shares. Whereunon we thought what an excellent thing it would be to set some of these youths to printing; that they might learn to spell, and also print useful things to distribute among the scholars. Ac- cordingly, our bonus was invested in a press, type and furniture. And well we remember the delight with which we took the composing-stick in hard for the first time, and set up those beautiful lines:

I slept, and dreamed that life was Beauty. I woke, and found that life was Duty. Was thy dream then a shadowy lie? Toil on, sad heart, courageously, And thou shalt find thy dream to be A noonday light and truth to thee. Our press was a rickety old wooden instru- ment, and our type was worn, and none of us understood our business. However, the neigh- bouring printers were very kind in resolving onr difficulties; and by dint of pains we managed to print many a useful paper, and even ventured on a book of " Songs of Progress and Affection for the People," the first edition of Athich was sold off very speedily. By the time the factories got to work again, and the school closed, one of our young men had so far improved, that though he was but a bad speller and grammarian (having had no education but that of a Sunday ' school, and not having sci much as seen a press or a type three months before), he was able to set up a small tract, impose it, correct it, and work it off without assistance. He was a cotton spurner by trade, but having been out of work 17 consecutive months, through the stoppage of of a factory, he was anxious to devote himself to some other business. As we had found the press very useful, and hoped to make it still more so, we entered into a co-operative society, with him and a few others who remained desti- tute of employment. We moved our machinery to some premises belonging to the Mechanics' Institution; got our press entered according to law; and set up for ourselves. Since that time we have exchanged our old press for a new Col- umbian; have obtained various additions of type; and have built Oberlin a new house. We neither profess nor expect to rival practised, well-taught hands •in our execution; but we hope that our work .will be both readable and read. We hope that it will always be worth reading. Oberlin is glad of " jobs"; but he will never print bills for publicans, or pawn tickets, or puffs of tobacco, or anything else that (whether rightly or not) he deems injurious. There is said to be no rule without excep- tions; and we hope that our good friends who belong to unions; and can show indentures, will consider this attempt of ours as one. We are not opposing the trade unions; but, under the extraordinary circumstances a the case, we had no power of seeking their protection. We have, indeed, served an apprenticeship to cor- recting proofs of' twenty years' standing; but we were never bound. The only indentures we can show are careful hands and willing hearts: and we belong to the blest and blessing 'union of those who are anxious to, do good, .who., are anxious to be taught, and who are anxious to be faithful to the cause of Him Who com- manded us to "work while it is day." The "new house" alluded to was a room built over the Committee Room at the rear of Cairo-street Schools. From the " Oberlin " flowed a series of Oberlin Tracts, written in a popular yet piercing style, which carried to all who could read Philip's views on the town, its inhabitants and its problems. For the advan- tage of worshippers (few of whom could sing to his liking) he published "Selections from the Psalms and other Religious Poetry Arranged for Chanting," and for the advantage of his pupils., . friends, and the scholars of his schools he pre- pared "Songs of Progress and Affection." This last-mentioned contained the music of ninety familiar songs, hymns, rounds and catches. as well as lessons in the rudiments of music, sing- ing and elocution. And all bound in cloth for a modest sixpence! "THE HELPER." During the year 1850 he published "The Helper," a monthly periodical of twenty pages,

===== 0002601_19140530_art0213.txt | included=true | included =====
MOUNFI ELD.

intended for "young men and young women who are entering upon life with good resolves, and want help in directing and strengthening their principles." In his opening address he wrote: "Man has two hands. One of them he must lift up to heaven, that God's angels may strengthen him and lead him thitherward; with the other he must help onward his brother who is less favoured than himself. Unless he hold his hand up to heaven, his brother will pull him back; unless he hold out his hand to his brother, God will not lead him on." Most of the articles in "The Helper" were his own, and some of them, with their fine blending of affection, caustic and cominon-sense, were reprinted as Oberlin leaflets. Among these are lively essays on such topics as "Fops," " Scolding " and "Rude Manners"; others are earnest temperance appeals, "Drink, but Re- member," and "Respectable Man-killers." He attacked tobacco with the same whole-hearted- ness, and published "Have Christians a Right to Smoke?" "Smokers, Beware !" " Don't Poison My Air." This last was translated into Welsh, and is said to have made converts wholesale in the Welsh quarries. Other articles were directed against the practice of hanging and against war. (so arranged that they co the rest) he wrote with thdeetach outered pfraogmes plainness upon sins which grip the young, and Uulpdon be reviews and quotes the best literature upon the subject. But " The. Helper " did not receive very wide support, and in an article "The Helper Not Helped" he announced its discontinuance. A little later he published a "Town Council Reporter," intended to give stimulus to the municipal fathers and to record their sayings and doings, at a time when the town had not a newspaper to perform that salutary duty. THE PARSONAGE AND THE BOARDERS. In 1849 Cairo-street was opened, and of the first two houses built to the new frontage, one was designed as a parsonage. This house, the next to the chapel, became his home, and here he began to live a communal life with young men in whom he found interest. His sister had married Mr. Robert Gaskell, and, bereft of a housekeeper, he formed his household upon a new plan. He received into it a few young men of the working class, who were to pay him what their food and lodging would otherwise cost. He and they took their meals together in the kitchen, the mother of one of the young men acting as housekeeper and cook. Of this experiment, Mr. Robson wrote:— " Another feature of Dr. Carpenter's moral character, and almost Peculiar to himself, and to which Warrington is at this moment indebted for the existence of the Whitecross Iron Works" (the reference is to Mr. Frederick Monks, who was one of the inmates of his house) "was the personal friendship he formed for young men in whom he discerned a desire for mental and moral improvement. For many years he had a succession of such living with him, en terms of I social equality, in his own house. They worked at their trades, but lived and boarded with him, and in this way received influences from him which have borne fruit in after years. His un- failing industry, his promptitude, his wonder- ful and never-failing punctuality, his perfect purity, his high-toned charity, and his warm and earnest heart, wonderfully fitted him to in- fluence and educate young people, as the event has shown." Of the history of his young men there is no room to speak here. The place they took in the life of the town is their own high testimonial to the value of his teaching and training. In October, 1851 his house was entered and ransacked on a Sunday evening. ' The event would not, perhaps, be worth recording in an account of his life were it not for a handbill which the useful " Oberlin " ran off for him, and which aroused keen interest. Here is the announcement 1—

ROBBERY. Whereas, certain person or persons did feloniously enter my Dwelling-House, last even- ing, between the hours of 5 and 8 p.m., while I was engaged with my congregation in the dis- charge of my ministerial duties; and, having burst my Desks, Drawers, and Bureau, did abstract therefrom a plain gold Mourning Ring, with the name N. Pearsall thereon, also a silver coin with the letters I.S. on one side and two candlesticks on the other side, also gold and silver coin belonging to myself and my house- keeper, of the value of about Ten Pounds; and did also abstract from my plate basket three Silver Table Spoons, engraved P.P.C.; I hereby give notice that I offer no Reward for the dis- covery of the said Parties, if for no other reason, because I have incurred sufficient loss already. If, however, the party should be dis- covered, I do not intend to prosecute them, (i) Because my evidence will not be received in a Court of Justice or' ,ss I swear, which I am for- bidden by our Lo.•<' to do (Matt. 5, 34); (ii) because I believe ",at transporting the said . Parties or sending them to )ail would make them worse than they are, and I am forbidden to recompense evil for evil (Rom. 12, 17); andi (iii) because that would be a strange way of showing the forgiveness which I am bound to exercise (Matt. 6, 15). If the said Parties should see this Document, and if they will come to see me. I hereby promise to do them no harm, and I shall be glad of an opportunity of conversing with them. If they are afraid to meet me now, we shall meet hereafter, when we stand together to ren- der up our accounts at the lodgment seat of Christ (2 Cor. 5, 10). Lastly. I earnestly beg of them to give up their present evil courses (Eph. 4. 28), and to live a useful and holy life, that they may have part in the mercy of God which is by Christ Jesus (Rom. 6, 21-23.). PHILIP PEA RSALL CARPENTER, Minister of the Gospel, Chapel House, Cairo Street, Warrington, . Monday morning, Oct. 20, 1851. A few gentlemen presented him with ten pounds to cover his loss. This was accepted only on condition that he might at once give it to an Oberlin workman to enable him to emi- grate. To the unworldly Philip the occurrence had a value—it warned him to give away his re- maining silver that there might be so much temptation the less in the house. ,And the warning was promptly taken.

WORLD OF LETTERS

New Books.

The World Set Free. By H. G. Wells.

London: Macmillan and Co., Ltd.

As most of our readers are aware, this is not the first book Mr. Wells has written in which he has told us of the wonderful things that will happen to mankind in the future, and the re- markable regeneration of Society that will follow. What is noticeable, however, is that these changes are not to happen in some uncer- tain and distant period of the world's history. The great upheaval will occur in the miLdle of this century, and already, according to Mr. Wells, the cause is revealed which will produce this strange effect. The book begins with a brief survey of the history of mankind. The progress man had made from prehistoric times had been painfully slow, and at the end of the nineteenth century his advance in the acquirement of external power was but small. Then came the discovery of radium. Further discoveries followed, which practically revolutionised the world and pro- duced gigantic replacement of industrial methods and machinery. Steam engines were scrapped, Aviation became the ordinary mode of travel, because radio activity became the propelling power, as it was absolutely safe and the cost was infinitesimal. These changes could not be brought about without a great dislocation in the industrial world. Old methods of produc- tion being abandoned, the workers were thrown out of employment by hundreds of thousands. Then came the great war. The central Euro- pean Powers suddenly attacked the Slay. Confederacy, and France and England went to the help of the Slays. But the new discoveries had not only revolutionised industrial methods. They had produced engines of destruction of incalculable power, so that shortly after the war broke out a few bombs dropped from ,ttro- planes were sufficient to destroy Paris, Berlin, London and other places, with a loss of life almost beyond computation. It was impossible to continue warfare under such conditions. A truce was called, and presently, at Brissago, a remote place in Italy, the rulers and leading men of the world met in conclave. The result was the declaration of a World Republic—the Government being in the hands of the notable men who were met together Brissago. For the description of what followed, we must refer the reader to the book itself. Mr. Wells is a brilliant writer, of pronounced Socialistic views, but when he comes to reconstruct the world we are driven to the conclusion that he has not materially improved it. The progress of the race is slow, and it is not by such sudden and violent methods as those so vividly described in this book, that the happiness and welfare of mankind will be secured.
