Literary Gleanings.

"FASTING AS A CURE."

. Speaking at the Royal Institution of Public Health on "Fasting as a Cure," Dr. Oldfield described fasting as an exceedingly valuable, a most important, and a logically scientific method of treating disease. No physician, he said, need be afraid of an ordinary well-fed patient going without food or drink for three or four days. If water were taken there was no risk in a person going from six to twenty days, provided he were well fed at the start. The moment, be said, a physician could with knowledge and experience take up that treatment his battle was won in many diseases. Fasting was a natural remedy, and our ances- tors partly understood it when they employed bleeding as a remedy. Generally speaking, he said, the finance of Mr. Lloyd George should be applied to the treatment of fasting. Starve the rich and feed the poor, make the fat man starve and feed the thin man; but he did not believe in Mr. Lloyd George's practice of forcing the starvation of the rich in his treatment. He did not think that young men and women earn- ing their own living ever over-ate. In fact, no man under 40 who earned his own living could overeat. The middle classes, he thought, were the biggest eaters, and the aristocrats the smallest.

ABOUT TEA-DRINKING. Is tea good for one? Is it really bad for one? . These are questions often heard, and people are always wondering what are the true answers I to them. Some people are ready to -declare that tea is poison; others are quite as confident that it does no harm. Tea-drinking can certainly be harmful; it can also be harmless, and even beneficial. Very strong tea, and tea which has stood in the pot for a long time, is bad for everyone at all times. Anyone who has got into the habit of drinking tea that has been stewing is taking slow poison into his system, and is sure to feel the bad effects sooner or later, perhaps when he is in most need of vitality to stave off an attack of illness. A safe rule is never to drink tea that has been standing for more than ten minutes. Nor will pouring fresh hot water on tea that has been standing, to weaken it. mend matters. The poison is already there, and cannot be removed. Tea taken after meat does invariable harm. Nothing is more indigestible or harmful than the " high " tea which begins with meat and I ends with two or three cups of strong black tea. which may have been standing in the pot throughout the meal. The breakfast tea is harmless if the breakfast itself is simple. But neople who take meat, bacon, or fish in fairly large quantities at the morning meal would do better to drink coffee instead of tea. • Tea is best in the middle of the day, with some simple fare. Then it acts as the right sort of stimulant, and does the system good. Too much tea-drinking is taking more than three or four cups in the twenty-four hours. Even those ,should be moderately weak, and not too hot.

A SENSATIONAL SALARY.

Miss Wish Wynne, who, before she made such a hit in "The Great Adventure," was chiefly known to music hall patrons, tells an amusing story of how she first won fame with her char- acter sketches, to the readers of the May "Pear- son's Magazine." "One fateful day, wandering in Greenwich, I spied a poster advertising the fact that 'Trial Turns' were wanted at the local Palace," she says. "So down I went, paid my money, and sat through the show. It was rather ghastlY, ; and as I listened to the 2d. gallery hooting, jeer- ing and 'cat-calling' my spirits sank to zero. 1 At last I noticed a bulky gentleman, owning a ; vast shirt front, diamond studded, lolling 'against the bar. "Dubbing him the manager, I got busy with- out delay. "'Will you engage me for a trial week from next Monday? I'll play for nothing!' I re- marked carelessly. "He glared at me, and growled, 'Give you a v,-eeli? For nix? What d'you do?' I promised that my show would be all right, and to raY amazement he grunted, 'All right; come down next week. No salary, mind!' "Well, I went down and played my character sketches, much as I play them to-day. Nervous? I should say so. But, mercifully, that rough crowd liked me. In music-hall slang, I 'went

great.' "When my show was over, my hand was seized by a large paw, and the manager led me before the footlights. Then he made this memor- able statement: " Ladies and gentlemen,—l have pleasure in stating that Miss Wish Wynne has been retained for next week at a large increase of salary!' That finished me! "Eventually. I was handed the mighty sum of £2 10s. as my greatly increased salary,' and I've kept the identical coins to this day se a souvenir.”-

A HOLIDAY COMPETITION.

My Dear Children,— As promised last week, I have now much pleasure in announcing a new competition. I know this is not a season when you care to be in the house penning an essay, but, as the sub- ject is topical, I hope a large number will find a little time from play to take part in the com- petition. I want you to write a short letter telling how you spent your Whitsuntide holidays, what you did and what you saw. Everyone of you will go somewhere, even if it is only for a walk in the country, and all must see something which interests. Just tell me all about the things which interested, and you will be able to pen a nice essay. As it is the season of play I will give you plenty of time to write your letters, and shall not close the competition until the 27th of June. Now., that leaves you nearly a month, and I hope at the end of it to have a large pile of letters awaiting me.

Your loving UNCLE JACK.

PUZZLERS.

Who had the greatest appetite that was ever heard of?—The man who bolted a door, threw up a window, and swallowed a whole story. Why are Government clerks like the foun- tains in Trafalgar Square?—Because they play from ten to four.

THE WESTERN WIND. By E. S. FLEMING. A whisper stole through the murmuring pines. Telling a tale to me. A tale of the cherry trees which blow Their scent from the gates of Long Ago, A tale of the Western Sea.

A tale of the wealth of Indies' store, That thrilled me oft and again, A tale of pirates and robbers hold, Who sailed in the troublous times of old, Afar on the Spanish Main.

And there swept through the branches of the pines, A tale which stirred my soul, Of the wild, free life on adventure's quest, In the distant seas of the Golden West, That led to a warrior's goal.

The voice that told it came from the Wes From the glories left behind. That gleamed like fruit of Hesperides— And breathed through softly sighing trees, From the lips of the Western Wind.

THE SOUVENIR.

When I was on a hunting trip in North- Western Colorado, I made my headquarters at a friend's ranch. The foreman of the ranch was one of the best hunters I have ever known, and I enjoyed riding the trails in his company. On the wall of his cabin was the skin of a mountain lion that I greatly coveted. I asked him whether it was for sale. "No," he answered. "It's a souvenir of an adventure. ..I don't care to part with it. It was during the last winter I attended school that I met that lion. There were four families living within about a mile of one another, and we each owned some good hunting dogs. We had two collies and four Airedales. One of the neigh- bours had a couple of wolfhounds; most of the others. had different crosses ot the shepherd. Altogether, we could muster a pack of about twenty. "One Saturday in February my brother John and I and four other boys started out for a wild- cat hunt. We took with us 15 dogs. It was a sunny day, yet freezing. The crust of snow was strong enough to hold up the dogs. We boys all used skis. It was an ideal day for 'cats,' and before noon we had killed two fine specimens. " After skinning them and eating our lunch, we followed up a ridge covered with scrub-oak toward the head of a large draw. The sandstone cliffs formed ideal hiding-places for the big cats. We were nearly at the head of the draw, when we heard the Airedales fighting with some ani- mal a little way ahead in a grove of quaking

aspens. All the other dogs joined in, and we knew from the racket that they had tackled something larger than a cat. As we hurried up, one of the Airedales yelped as a dog yelps only when lie has got a fatal injury. Then we heard one of the collies give the same cry.

" Coming-out of the oak brush into the open, we saw a long, sinewy beast run up a quaking aspen that in falling had lodged against another tree at an angle of about 45 degrees. There the brute stood and snarled at the dogs some 20 feet below. Three of the dogs were dead or dying, and the mountain lion looked able to dispose of all thq rest of the pack. "We had brought no rifles, but one of the fellows had a six-shot revolver, and he took aim at the animal. The rest of us cautioned him against it; if he should only wound the animal, more of the dogs and perhaps one of us might suffer for it . "There were a number of fallen trees about, half-buried in the snow. One, about 15 inches in diameter, had fallen across another, so that it lay about 18 inches above the snow, and very, nearly parallel with the tree on which stood the lion, and eight or ten feet away from it. I stepped from my skis to this log. "Then my brother, who was perhaps 20 feet behind me, did a very foolish thing. He caught up a handful of snow, made it into a ball, and threw it at the big lion; it struck the animal full on the nose. The lion, with a spiteful snarl, launched himself straight at me; at the same instant every dog jumped towards the lion. I tried to leap from the log, but slipped and fell. Rolling over on my back, I found myself directly under the log; at the same instant the lion landed on the log, and slipped so that a front and a hind paw were on each side of the log. "I caught the front palVs, which were only a few inches from my face, crossed them under the log, and held on with all my might. The dogs were on the lion the moment it hit the log; as it felt their teeth, it struggled so fiercely to free itself that my arms were almost torn from their sockets. Its hind paws were working vigorously; had it not been for the protecting log, I should have been tern to pieces. As it was, my heavy leather chaps and corduroy breeches were slit to ribbons, and my leg was torn open almost to the bone from the knee to the ankle. Moreover, I was choked and blinded by the snow that the dogs threw in my face as they struggled with the fierce brute. "At last I felt the muscles of the front paws relax; the body, borne down by the weight of the dogs, slid to one side. I let go of the paws, rolled out on the opposite side of the log, and struggled to my feet. I had to fight off some of the dogs, who in the excitement took me for a wild animal. The boys, when satisfied that I was not seriously hurt, turned to help the do s, but the fight was over. The two wolfhounds lg d the lion by the throat, and one of the Airedallaes had it by the jaw.

"My brother bandaged my wounded leg as best he could, while the other boys skinned the lion.. They voted that I should have the skin, and I had it tanned and mounted, as you see it."

"PRINTERS' PIE." Out of Print.

"Printers' Pie" has once more broken the record. Several thousand additional copies were printed this year and yet the publisher was unable to supply the demand. It is antici- pated that the public will have devoured the whole of the edition within the next few days.

BEAUTIFUL TONES.

When I mentioned some weeks ago an acid fixer that had proved so efficient, I promised to take the first opportunity of describing a similar developer for lantern plates and bromide prints. The information is obtained from the same source. The developer has Amidol for its active agent.

Water, 20oz. Sodium sulphite, li oz. Sodium bisulphite lye, 10dr. Amidol, 60gr. Potass bromide, lOgr. Mix in the above order.

The exposure must be somewhat longer, and the lye causes the development to be /more slowly completed than with ordinary Amick) developer, but the beautiful blue black tunes given by Amidol are perfect. The whites are also of perfect purity, and staining seems to be out of the question. Pressure marks on post cards are reduced to a minimum, and the fingers are not stained.

After rinsing in water the print or plate is fixed in the acid hypo fixer given previously. I do not consider it a good developer for nega- tive—the deposit is so thin and transparent that intensification would be often necessary. For that same reason it is an ideal developer for lantern plates or bromide papers, especially for those who have the intention of subsequently colouring them with pigments.

PHOTOGRAPHING BIRDS' NESTS. The "Amateur Photographer of May 4 con- tains four beautiful illustrans of the work done by C. W. R. Knight in this department ef camera work. The article also contains three prints showing how the pictures were obtained, and like the Keartons and Oliver Pike and many other photographers of bird life, the patience expended as well as the risks run must be remarkable. The nature of the work may be guessed from the subjects of the pictures given in this number—" Herons and young," "Carrion Crow and young," "Kestrel Hawk feeding her young," "Rook about to feed 'her young." "To obtain pictures of the adult birds at the nest," Mr. Knight says, "will entail much more hard work and patience than can possibly be described here; we will therefore conclude by saying that the exposures are gener- ally made with the aid of a string attached to the shutter of the camera, or of a hundred feet or so of rubber tubing."

SNAPSHOTS.

After a negative has been bleached in the ordinary way with mercuric chloride end washed, it may be darkened by means of dilute rodinal instead of ammonia. The usual mixture of one part of rodinal to thirty parts of water will answer very well. The extra vigour ob- tained in this way is quite as great as, if not actually greater than, that obtained with ammonia. _ .

----- Those who are interested. in the new Paget Colour Photography will do well to get the little booklet on the process from Mr. H. Smith, Bridge-street, or from Messrs. Hill and Co, Buttermarket-street. At the various indoor meetings of the W.P.S. these beautiful plates have been shown on the screen, some by War- rington workers and some by the professionals at the Paget factory.

WARRINGTON PHOTOGRAPHIC SOCIETY. Nine members utilised the half-holiday last Thursday by cycling through Warburton, over to Glazebury, and back through Culchetb. Snap- shots were easily managed on account of the bright sunshine, and were taken at Lymm, War- burton and on Chatmoss. Tea was obtained at the Raven Inn, Glazebury, and a most inter- esting walk followed over the moss to Astley Station. Fortunately, the ground was very dry after the hot week's drought, and so neither ditches nor the heaps of moss litter proved much of a barrier to eirlier ladies or gentlemen. These well known heaps, by the way, will have disappeared in two years, for the whole of the moss has now been stripped of its top covering to the depth of four feet six inches, and will in future be fit for agriculture. The 7.35 train took the party back to Glazebury, whence a quick and uneventful ride soon placed them back in town. SILVER-STREET.

WORK-A-DAY WONDERS.

• VII.-TELLS OF TRANSFORMATIONS • I WORKED UPON A WEDDING GOWN.

'

"I never told you, did I?" said Patty, "that I'm a widow with a nice little foundation of an income, which I add to by taking private orders for millinery. Well, I never talk much about it—and I shan't begin now. "But what does seem comical to me is this— There was I one Monday afternoon—working away with that peach-blossom poplin Mrs. King gave me for Diamond-dyeing her parlour cur- tains, and there was she sitting babbling away about her wedding day when she first wore the poplin, and begging me not to dye it too dark. Keep it flowery, my dearie,' she said—' just the colour of full-blown lavender; for I've got a notion that it is to be a wedding gown again.' "Well—it WAS silly, but true as I'm telling you I had a bit of a cry as I hung out the poplin with every care, to dry in the garden. But I went back indoors and set to reading the Diamond Dye Book of Directions to the old lady —and was glad it interested her.

"Then I-brought in the poplin—and pressed it under muslin and—well—l was thinking my own thoughts when in burst a man, knocking and opening the door at the same time—and 'Mother,' said he, Where are you?' He took her clean up in his arms and carried her round my kitchen—and then set her down again—and looked at me and at the lavender poplin and laughed like a happy boy. . _ _

"Well, I shan't say any more--bnt my cheeks just flamed as his mother told him about the wedding gown and what she had said to me an tour before."

(To be continued.)

ATTACK ON ST. HELENS POLICE.

" Come on, the boys from Gerrard's Bridge, let us take them off the —," was war-cry of James Clearey, at St. Helens, in leading an angry crowd against three policemen at ten minutes past eleven on Saturday night. Con- stable Learey said at the Police Court on Mon- day that he was called to a fight in Central- street, where Constables Hurst and Toft had some men in custody. He arrested a third man, when Clearey rushed at him and pulled his prisoner away. Witness took Clearey into cus- tody, and he became very violent. All the way to the Town Hall he called on the people to take the prisoners away, saying he did not mind doing six months for what he had done. Clearey, who had been convicted twenty times before, was fined £5 and costs, or two months.

===== 0002601_19140530_art0235.txt | included=true | included =====
RUNCORN YOUTHS AND CYCLES.

kr

r

Two youths named Thomas Hushin and Thomas Snowball were severely warned by the Runcorn magistrates on Monday, and placed under the probation officer for twelve months, for stealing two bicycles, value £3, from the yard premises of Mrs. Taylor, 6, Albert-street. A detective-sergeant saw Snowball on "Priday riding a bicycle in Brunswick-street, and when questioned said he had -got it from Kirkham's." He identified it as one of the missing machines. He afterwards saw the other lad and recovered the other bicycle from a cellar at his home. The lads were told that if they came into the hands of the police again they would be sent to an industrial school.

CHAT and GOSSIP.

By MARJORIE.

The visit of their Majesties of Denmark had one result, which gave unfeigned pleasure to Londoners, and that was the reappearance of Queen Alexandra in Court circles, and at the Opera. For the last four years the Queen- Mother has lived in strict retirement, but ,It is now hoped that having broken through her se- clusion, she may be seen this season at Court, and in public; more especially as she was pre- vailed upon to open the thirtieth exhibition of the Home Arts and Industries Exhibition, held recently in the Albert Hall. There have been during this month of May no fewer thun five queens and an empress in London and its neigh- bourhood. In addition to Queen Mary, the Queen Mother, and Queen Amelie, and her daughter-in-law, the Consort of King Manoel, the Empress Marie of Russia and the Dowager Queen of Holland were on visits to this coun- try at the same time.

Frenchmen and Erudite Frenchwomen.

The Paris University recently bestowed the degree of Docteur des Lettres on Mdlle. Dupon- tal, who was the first Frenchwoman to win this distinction. Mdlle. Dupontal's achievement has been eclipsed by that of Mdlle. Leontine Zanta, who has just won the degree of Doctor of Phil- osophy. Her theses were most difficult, but she. expounded them with great. brilliance and erudition. This is the first time the degree has been won by a woman, and Freneahmen shown greater appreciation of erudite women than they receive in this country, where the learned socie- ties are much more disposed to withhold honours from them.

Honours for Women. Frenchwomen have little to complain of as regards public recognition of their services, for they have always been eligible to receive the, ribbon of the Legion of Honour, which Napoleon founded in 1802, but in this country the public work' of women is ignored, and it is only as "wives of decorated husbands that honours come to women. There is a growing feeling that a special Order should be created for distinguished women, who, whatever their qualifications for public affairs, can claim equality with men in

MEMS. FOR MOTHERS.

Children and Their Companions.

One cannot help feeling a little sorry for the only child, with no brothers and sisters, espe- cially if there are no near neighbours, or if there are children within reach who are not considered fit playmates for the solitary one. No child can be expected to thrive and to possess the buoyant spirits of youth unless it has the companionship of others its own age. Society rubs the angles off the childish temper; each little one finds its own level, and the quar- rels, so deeply deplored by their elders, serve to fit them for the battle of life. Children, too, must exchange thoughts and opinions with others, and to see a young Child always playing alone and in silence is a sadden- ing sight. When school age is reached the conditions are in a sense improved, children choosing their own playmates, and here there are unforeseen difficul- ties to the child who up till that time has had no youthful companionship, and therefore can- not at first understand the rule of " give and take." Useful Shawl Border. Cast on 37 stitches. •

the bottom, long with graceful lines, and reaches from the neok to within a foot of the ankle. It is not unlike the pinafore that was so popu- lar some years ago, but the Rajah has long sleeves and no part of the under-dress shows except round the feet. The fulness at the waist is held by a sash, which can be swathed and fall below the hips, or be adjusted after the Empire fashion, or a belt may be worn. The neck is low, but round, and is, in some cases, finished with a frill of tulle or net. This shape is par- ticularly suited to all washing materials, and if made in white, could be worn over different coloured skirts, and thus afford constant variety. It could also easily be built by the amateur, lieing so simple in cut and make.

Evening Wraps. For evening Wraps, midnight blue and various shades of rose are favourite colours. Yellow is also popular, more especially in gold shades. The sleeves are usually very ample, but in some instances are nothing more than a slit in the folds of the cloak, or coat, for both shapes are seen. Taffetas makes ideal summer evening wraps, and it is now so supple that it falls in the most graceful folds or draperies. With collars of beautiful lace or embroidered net, these are very- desirable garments. For the wealthy woman there are exquisite brocades lined with painted ninon, usually a conven- tional flower on a white or delicately tinted ground. The lining is so arranged that it shows as the wearer moves, for to hide so much beauty would be barbarous indeed.

The Boudoir and Flower Cap. There seems to be a sudden outbreak in the direction of caps, and the most charming con- fections •of muslin and lace are shown this season for home 'wear under the generic term of boudoir caps. It cannot be denied that the delicate filmy lace provides a soft and becoming frame to the face, especially in the case of an elderly woman, but there is no reason 'AV a young girl should hide a beautiful head of hair under even the prettiest conception of the mil- liner. A much more sensible idea, and pretty also, is the close-fitting flower toqiie or cap, made of all kinds of dainty blossoms crushed closely together, and without foliage. With 1830 dresses these tiny chapeaux are quite in keep- ing. The sole trimming is a long feather rising from the centre and held in place by a caba- chon of beads 45r jewels. For the matinee these little caps are ideal headgear, whilst they may be worn at smart afternoon functions with an appropriate dress.'

WOMAN'S WORLD.

matter intellectual. Women are throwing off the shackles of their sex. They do not ask that homage should be done to feminity. They de- mand the right to be grouped amongst men by right of their achievement. In music, drama, literature, art, science, and medicine, there are women who can stand on a level with men, but because of their sex they are passed by when it is a question of distribufing honours.

Fashions Afford Variety. Much more variety, with but little additional expense, may be obtained from this year's fashions, as compared with those of last season." And this should reconcile many women to the; marked changes that have come about, some of which, it must be confessed, are not for the better. For instance, two skirts may be ordered with a tailored suit, and each can be worn, turn about, with the coat. One will be of the same material, the other will be striped or checked, matching the plain coat in colour, or a direct contrast—such as mustard with blue, or red with black. Both skirts are frequently of the severe type, the coat falling below the hips. The new tunics will afford much variety also, in dresses made .from the new washing materials, which are more fascinating every season, and this year beat all records. Skilful Needlewomen and Cutters.

Never, perhaps, since the history of clothes began to be recorded has so much care and skill been necessary in cutting out and handling, as is required by the delicate, fairylike, and ex- Pensive fabrics now in general use. Tulle, fine net, ninon, mousseline de soie, and other trans- parencies, are almost as fine as the spider's web, and yet the most beautiful hand em- broidery is worked upon them. Anything but the most delicate manipulation would utterly ruin these fragile fabrics. But so skilful are the embroidresses, so light is the touch of the worker, who fashions garments, lavishly adorned with flowers, motifs, and stitchery, one would imagine they had never been touched by human hands. Not only the lightest, but quite heavy fabrics, are embellished by handiwork. Lawns and cotton materials are hem-stitched, dr worked in tiny dots, and motifs and arrow- head stitchery finds a place on serge-tailored dresses.

The Maharajah Tunic. A type of tunic that will be sure to find favour is the Rajah; one of the latest develop- ments of this popular and important part of a modern dress. It ig simple in make,• full found

HINTS ON HEALTH.

Treatment of Measles.

Anything connected with measles is important to the health officer, for measles and whooping. cough are the two most deadly and destructive diseases which menace the life, and after well- being of the young child. I fear them more than either diphtheria or scarlet fever, of which the general public have such a dread, just as they regard measles and whooping-cough so light- heartedly. My own plan is to allow nothing but cold water, aerated water, lemonade, or weak tea for the first '4B hours after the rash appears.

Keeping Mouth Sweet

This helps to keep the mouth, pharynx, and naso-pharynx sweet and clean, and as no solid food is given putrefaction is largely prevented, and so there is less danger of mischief sprew ling along the Eustachian tube. I insist on the chil- dren sleeping between sheets and that the patients be sponged down all over twice a day at least, and that they be kept cool and not have their beds right up against a roaring fire, as was so common a practice in former days. I think a fire in the bedroom is useful, for it sometimes relieves a troublesome cough, but I insi,§t on open windows but avoidance of draughts. I ask the mother or nurse to flush the room thor- oughly with fresh air twice a day at least.

That Cough. I tell them that the irritating cough is not due to "cold," but is the result of the poison of the measles; that it will disappear as the poison is got rid of, and that the best channels of elimi- nation are the skin and the kidneys. I tell the attendants to keep the mouth clean and to attend to any discharge from the eyes and nose by washing with water that has been boiled and allowed to become lukewarm, and I point out the advantage of using small pieces of, absorbent cotton-wool or clean rag, which should be used only once and then burnt.—Dr. Gordon Sharp in the "Lancet'

===== 0002601_19140530_art0245.txt | included=true | included =====
COOKERY CORNER.

Some Useful Recipes.

This recipe is one which will be appreciated by those whose mid-day meal is little more than a snack. Mince finely some cold boiled ham, fat and lean; add a little minced parsley, and season with salt and pepper. Butter a pie-dish; in it put a slice of bread, the crust removed; pour over some gravy to moisten it, and on the bread put a layer of the minced ham. On this place another slice of bread, moistened, more ham, and so on until the dish is full. Bake in a moderate oven for about three-quarters of an hour. Turn it out, reversed, on to a hot dish, and replace it in the oven until brown.

===== 0002601_19140530_art0246.txt | included=true | included =====
Watercress Salad.

Select the large, brownish green kind, which is Considered the best. Wash a loose handful in salted water, and swing it in a cloth till dry. Break it in small pieces, but do not cut it with a knife. Have ready three hard-boiled eggs; take off the shells; cut the eggs in halves, without breaking the whites, and. pound the yolks; mix them with the watercress; season with salt, pep- per, oil, and vinegar, and arrange lightly on a glass dish. Fill the white halves with finely- minced beetroot (cooked) and place round the .salad as a pretty garnish.

===== 0002601_19140530_art0247.txt | included=true | included =====
Cucumber With Asparagus.

Cut two large cucumbers into pieces 21n. long, take off the rind with a fluted cutter, and re- move the seed part with an apple corer; boil the cucumbers in salted water for ten minutes, and drain them on a cloth. Fill the cavities in the cucumbers with rather firm quenelle of veal, and braise them with stock and vegetables in a covered casserole. When serving, garnish the dish with boiled asparagus points, sprinkle the cucumbers with chopped parsley, and pour round a little nicely-flavoured with white sauce, in which the essence of the braise is reduced.

WOMAII SUFFRAGE.

The Lords .and the Step Forward.

BY A LADYAORRESPONDENT.

The "Common Cause" truly observes that in the debate on Lord Selborne's Women's Suffrage Bill, another step forward has been made. No one who was present in the House•could doubt this. The serious, and, for the most part, high tone maintained, the representative and respon- sible positions of those who supported the Bill, the acute anxiety evinced by Lord Curzon as mover of the rejection, and his dependence upon a bitter and insistent appeal to the prejudices created by militancy—all these things made for an atmosphere of intense reality. We read daily in our newspapers of what a powerful minority, grimly determined, can do. We see that it can paralyse physical force itself. The whole circumstances Of the Lords' 'debate have shown to an astonished world, and to the party machines, that there is some such power as this in tl4 Stronghold of Conservatism fight- ing for the women. •

-The Press have taken this second reading very seriously. " The Westminster Gazette" comes into the open in an important leader, averring that "step by step this movement has gained ground—often with the co-operation of opponents who have themselves given away the logical ground of opposition—until its eventual success on the political side is scarcely in doubt." It sums up with a shrewd word of advice to the party men on the situation: "We sympathise with the Liberal suffragists who object to a class vote, but there must be some- thing experimental in a first measure of en- franchisement, and all parties must be ready to make some sacrifice to get a step forward." The figures in the division were 104 against and 60 for; 'and the canvassing returns in con- nection with the Bill give some 30 more to each side. A suffragist strength of N in the Lords, with some of the strongest of the Conservative front rank men in it, is not negligible, and it is evidently the very sincere wish of the Liberal Press that their party leaders should not so regard it. Not only "The Westminster," but the," Manchester Guardian" and the " Nation" are all in their degree at one in this. „„

The "Nation" says: "It is rare for a pro- gressive measure to be defeated in the Lords by so slight a majority as 44, and such a division may well give cheer to a sharpening Tory appe- tite for a Suffrage Bill. By refusing to support women's suffrage, Liberalism ceases to be a national force, and leaves its initiative and formative work to its rival, the Labour party, or even to its enemy, the Tory party. So far as Conservatism is concerned, we have no doubt at all that if a Tory Government comes in 'this year or next, it will pass a limited Suffrage Bill."

Meanwhile, " The Times" has been publishing some striking articles on woman and her place in the State, and a "Morning Post" leader writer recently devoted some space to the ex- pression of his indignation at the disabilities imposed upon the girl Post Office clerk because she has no vote. The face of the waters is troubled. The next—the decisive—step forward must be taken, and with any party able to form a Government lies the happy chance of taking it.

A NEWTON-LE-WILLOWS DANGER SPOT.

At an inquest on Tuesday into the drowning of an eight-year-old boy, Prank Parkinson, in the Mill Brook, Newton-le-Willows, a member of the jury said this was a very dangerous place, and he suggested that some protection should be made to prevent a similar occurrence. The property belonged to Lord Newton and the Lon- don and North-Western Railway Company. The Coroner (Mr. S. Brighouse) said he would write to the agent of Lord Newton, the Railway Company, and the Newton Urban Council, ask- ing them to consider the matter. The boy, it was stated, went down by the brook gathering flowers. When returning home over the bridge he fell into the water. His companions were frightened and ran away, and eventually they went and told the father. The brook was dragged and the body was recovered. A verdict of "Accidental death" was returned. Volk

It was characteristic of Johnny that in his moment of triumph he remembered it was hard on the other fellows.

After lunch Johnny set off for Downing Street. He walked with such a step and air that some weary pedestrians turned to look after him with a sigh of envy, wonder- :ng -chat particular piece of goodi fortune had happened to him. ===== 0002601_19140530_art0251.txt | included=true | included =====
Jocular Remark by _Mother Results

n Suicide.

DIED AT SIXTEEN.

, A remarkable story of a young Warrington girl's suicide Was narrated to Deputy-Coroner F. A. Jones and a jury at Warrington Coroner's Court on Wednesday afternoon. The victim was Amelia Livesay (16), of 27, Farrell-street, who was found drowned in Fairclough's dam on Monday. * Alice Livesay, the mother, said her daughter had worked at Howley Paper Mills. She was usually a bright girl, and had no trouble of • any kind. She was quite cheerful when she came for dinner with her elder sister, Ada, on Monday, and ate a hearty meal.

After she had dinner: added the mother, I said to her, "You will have to buck up at your work, as Ada seems to be getting on much quicker than you." She then went into the backyard, and later Ada told me she was crying, but when I went out she was not to be found'.

Livesay described how she went in search of the girl, and at Fairclough's dam she saw a man hold up a hat and jacket, which she recog- nised as having belonged to her daughter. She was informed that the girl had jumped into the water. Later her body was recovered.

===== 0002601_19140530_art0252.txt | included=true | included =====
No Illness or Trouble.

The Deputy-Coroner: Had she had any serious illness ?—No. •

Was she a sensitive girl?—Very, at times. At other times she was not.

Did she work on piece or by the day?-0n piece. I thought she was not earning what she might have done. Did you say that as a mother to try to get her to get on?—Yes. • Had she trouble at the work?—No. -Did she ever threaten to take her lifeF—No. Had she a young man ?—I never heard of one. She might have talked to several. The Foreman of the Jury: Was there any un- pleasantness at the works?—No. The Deputy-Coroner: You drew an inference because she was bringing in less money than her sister?—l said it in a joke, because Ada gets a lot more money than Amelia did.

===== 0002601_19140530_art0253.txt | included=true | included =====
WEIR-KEEPER'S STORY.

Luke Dumbell, weir-keeper, employed by the Ship Canal Company, and living at Paddington Post Office, said he saw the girl take off her hat and jacket and make for the river. When she saw him she picked up her clothing and ran away. Soon after, while at a distance, he saw her jump straight into the dam. He ran to his boat for grappling irons, which he always had in the boat, and finally got the body out. The girl had dived right into the water and disap- peared at once.

Alfred Livesay, the father, described how the body was discovered, and added that he did not know of his daughter having trouble at her work or anywhere else.

===== 0002601_19140530_art0254.txt | included=true | included =====
A FAIR, AVERAGE WAGE.

Mr. Fred Chadwick, manager of the Howley Paper Mills, said he had never received any complaint about the deceased. She did her work all right, and was a fair worker.. She earned a fair, average wage for a girl of six- teen. The firm had never complained about her or her work.

The Foreman: Has there been any reduction in her wages?—No; there has been a slight im- provement. _

The Deputy-Coroner said the evidence left no alternative decision to the jury. It seemed a trifling matter, not a rebuke, which caused her to take her life. She seemed a very sensitive girl. The jury returned a verdict of "Suicide while of unsound mind."

WORKS' ASSOCIATION.

First Round Completed.

The last of the matches in the first round of this competition were played on the Horse and Jockey Green on Saturday. Amongst the popular victories was that of Walker's No. 1) over Crosfield's (Yard Department) by 31 points. The handicapping was slightly in the victors' favour, and with little difficulty they compiled the score of 184, as against 133. R. Ritchie and J. Titchard, handicapped at five each, put up a good game, for their scores 're pretty even all the way. At the sixteenth end 14 each was"-recorded, but Ritchie sgcehis win- ner in, on the jack delivered by Titchard, in capital fashion. FLETCHER'S WIN. - -

By eight points the men from Fletcher's beat Richmond's (No. 3) and tney.hitl to amn eight of the individual games to do that, such was the large handicap of their opponents. J. Grif- fiths (5), Richmond's, ought to have won against J. Broadhurst (2), for he had a lead of 11-7 at the ninth end. Broadhurst got the. "little 'un" and made good use of it, running to 13-11 in five ends. Griffiths peeled in two ends, but two singles gave Broadhurst a well-mirited win. F. Isherwood (4) gained a notable win for Fletcher's, when, after having led nearly all the way, he beat Chamberlain (6) 15-13 in fourteen ends. T. Bowker (6) and S. Ray (5) had a stiff tussle, for they peeled at 7, 10, 11, 13 and 14, before the Richmondite drew away with the odd chalk.

===== 0002601_19140530_art0259.txt | included=true | included =====
C.L.C. AND MONKS' WIN.

The Railwaymen had a grand advantage in handicap, and if they had not had such a decent start it is doubtful whether they would have won by the nine points over Armitage and Rigby's. J. Firth (2) and W. Beardsall (7) played one of the best games, Firth, despite the disadvantage lie was under, running his woods so well that at the 16th end he only lost by one point. J. Smith (7) and E. Jordan (7) were a real pair and no mistake, for it took them 12 ends before they decided the game, Jordan being the fortunate one to score eight points. It was always Monks' match against Grap- penhall Tannery, and they were deserving of their 27 points' victory. They have a good handicap and should go well in the competition.

WHITECROSS DEFEATED. The Drawers from Whitecross played well against P. and R. (Rolling Mill), but the latter played better and won by four points. It was a good game and one would like to see the same teams 'meet on level terms without any handi- caps on either side. It would be a " toss up" as to who would win, for on Saturday there was not a man who could say he had been pitted against an unreasonably superior opponent. Of course, that in one way show's the success of the Association's handicapping system. There were several interesting games played and some tight finishes witnessed.

LONGFORD TEAM'S WIN. Longford (No. 2) had two hard matches before them, for they only beat Walker's (No. 2) by five, although, to give them every credit, they were far behind in the handicapping. They won some near games and lost a few, but only by small margins. In their replayed game with Whitecross (tialvanising Department) they on by 15 points and thoroughly deserved their victory.

Liverishnesz.

What is liverishness ? It is the symptom, or complaint, that betokens a disordered conuik ion of the liver. It is the distressing state of ill-health that banishes cheerfulness and makes the world seem a place of misery; that makes individuals irritable and " touchy " ; or causes headaches and biliousness. In short, liverishness is the smile-banisher, the joy-killer, the enemy of cheerfulness and laughter. But its depress. lug effects can be banished by a short course of Mother Seigel's Syrup, the world-famous herbal remedy for liver, stomach, and bowel troubles. It is very simple to take Mother Seigel's Syrup, after meals, so simple that tens of thousands buy and use Mother Seigel's Syrup with great benefit when ever they feel out-of-sorts, through faulty action of the liver, stomach', or bowels.

FRIENDLY MATCHES. ,

PENKETH BOWLING CLUB. The above club held a friendly main on Satur- day, their opponents being the Heath-road Club. The home side had matters all their own way, only four of the visitors managing to record a win. A. Skelhorn, who defeated H. Clark by 15-5, was Heath-road's best man. A very pleasant afternoon was spent, the weather turn- ing out far better than was at first expected. The scores were (Penketh names first) A. Carrington 15; E. Monks, 5. W. Shave, 15; A. James, 10. CRICKET.

WARRINGTON'S COMFORTABLE WIN. Northwich Easily Defeated.

Warrington are keeping up their series of vic- tories, and on Saturday they secured a splendid win over Northwieh in the match played at Arpley. The visitors were got rid of at the cost of 105, and Warrington knocked up 150 for four wickets. The first half-century of the season for War- rington was compiled by T. Wright, who played fine cricket all through, and it was a pity that he had to retire when he seemed set. His score was then 52. 11. Hawthorn carried out his bat with a nicely made 2.5. The Warrington bowlers were in good form. Bannam took five wickets at the cost of 30 runs. B. Silcock had two for 18; S. Partridge, two for 26; and W. Bennett, one for 15.

===== 0002601_19140530_art0266.txt | included=true | included =====
CAIRO-STREET STILL WINNING.

Cairo-street continued their merry career by defeating Orford somewhat easily by 7 wickets. So deadly was the bowling of Ellsmore (5 wickets for 3 runs) and Mather (5 for 6), that the Orford team collapsed in surprising fashion, being all out for 13 runs. For the winners Bailey batted well for 15 runs.

===== 0002601_19140530_art0267.txt | included=true | included =====
WINWICK'S NARROW DEFEAT.

Winwick Asylum C.C. entertained Winstanley Park on Saturday, and suffered defeat by 12 runs. W. R. Walker W inwick) was in good form, and knocked up the highest score for the Winwick ground this season, 57, made up with three 4's, six 3's, seven 2,'s, and thirteen singles. G. Humphrey (Winstanley Park) WEIS close be- hind him, with 53 to his credit. His score was composed of six 4's, two 3's, three 2's, and seven- teen singles. A. Aspinall (Winstanley Park) scored 31; E. Price, 19; and R. Nicholson, 18. J. Walsh (Winwick) had a short life, but a merry one. He had only six scoring strokes, but came out with 22 to his credit. He hit three 4's and 6, and then two 2's. A. Simpson, a son of the superintendent, scored 19 before being run out. G. Dawson scored 11, and Dr. Stone carried his bat out for 10. Walsh bowled two overs, taking two wickets for seven runs.

STOCKTON HEATH DEFEATED. Stockton Heath travelled to Hale Hall on •Ssturday, and although defeated were by no means disgraced. Hale batted first and com- piled a total of 83, 29 of these being made by Captain G. Ireland-Blackburne. This batsman scored rather freely off Egerton,' whose average was five for 46. Stringer bowled well for Stock- ton Heath, taking three wickets for 11 runs. Stockton Heath were only successful in getting 35 runs, owing to a combination of unfortunate circumstances. Three out of the first six. bats-

men were run out for one run between them, owing to misunderstandings arising. Ludden bowled well for Hale, his average for four wickets being 3.25.

===== 0002601_19140530_art0268.txt | included=true | included =====
PADGATE WESLEYANS HEAVILY

DEFEATED,

Padgate were again defeated at home on Sat- urday. Working Men's Mission scored 77 for the loss of 2 wickets, and then declared. Appleton batted well on a very bad wicket for his 35. Pai'gate, with two players short, registered 33 for eight wickets, Birchall being top scorer with 11. M'Garr was the most successful bowler for the Mission, securing five wickets for a very few runs.

BOLD-STREET WESLEYANS v. WYCLIFFE. Owing to the bad weather and consequent sodden nature of the pitch, low scores were the rule in this match, which was played on Satur- day at Arpley, on the ground of the former. Wycliffe batted first and knocked up a total of 20, the Wesleyans in their turn surpassing this by the narrow margin of four runs. As no player reached double figures some good bowling averages were seen. For Wycliffe, Brandwood took eight wickets for nine runs, while for the Wesleyans L. W. Weaver took six for 10, and W. Stringer four for 10.

THE PIONEER LIFE ASSURANCE CO., LIMITED. Twenty-second Annual Meeting. At the recent annual meeting of the Pioneer Life Assurance Co., in Liverpool, the Chairman I (Mr' G. B. Smith-Brodrick) congratulated the , meeting upon the substantial success of the last year's work. He directed attention to the annual report, which shows the progress of the company in recent years, as follows : Life Premium Life Assurance Income—combined. There was every reason to anii- cipate good progress again this year. He pointed out that the insurance funds—which is the set.urity for the fulfilinent of the company's liabilities to policy-holders—had more than doubled in, the last five years. These funds were put away in investments of the soundest character, and they brought a good regular re- turn in interest. IN THE COURTS.

BOROUGH POLICE COURT.

SATURDAY.

Before Messrs. R. T. Fairclough (in the chair), A. J. Jolley, T. Pemberton and Councillor Locker. _.

Conduct.—James Thomas Bate, or Kearns, of 3, Silver-square, was charged with being drunk and disorderly and assaulting P.C.'s Turner and Richards.—The evidence showed that prisoner was warned several times to go home, as he was very drunk. He was wearing the uniform of a sailor, and when they were questioning him about it, he struck the policemen in the face. He began to bite and kick, and his language was most disgusting.— Prisoner contended he was not drunk. He had only been in Warrington a fortnight, and out of that he had been in custody seven days. He had been discharged from the Navy win a good character.—The Chief Constable said that pris- oner was very drunk, and had his sailor's hat on the wrong way. He could have been charged with bringing contempt on the King's uniform, but the police had refrained from doing so. He was one of the worst characters and biggest brutes in Warrington.—He was sent to prison for two _months...

_ Did Not See the Officer.—William Delooze, who gave an address at a lodging-house in Bar- bauld-street, was charged with entering the premises 16, Wilderspool-causeway, the same morning.—A police officer stated he watched prisoner behaving in a suspicious manlier in the neighbourhood of the premises, and saw him enter and close the door behind him'. Prisoner told the officer he found the door open and that he worked on the premises.—In court he said he did work on the same premises, but not in that particular shop, He saw the door open and went inside the building to see who was in. Amid laughter, he said he did not see the officer before he went into the shop.—He was sent to gaol for 14 days.

T U ES DAY.

Before Councillor H. Roberts the chair) and _

Mr.._C. Jackson Holmes.

A Prisoner's Request.—Mary Hill, of 45, Corn- wallis-street, Liverpool, a married woman, was charged with stealing a pair of ladies' boots, the property of some person at present unknown.— Prisoner went into a shop in Mersey-street and offered the boots for sale. They were bought by the shopman, who immediately informed the police. Prisoner, the police said, had attempted to make it appear that the boots had been worn. —A remand for a week was ordered.---Prisoner: Can I be sent to Matiehester?—The Chief Con- stable: Yes, if you are found guilty later on. (Laughter.)

WEDNESDAY.

Before the Mayor (in the chair), Alderman Smethurst, and Messrs. C. Jackson Holmes, W. T. Williams and Hayward.

To Give Them a Chance.—Themas Henley, giving the address 40, Sharp-street, was sum- moned by his wifo, Janet, of 32, Winifred-street, for neglecting to provide reasonable mainten- ance.—Mr. T. S. Steel defended.—The couple had been married for five years and had three children. The magistrates adjourned the case for a month, to give the parties a chance to come together again.

===== 0002601_19140530_art0290.txt | included=true | included =====
MONDAY.

Before Mr. S. Wright (in the chair), Aldermen Smethurst, James Evans and Bolton, and Messrs. W. Roberts and W. Furness.

Gaming on Fair Ground.—Francis J. Coggins, 205, Winwick-road, was charged as a rogue and vagabond with playing a game of chance on the Fair, Ground.—The cloth which was produced was of a white glazed material and was divided into squares by numerous black lines.—Detec- tive-Sergeant Reddington said he was on duty in the Fair Ground on Saturday night, in com- pany with Detective-Sergeant Holland, when he saw prisoner at a stand with the cloth, pro- duced, lying on a flat surface. He had a foot- mark five feet from the cloth, and prisoner was selling discs at three a penny. The discs were the size of a penny, and the players had to throw them on the cloth. To win, a disc had to fit in one of the squares. It was a pure game of chance. One patroniser of the stall had 33 attempts and managed to get in two winners. The chance was a 100 to one against the players and a person had just as much Chance if they threw all their discs together.—Sergeant Hol- land corroborated.--Prisoner said there , was skill in the game. To win they had to throw the disc so that it dropped flat on the cloth, and not let the disc slide.—The Chairman said pH- soner had a chance of earning an honest living. —Prisoner was fined 10s., including costs.

===== 0002601_19140530_art0291.txt | included=true | included =====
SWIMMING.

Warrington's Fine Win Over Runcorn.

WORKS' COMPETITION.

The Warrington Swimming Club opened their season on Thursday evening, when, considering the counter-attractions, there was a very good attendance.

The first item on the programme was the first round of the inter-works squadron handicap, and some very good swimming was witnessed. Every credit is due to the handicappers, who performed their duties in such an admirable fashion. The results were as follow :—Corpora- tion Tramways beat Co-operative Stores by two yards; E. Forshaw and Sons beat Private Traders by a quarter of a length; Whitecross were easy winners against Post Office No. 1, who wore rather badly handicapped in having their best man away; and Pearson and Knowles beat Messrs. Crosfield's No. 2 by three yards, after one of the most exciting races seen at the Baths for ,quite a long time. There was plenty of excitement in the water polo match between Warrington and Runcorn, the homesters starting their League fixtures in the Liverpool Second Division by a glorious win of seven goals to two. Such a result augurs well for fife local club, as they are the first team to defeat the " Linnets " this season. The Warrington team was as follow: C. Woods, S. lace, L. Graham, C. Turner (captain), B. Pepper, 11. Sisson and J. Graham. The referee was Mr. J. Smith, of St. Helens.

Warrington won the 'toss, and selected the deep end. From the throw-in Runcorn were first on the ball, but owing to a misunderstand- ing between their backs, Warrington were early on the aggressive, and scored through H. Sisson, the same player, after splendid combination between the whole of the home players, scoring two more in quick succession, thus perform- ing the hat trick. Then came Runcorn's turn, Riley scoring with a • well-placed shot in the corner of the net, well out of C. Woods' reach. Half-time arrived with the score: Warrington, three goals; Runcorn, one goal.

The second half commenced in spirited style, and Turner opened his account with an easy goal, being quite unmarked. Ince soon followed with another, and then came the goal of the match by the " hot stuff," Pepper, who gave the visiting goalie no chance whatever with a shot from half-way. Runcorn got away on the right and Riley added another goal, thoroughly de- ceiving Woods. The play was now getting rather one-sided. and Warrington increased their lead through lace, with a good shot. The final came with the result: Warrington, seven goals; Runcorn, two goals. Both teams are to be congratulated on the fine game they played, which was free from fouls.

Tijurth

CAIRO-STREET CIIAPEL.

The Rev. W. H. Drummond re-visited War- rington on Sunday, and conducted the Sunday school anniversary services at Cairo-street Chapel. His inspiring sermons were listened to by large congregations, and in the afternoon the children were given an interesting address. The collections for the day amounted to £8 17s. 6d. Mr. Drummond resigned the pastorate of Cairo-street Chapel in 1900, proceeding to Bel- fast. Resigning the pastorate there, he then took up his residence in London as editor of "The Inquirer," an organ devoted to the inter- ests of the Unitarian body. He has the over- sight of several provincial ohurches.

GOLBORNE-STREET ANNIVERSARY

Good attendances marked the Golborne-street Baptist Sunday School anniversary services on Sunday, which were conducted .by Mr. J. Louis Fenn, of Liverpool. The preacher gave an in- teresting children's address in the morning. The afternoon musical service was presided over by Mr. J. A. Ransome, who gave a brief address upon " Thoughts." Miss Vanda M. Taylor drew useful lessons from "A Bunch of Flowers," tak- ing their colours to represent different virtues. The special music was rendered in a very effective manner by the choir and scholars, under the able leadership of Mr. 11. Barnes. Mr. S. Bickers manipulated the organ.

===== 0002601_19140530_art0300.txt | included=true | included =====
WYCLIFFE P.S.A.

There was a good meeting on Sunday, when the President, the Rev. T. T. James, presided. Mr. Drewitt gave the address upon " The rich man and Lazarus." In life, he said, the rich man cared not for the poor man, but in the place of rewards, the poor man had the best peace, and the rich man had the torment. To avoid the torment let them not be indifferent to the poor, who were our brothers, but let them be ever ready to help and cheer. The Secretary read an acknowledgment from the Treasurer of the National Institute for the Blind for the contribution he sent the previous week. One new member received a hearty wel- come. _ _ _

Mr. Payton presided at the organ and rendered an organ solo, which was much enjoyed. Mr. McEachern and Mr. Yearsley also took part in the meeting.

===== 0002601_19140530_art0301.txt | included=true | included =====
ELMWOOD-AVENUE SUNDAY SCHOOL ANNIVERSARY.

The first Sunday school anniversary services in connection with Elmwood-avenue Congrega- tional Church were celebrated on Sunday, when Alderman W. G. Townend, of Middleton, occu- pied the pulpit and gave splendid discourses. The morning service was specially devoted to the children, and a suitable address was given. The primary department sang very nicely under the leadership of Miss Wells. In the afternoon the choir and scholars suc- cessfully rendered a service of song, entitled "Cloud and Sunshine." Mr. W. 11. Wallington conducted, and the soloists were Misses F. Senior, D. Travis, E. Wallington, and Messrs. J. Mather and W. 11. Wallington. The devo- tional part of the service was led by Mr. P. Morrall.

Alderman Townend based his remarks in the evening upon the text "What I have written. I have written," and emphasised the necessity for greater care being taken in what was being written every day. The choir ably rendered the anthem "I will extol Thee." Mr. R. Wal- lington was organist, and the pianist was Miss Leathley. The attendances throughout the day were large, and the collections amounted to 49 Bs.

Since its opening last September the Sunday school has made great progress, the number of scholars having been doubled, and a successful primary department instituted.

===== 0002601_19140530_art0302.txt | included=true | included =====
ASSOCIATION FOOTBALL.

WARRINGTON AND DISTRICT LEAGUE.

The first annual meeting of the above League was held on Wednesday night, when there was a fair attendance of delegates, Mr. J. Wright presiding. The Secretary gave a sliort report of the past season's workings, which was accepted as very satisfactory. The Treasurer's report was read and showed a deficit of Bs. 4d. on the season's work. The de- ficit was due to the attendance at the hotpot supper not coming up to the number expected. On the proposition of Mr. Lawton, seconded by Mr. Appleton, the treasurer's report was passed. There was a small balance on the working of the Minor Medal Competition. The following were elected to office for the ensuing season: President, Sir Gilbert Oreenall; vice-presidents, the Mayor (Councillor P. Peacock), Messrs. H. R. Armitage, A. Crosfield, J. Dodds, and Starkie ; chairman, Mr. P. Booth; vice-chairman, Mr. J. Wright; treasurer, Mr. R. Garner; assistant hon. secretary, Mr. C. S. Essenbigh ; boa, secretary, Mr. R. Brandreth, Orford, Warrington. No resignations having been received from any of last season's members, they were de- clared elected for the ensuing season according to rule. The following new clubs were repre- sented : Grappenhall St. Wilfrid's, Latchford, Elmwood-avenue, Paddington A.E.C., and the South Lancashire Regiment. A discussion on the rules of the League took place, and several important alterations were suggested, but owing to the length of time which this took up, the business could not be con- cluded. The meeting was adjourned to a later date, when all the clubs will be notified to attend.

IRON AND STEEL TRADE FEDERATION. • The quarterly meeting of this Federation was held at the Midland Hotel, Manchester, Mr. James Gavin, of Glasgow, presiding over a full attendance. The meeting principally devoted itself to the Checkweighing Bill, now before the House of Commons, and an active propaganda was suggested in order that the Bill may not be dropped. Several amendments which have been suggested by the employers were discussed, and there was a consensus of opinion that these amendments would, in the opinion of the com- mittee, make the Bill inoperative, and Messrs. James Gavin, Ivor Gwynne, George Beadle and James Cox were instructed to take such steps as were deemed necessary to further strengthen the Bill and to oppose amendments which it is understood the employers intended making. Local Members of l'arliament in all the iron and steel producing constituencies are to be can- vassed in favour of the Bill. Favourable re- ports were given upon the proceedings which had taken place in regard to the eight hours' movement, and which is one of the first planks of the Federation's activities. The financial accounts showed a sum of nearly ..C7,000 paid into the Federation in entrance fees, and were considered eminently satisfactory.

THE EXAMINER, SATURDAY, MAY 30, 1914.

LOCAL INTELLIGENCE.

THE EXAMINER, SATURDAY, MAY 30, 1914. FOOTBALL CLUB'S SMART DEAL.

THE NEW PRESIDENT. Councillor Osborne and the Club's Prospects. Mr. Locker proposed the election of Councillor Osborne as president. Alderman Tinnion seconded, and the proposi- tion was enthusiastically carried. Councillor Osborne cordially thanked the members for conferring an honour upon him which he highly appreciated. The idea of the Intermediate League was to develop the young idea, for the boys and youths of to-day would be the players of to-morrow. The committee were determined, with the co- operation of the teachers, to found an inter-

— , N; , 4°N , 4 '''''. "::' • -, ^ . •,` 4., ~ 1 a • , •'; • k«.,:s " 55,... * -;-- s, --.,•'', ','',,t*: -M. , „ ;" , ~‘,,,,, 41 •,` t,f ' „ , • ...,„,?, ~ .',,,,, ;,,,,;I,=: ' '' ' 1 , , ' OUNCILLOR Pre°siclesßOßNEnt. The New

company were prepared to sell for £2,100. They were also prepared to give .6500 as a donation towards the purchase price. (Applause.) That meant the club would get the freehold for .61,600. He considered that was one of the best strokes of business ever done by the club or for sport in Warrington. (Applause.) COMPLETED ARRANGEMENTS. Mr. Hayes: Has a definite arrangement been made by the parties? Councillor Osborne: Yes, the contract is in the hands of the Secretary. Mr. Trow moved that a benefit be given to Naylor and Jordan. Mr. Plinston seconded, and on a ballot 258 votes were given for and 161 against. On the motion of Alderman Tinnion, seconded by Mr. E. England, Mr. Pemberton was unani- mously re-elected treasurer. Councillor Osborne proposed thanks to Mr. Locker, the ex-president, and expressed regret at losing his services, as he had worked with en- thusiasm for the club. Mr. Critchley seconded, and the resolution was carried. Mr. Locker, in reply, wished the club greater success, and he hoped the players would show consistent form from the beginning to the end of the season. (Applause.) On the motion of Mr. Chalmers, seconded by Mr. Davies, and supported by Councillor Osborne, the new president, thanks were passed to Alderman Tinnion for his services as vice-. . chairman. Alderman Tinnion said he was one of the oldest members, and had been connected with the club for over 30 years. p ELECTION OF SECRETARY. Much interest was taken in the ballot for the position of secretary. There were two candi- dates, Mr. T. P. Bradshaw, the retiring secre- tary, and Mr. W. Mason. The figures were announced, amidst loud cheering, as follow: Bradshaw, 354; Mason, 79. Mr. Bradshaw was declared elected. THE NEW COMMITTEE. The following committeemen were elected Messrs. F. Barber, 386 rotes; T. Bunch, 360; T. Barnes, jun., 319; J. Winstanley, 311; J. T. Sampson, 302; W. Hill, 296; W. Mason, 264; and J. F. Foster, 194. The unsuccessful candidates were: Messrs. J. 11. Whitham, 191; J. Sndlow, 128; A. Wright, 118; and W. Littlefair, 36. It was unanimously decided to grant the Secretary and Treasurer an honorarium each of the amount of £35, .and .£5 was voted to the auditors, Messrs. W. Webster and G. Gillin, who were unanimously re-elected.

There are still many people who buy spectacles as our grandfathers bought them fifty years ago. They patronize the door-to-door pedlar, or nick a pair from a tray exposed on some market stall. This is amazing when one thinks of the progress which has been made in the science of spectacle fitting. Our Grandfathers were blameless in this respect. They had no opportunity of doing differently. This cannot be said of their children. Have your eyes tested by Johnston Watson Sight Testing Optician, 84, BRIDGE ST. WARRINGTON. Steel Spectacles from 2/6. Rolled Gold from 8/6.
